#4910. BYD and Geely approached FIA about a 12th F1 team, says Ben Sulayem

BYD and Geely have both approached the FIA about entering Formula 1, the sport’s governing body confirmed. FIA president Mohammed Ben Sulayem named the two Chinese automakers while speaking at the New Economy Forum in Madrid, as first reported by Grande Prêmio.

But there’s no open process for a 12th team, and neither company has a deal. The FIA is also pushing F1 toward the kind of engine an EV maker has the least reason to build.

“One of them was BYD and another was Geely,” Ben Sulayem said of the manufacturers that have reached out about joining the grid.

He said he suggested that BYD buy an existing team instead of starting a new one. BYD wasn’t interested in that route. It wants its own team, so Ben Sulayem told the company to come back with a “serious proposal” that shows the project can work.

He also made it clear that Chinese manufacturers won’t get special treatment. Any bid would go through the same process as other prospective entrants, and the rules won’t be rewritten to suit them. Right now, there’s no expansion process at all: the FIA isn’t actively looking for a 12th team.

Still, Ben Sulayem repeated that he’d like F1’s next newcomer to come from China, pointing to the country’s EV technology and the Chinese Grand Prix in Shanghai.

This isn’t new for BYD. Back in March, we reported that BYD was exploring a Formula 1 entry, either by buying a team or building one from scratch.

In April, executive vice president Stella Li said BYD had met F1 CEO Stefano Domenicali in Shanghai and that an entry was “something we’re discussing.” By July, the tone had cooled. Li said “the dream is always there, but we don’t have a concrete agenda,” and special advisor Alfredo Altavilla set a clear condition: “We will never participate in Formula 1 just to put a sticker on the side of a car.”

Altavilla said BYD would only consider F1 to the extent its technology can serve the sport.

Geely is the new name here. The group sold 3.02 million vehicles last year and owns Volvo, Polestar, and Lotus, a brand with decades of F1 history. It’s also heading into Canada next year as Chinese automakers push hard into new markets.

The last newcomer, Cadillac, took a long road. The FIA opened an expression-of-interest process in 2023, and GM’s team only joined the grid as the 11th team this season, running Ferrari power until its own power unit arrives. Audi got in the other way, by taking over Sauber.

Here’s where it gets awkward for an EV maker. The 2026 F1 power units split output roughly 50/50 between the combustion engine and electric power, with the MGU-K jumping to 350 kW.

The FIA is already walking that back. In June, it agreed to shift the split to 60-40 in favor of combustion for 2027 and 2028. And Ben Sulayem has been pushing for V8 engines running on sustainable fuel with “minor electrification” for the next rules cycle, starting in 2031 at the latest, possibly 2030. Domenicali says F1 should have a package ready to discuss by the end of this year.

So the FIA president wants a Chinese EV maker on the grid, while pushing a rulebook that makes their main strength less relevant.

We like the idea of BYD or Geely in F1. Chinese automakers have the money and the engineers, and they badly need brand prestige outside China. BYD in particular has been on a performance push with Yangwang, and F1 would be the loudest possible way to tell Europe it’s not just a cheap car brand.

they look for visibility/recognition on the world stage, regardless EVs. And in some (primitive) markets they market themselves as hybrids automakers, not as EV automakers.

They will make a hybrid power unit (which they already make anyway) to comply with the backward F1 regulations.

But Ben Sulayem’s comments don’t move things much. There’s no entry process, BYD has already turned down his main suggestion, and Geely’s interest is a name dropped at a conference, not a bid.

A Chinese would likely be good for F1 as well. Motorsports are on the rise in China, and that would be the cherry on top in helping popularize the sport in the world’s largest market.

The bigger issue is the engine. Altavilla was clear that BYD isn’t interested in slapping a logo on a car. It wants F1 to showcase its technology, and BYD’s technology is batteries and electric drive. The FIA’s V8 push goes the other way. It’s hard to sell an EV giant on a sport that’s reducing the electric part of the powertrain.

If you’re following how fast EV tech is moving, from BYD’s batteries to F1’s hybrid power units, you can put some of that tech to work at home: pairing an EV with rooftop solar can cut your driving costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4909. SBF’s 'The Altruists' Plot Twist: FTX’s Anthropic Shares Would Be Worth $43 Billion

SBF’s 'The Altruists' Plot Twist: FTX’s Anthropic Shares Would Be Worth $43 Billion

Sam Bankman-Fried had an eventful Thursday—at least on the internet.

Anthropic went public, Netflix released “The Altruists,” and Crypto Twitter imagined the fortune his collapsed exchange might have made by keeping its AI investment.

At Anthropic’s November 19 closing price of $97.17, FTX’s former shares would have been worth $43.28 billion. At the $111.50 opening trade, their theoretical value would have reached $49.66 billion.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That money never belonged personally to Bankman-Fried. The bankruptcy estate sold the shares to repay creditors after FTX’s 2022 collapse.

The former CEO is serving a 25-year sentence for fraud after using FTX customer funds to make investments and cover losses, plus got hit with $11 billion in forfeiture—a fraction of what his single Anthropic investment would have returned today.

A company linked to Alameda, FTX’s sister company, committed $500 million to Anthropic in October 2021. The money converted into roughly 44.54 million preferred shares during the AI startup’s 2022 Series B, about 13.6% of the company.

Bankruptcy administrators sold the position in two batches in 2024 for approximately $1.34 billion. Buyers included an Abu Dhabi-linked fund and Jane Street, Bankman-Fried’s former employer, as filings showed.

At Thursday’s closing price that investment would have produced a total of nearly $43.28 billion—roughly 32 times what creditors’ administrators actually collected.

Sadly, that fortune is hypothetical. Later financing diluted FTX’s percentage ownership, the shares carried restrictions, and the estate needed liquidity to compensate customers who lost money when FTX went belly up. A judge approved the sale in February 2024.

Tiffany Fong, who rose to Twitter fame after interviewing SBF just before he was arrested, shared an AI-generated video of a crying Bankman-Fried drinking in a car alongside the $40 billion-plus calculation. Another account posted a crying-man meme about him watching the listing from prison.

Sam Bankman-Fried’s $500 million investment in Anthropic would now be worth over $40 billion.

The FTX estate sold it for only $1.34 billion during bankruptcy. pic.twitter.com/H2tua0D4V0

— Tiffany Fong (@TiffanyFong) November 20, 2026

Vietnamese commentator Yuki Sato likened the predicament to selling a house to settle debts before its value increased 100-fold. French poster Mehdi lamented SBF’s short-time focus.

FTX creditor advocate Sunil Kavuri reminded followers that customer deposits had funded the original investment and that the estate sold it to repay customers. Separate Anthropic investments tied to Caroline Ellison and Nishad Singh were handled through government forfeiture, so not the same estate sale, but still related in Crypto Twitter’s collective mind.

FTX and Alameda’s portfolio extended far beyond Anthropic. A leaked investment spreadsheet showed roughly $5.4 billion allocated to around 500 bets. These are some of the most important ones:

Alameda put $200,000 into Anysphere, maker of the AI coding tool. The estate sold the stake for the same amount in 2023. SpaceX acquired the company at a $60 billion valuation in 2026. If the original holding was about 5%, its undiluted value would be near $3 billion; subsequent dilution makes that an upper-bound mark.

The estate sold roughly 25 million to 30 million locked SOL tokens near $64 each in 2024, Bloomberg reported. At SOL’s January 2025 high of $293.31, that tranche would have been worth a bit less than $9 billion.

Bankman-Fried’s vehicle spent about $648 million on Robinhood shares in 2022. Authorities seized 55.27 million shares, which Robinhood repurchased for $605.7 million in 2023. At the stock’s 2025 intraday high of $153.86, that block would have been worth approximately $8.5 billion.

Alameda’s $1.15 billion Genesis Digital investment would imply around $290 million at a 2026 transaction valuation. By contrast, $40 million in drone maker BRINC could mark near $109 million at a funding estimate, and $20 million in Anchorage Digital about $31 million at its 2026 valuation.

A 461-position reconstruction puts $5.32 billion in total investments at roughly $59.5 billion on today’s valuation for those companies and assets—about $54.2 billion in hypothetical gains. The model assumes all investments were held and imputes values for illiquid positions.

That’s good for a 1,018% return, and proof SBF knew how to pick his bets. But even the best investments can't justify gambling with other people's money without their consent. He made that bet, too—and lost.

#4906. Bitcoin ETFs shed $1.8B in third-largest weekly outflow

Bitcoin ETF outflows reached $4.14 billion in November through Friday, nearing June’s $4.51 billion total.

US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) recorded $1.79 billion in net outflows last week, their third-largest weekly total since the funds began trading in January 2024.

The record was $2.61 billion in the week ended Feb. 28, 2025, according to SoSoValue data. The week ended June 26 ranked second, with about $2 million more in net outflows than last week, based on the reported daily figures.

Investors withdrew a net $4.34 billion over four consecutive weeks, bringing year-to-date net outflows to roughly $3.8 billion.

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT), the largest US spot Bitcoin ETF by assets, accounted for $1.37 billion, or about 76% of last week’s net outflows.

On Thursday, IBIT recorded $541.1 million in outflows, surpassing its previous daily high of $528.3 million on Jan. 30.

The fund broke that record again on Friday, with $544.7 million in net outflows, bringing the two-day total to $1.09 billion.

Monthly spot Bitcoin ETF flows. Source: SoSoValue

November net outflows across US spot Bitcoin ETFs reached $4.14 billion through Friday, about 8% below June’s record of $4.51 billion.

Bitcoin fell to just above $55,000 on Saturday, before recovering above $60,000 later that day.

Crypto trader Merlijn The Trader wrote on Sunday that Bitcoin (BTC) had fallen below the lowest band on the Rainbow Chart, a color-coded model that tracks Bitcoin’s long-term price trend.

#4905. DOJ seeks $1.17 billion in USDC tied to Binance hack; Circle could issue replacement tokens

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The U.S. Justice Department filed a civil forfeiture complaint Monday and separately asked a federal judge to keep the assets restrained.

The U.S. Justice Department is seeking to recover about $1.17 billion in USDC tied to the Oct. 29 Binance hack, according to a civil forfeiture complaint seen by The Block. In a separate motion also seen by The Block, prosecutors asked a federal judge to keep the assets restrained.

The amount comes from the balances listed in the complaint, filed Monday in the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia. U.S. investigators attributed the theft to North Korean military hackers identified in the complaint as Lazarus Group/Advanced Persistent Threat 38 (APT38).

Prosecutors obtained a temporary restraining order on Nov. 12. In Monday's motion, they said it was unclear when or whether the earlier order would expire and asked for a new order "so that there is no lapse in the restraint of the Defendant Property."

Circle has blocked access to the USDC but told prosecutors it cannot transfer the original tokens from the blocked addresses. The complaint says Circle "has agreed to voluntarily issue new USDC in an amount equivalent to the amount of the Defendant Property" and transfer it to the United States "upon receipt of an Order of Forfeiture from the Court."

In its Nov. 12 announcement, the DOJ said it had obtained court authorization to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion tied to the hack. The DOJ said Monday that U.S. authorities have helped freeze, seize or return more than $2.9 billion in crypto since the theft.

"Following the Binance theft, the FBI and our partners moved quickly to trace and freeze stolen assets and share critical information with virtual asset service providers," said Brett Leatherman, operations director of the FBI's Cyber Branch.

Binance put the losses from the hack at about $6.34 billion and said it fully replenished the affected assets at its own expense. The exchange separately obtained a $6.5 billion worldwide freezing order from an Abu Dhabi court as part of its effort to recover the stolen funds.

#4903. Nvidia Shares Sink to 52-Week Low After Report SEC Is Probing Accounting

Nvidia Shares Sink to 52-Week Low After Report SEC Is Probing Accounting

Shares of the AI chipmaker fell about 13% to $127.16 in afternoon trading after touching a fresh 52-week low of $126.64.

The drop followed a Reuters report that SEC enforcement staff are examining potential accounting issues at the company. Nvidia’s market value slipped to about $3.05 trillion.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Nvidia with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The Wall Street Journal reported Tuesday that the SEC was investigating Nvidia, including whether its disclosures misled investors. Reuters, citing two people briefed on the matter, said the inquiry centers on potential accounting issues, though it couldn’t confirm when the probe began or exactly what the agency is examining.

The SEC declined to comment, and Nvidia didn’t immediately respond to Reuters. Investigations aren’t evidence of wrongdoing, and many close without enforcement action.

Renewed attention has fallen on a change Nvidia made last year to how it reports revenue by geography. It switched from customers’ billing addresses to their headquarters, which removed Singapore from its regional breakdown.

The Financial Times reported this week that Nvidia made the switch as Singapore sales climbed to nearly half its global revenue. Reuters didn’t say whether the change is part of the SEC’s inquiry.

Investors have also grown wary of circular AI financing deals, in which chipmakers help customers fund chip purchases and data center construction. On Tuesday’s earnings call, interim CEO Colette Kress said Nvidia would be far more selective about such financing going forward.

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO last week following reports of extramarital relationships, including one with Alice Huang.

Alice Huang previously led Singapore-based Megaspeed, a major Nvidia customer, and U.S. authorities suspect her of being a Chinese spy; China has denied she is a spy.

U.S. authorities have been investigating Megaspeed since 2024 over possible export control violations tied to alleged shipments of Nvidia chips to China, according to the Justice and Commerce departments.

Former CEO Huang said Tuesday he would be honored to return if the board believed it would benefit shareholders and employees. Hours later, the board shut that down.

“The Nvidia board does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO,” board chair Harvey Jones said on the earnings call, adding that the company had hired executive search firm Heidrick & Struggles to find a permanent replacement.

The crisis has erased more than a third of Nvidia’s market value, even after the company beat expectations Tuesday with $115.8 billion in quarterly revenue, up 103% from a year earlier.

#4899. Claude Claims Historic Math Breakthrough One Day After Anthropic IPO

Claude Claims Historic Math Breakthrough One Day After Anthropic IPO

An Anthropic mathematician says Claude helped him crack the Riemann hypothesis, a 167-year-old puzzle with a $1 million prize attached.

His claim surfaced Friday, one day after the AI company’s stock-market debut, and has not yet been independently verified.

“I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis,” Levent Alpöge wrote in a four-page note. He says the method also resolves the broader Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, which extends the question to other mathematical functions.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Alpöge says he and Claude encoded their argument in Lean, software used to check mathematical proofs step by step. His note links to 14 downloadable parts of the proof archive, but its availability does not amount to acceptance by the mathematical community.

Alpöge says he messaged Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei about a possible announcement “late Sunday night,” November 15. He then consulted company leadership and said there was a constraint to respect, without explaining precisely what it was.

“The precise timing is horrible,” he acknowledged. Alpöge rejected a cynical interpretation of the timing, but did not explicitly say the company’s IPO caused the delay.

Anthropic began trading on Nasdaq Thursday, November 19, after raising $81.34 billion in its offering.

Alpöge figured in September’s AI math wars. OpenAI claimed it had solved the Navier-Stokes Millennium Prize problem as he and NYU mathematician Tristan Buckmaster pursued related fluid-equation work, sparking a credit dispute over research priority.

OpenAI followed in October with 722 manuscripts generated by an unreleased model, grouped into 372 families of results. Only 162 included Lean-checked main results, and mathematicians demanded stronger evidence rather than accepting the company’s flood of claims at face value.

Some mathematicians fear a “mathocalypse,” with AI solving coveted problems before researchers can learn from pursuing them. Toronto mathematician Daniel Litt warned that, without adaptation, “there’s just no more math in 50 years.” At an MIT panel this week, Buckmaster argued mathematics still requires human understanding.

First posed by Bernhard Riemann in 1859, the hypothesis predicts where certain zeros of the Riemann zeta function fall. The question matters because those zeros are linked to the distribution of prime numbers, whose patterns underlie extensive mathematical research.

A proof would be a historic mathematical result, since experts have been trying to solve that for over a century.

Lean can verify that formal deductions obey its rules. Mathematicians still need to reproduce the archive, check the formalized theorem matches the original conjecture, and examine the claimed result before considering it settled.

Anthropic previously reported progress on the zeta function without claiming to solve the full hypothesis. Alpöge’s new assertion goes far beyond that earlier work.

The Clay Mathematics Institute lists the Riemann hypothesis among its Millennium Prize Problems, each carrying a $1 million award. Under the institute’s prize rules, a proposed solution must be published in a qualifying outlet, survive at least two years of scrutiny, and earn general acceptance before the prize can be considered.

#4897. ‘MoldyRoost’ ads can steal crypto wallet recovery phrases from iPhones

The campaign used at least 480 ad domains to target users in more than 47 countries and could infect a vulnerable iPhone with a single tap.

Hackers are using fake ads to spread malware that can steal crypto wallet recovery phrases from vulnerable iPhones, cybersecurity company Confiant said Friday.

Confiant, which calls the campaign MoldyRoost, linked at least 480 ad domains to it between May 12 and Nov. 17. The ads targeted users in more than 47 countries.

“Just loading the landing page of a MoldyRoost ad on a vulnerable iPhone is enough to get infected,” Confiant said in its report.

Google Threat Intelligence reported in March that hackers were using a tool called Coruna to target visitors to fake crypto sites. MoldyRoost bought ads to spread the same tool on sites unrelated to crypto, where tapping an ad could expose a recovery phrase saved on a vulnerable iPhone.

Coruna can search Apple Notes for wallet seed phrases and read QR codes in saved images. A thief who gets a seed phrase can use those words to open the wallet on another device and move the funds.

The campaign also used DarkSword, another tool that can steal wallet data, which Google researchers also warned of in March.

In a Nov. 6 warning about DarkSword, blockchain security firm CertiK urged iPhone users to update their software and keep seed phrases out of Notes, photos and screenshots. It also advised avoiding unfamiliar links and using a hardware wallet for larger holdings.

#4896. Watch BYD’s robotic charging arm plug itself in and charge EVs in 5 minutes [Video]

BYD’s Flash Charging tech can charge an EV in as little as five minutes. Now, BYD is taking things a step further with integrated solar power and a robotic arm that plugs itself in. See how it all works in action.

After unveiling the Yangwang Altior in the UAE on Monday, its new ultra-luxury flagship electric sedan, BYD showcased its latest breakthrough technology.

The Chinese automaker launched a new integrated charging system in the Abu Dhabi desert that brings BYD’s “Three Green Dreams” to life.

In 2006, BYD president and CEO Wang Chuanfu revealed his vision of “Three Green Dreams,” including harnessing solar power to generate renewable energy, storing that energy in batteries, and replacing gas-powered vehicles with electric ones that run on clean energy.

The new charging system is the “world’s first,” according to BYD, to integrate solar power generation, energy storage, Flash Charging, and robotic charging together in one solution.

BYD’s Flash Charging tech enables a vehicle’s battery to charge from 10% to 70% in five minutes, essentially the same time it takes to fill up at a gas station. For a full recharge, or to go from 10% to 97%, it takes just nine minutes.

The new robotic charging arm automatically locates the vehicle’s charging port, opens its cover, plugs in, and then removes it once the session is over.

By combining robotic charging with its Automated Valet Parking, BYD said the new tech goes beyond automated driving and parking to the charging process itself.

Once the vehicle is parked, the robotic charging arm automatically takes over and connects it to Flash Charging without the driver even touching the cable.

A post shared by Michael Martinez (@mmichael_martinez)

Michaël Martinez, head of marketing at BYD France, posted a video (shown above) showcasing the new charging tech in action.

The integrated solution is built around BYD’s Solar-Storage-Charging (SSC) system, fusing solar energy generation, battery storage, and its 5-minute Flash Charging tech. Together, the setup captures energy from the sun, stores it, and distributes it as needed.

If they could tie this into a smart summon type of capability, where the vehicle communicates with the charger and moves the vehicle into the proper location for charging and then moves to a parking spot once charging is complete. Rental car fleets would benefit and so would those in shopping centers. There are so many possibilities and this looks far more mature that the old snake charging Tesla demoed.

BYD has over 16 years of experience in the solar industry with a portfolio that includes solar cells, modules, and systems.

Its solar division, BYD Solar, does business in over 100 countries and regions. By using solar power, BYD said, the SCC system can operate independently off-the-grid.

The new technology is part of BYD’s wider Flash Charging rollout. By the end of 2027, the company plans to deploy 6,000 Flash Charging stations globally.

#4892. Tesla Cybertruck had its best month in over a year. It’s still going away

New EV prices edged up in October, but buyers still paid less than they did a year ago. The average transaction price for a new EV in the US was $56,220, up 1% from September and down 2.7% from October 2025, according to Kelley Blue Book, a Cox Automotive brand.

Across all new vehicles, the average hit a record $51,109, up 1.3% from September and 2.1% year over year, even as prices rose more slowly than they typically do in October.

EV incentives barely budged from September’s revised 11.2% of the average transaction price to 11.3% in October. That’s up from 10.3% a year earlier and well above October’s industrywide average of 6.8%.

Tesla’s average selling price fell from September to $54,194, but it was still up 1.2% year over year. The Cybertruck helped push that average higher. Cox Automotive estimates Tesla sold 1,914 Cybertrucks in October, its best month in more than a year, at an average price of $92,380.

The sad thing is that there likely is a market for an affordable electric pickup truck in the U.S. to compete with Ford’s F150 and others. The Ford Fathom may be the best fit for that market, now.

That sales bump comes as Tesla prepares to end Cybertruck production at its Austin factory in mid-2027. Elon Musk said Tesla will dismantle the assembly line around June or July to make room for more Cybercab robotaxis.

#4891. Is the AI Bubble Finally Popping? Nvidia Crashes 48% as Wall Street Experts Sound the Alarm

Is the AI Bubble Finally Popping? Nvidia Crashes 48% as Wall Street Experts Sound the Alarm

Wall Street's artificial intelligence boom is facing its biggest test yet.

Nvidia plunged 14% on Friday, the Nasdaq Composite lost 5.3%, and the broader S&P 500 entered correction territory as investors questioned whether the trillions of dollars being poured into AI infrastructure will ever produce the expected returns.

The Nasdaq closed at 22,617.73, approximately 18% below its October 6 record closing high of 27,599.79. Its 10% weekly decline was the worst since 2020, while the index has now erased its gains for 2026.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Nvidia with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Nvidia, the company that powered much of the AI rally, finished Friday at $125.53, down 47.5% from its October closing record of $239.24.

The chipmaker lost nearly 23% during the week as the fallout from former CEO Jensen Huang's departure and reports of an SEC accounting investigation added to concerns about the AI business model.

Other AI-linked companies have suffered even steeper losses. CoreWeave, which rents computing capacity to AI developers, closed at $39.51, almost 80% below its 2025 peak, while Oracle has lost roughly 70% from its highs and finished the week at $101.19.

AMD shed 20.5% over the week, closing at $424.39, while Alphabet lost 10.3% to $265.29. The iShares Semiconductor ETF, SOXX, dropped 17.4% during the same period, showing that investors are selling across the companies supplying the AI industry, not merely its most speculative businesses.

The selloff accelerated just as Anthropic completed its historic $81 billion IPO. The Claude developer priced its shares at $83 and closed its first trading session 17% higher, but its stock fell another 9% on Friday as enthusiasm for the offering failed to restore confidence in the wider sector.

With so many stocks in the red, it’s logical that many AI fanboys are starting to get nervous.

Traders on Polymarket have become increasingly pessimistic. Contracts assessing whether the AI industry will experience a defined downturn showed approximately 54% odds by December 31, 2026, and 65% by June 30, 2027, according to Friday's market snapshot.

The market has attracted approximately $4.1 million in trading volume, but its definition of a bubble bursting is more demanding than a falling stock market. At least three specific events must occur within a single 90-day period, including Nvidia closing 50% below its all-time high, SOXX falling 40%, major AI suppliers experiencing similar collapses, or severe financial problems at AI developers.

SOXX has already fallen approximately 43% from its split-adjusted record closing price, satisfying one of the specified price thresholds on paper. Nvidia is approaching another, although crossing either threshold alone would not settle the contract.

The damage has also spread into cryptocurrencies, undermining expectations that 2026 would bring a sustained recovery. Bitcoin finished the week near $58,895, down 9.8% over the period and more than 50% below its 2025 peak.

The total cryptocurrency market capitalization fell to approximately $2.01 trillion, down from more than $4 trillion at its peak. As Decrypt reported Friday, the Crypto Fear and Greed Index dropped to 9, signaling extreme fear and its weakest reading since February.

This was supposed to be the year of the usual pre-halving moon. Instead, for the first time in the history of Bitcoin, the coin is on its way to have two consecutive red years.

Bitcoin has increasingly behaved like other speculative assets sensitive to liquidity conditions, particularly when investors expect borrowing costs to remain elevated.

The pressure is also visible in investment flows. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded $582.2 million in net withdrawals on Thursday, while forced liquidations added to the selling as Bitcoin broke below the psychologically important $60,000 level.

Owen Lamont, a portfolio manager at Acadian Asset Management and former University of Chicago finance professor, believes the warning signs have become difficult to ignore. After arguing in January that AI stocks were not yet in a bubble, he told Fortune this week that he now assigns a 70% probability to the U.S. stock market already being in one.

Lamont tracks four warning signs: excessive valuations, widespread belief in a speculative boom, new stock issuance, and investors pouring money into the market. His latest assessment suggests all four are now present, although his issuance figures are heavily influenced by SpaceX's massive IPO earlier this year.

Bank of America's November fund manager survey offers another indication of deteriorating confidence. Investors reduced their technology exposure by the most since 2006, and 52% expect at least one major AI hyperscaler to announce a capital spending cut within six months, Fortune reported.

The European Central Bank has also raised concerns about how AI development is being financed. Its November review estimates that capital expenditure by major American technology companies could reach $5.5 trillion cumulatively by 2030, with growing reliance on debt as infrastructure spending outpaces internally generated cash.

The similarities to the dot-com era are difficult to dismiss: investors are assigning enormous valuations to companies based on projected demand, businesses are racing to build infrastructure ahead of proven profitability, and financial markets are increasingly dependent on a small group of technology companies.

During the original dot-com collapse, the Nasdaq lost approximately 78% between March 2000 and October 2002. The present decline is considerably smaller, but history also offers a reminder that a technology can succeed commercially while the companies financing its expansion lose enormous amounts of market value.

There are important differences, however. Many of today's largest AI beneficiaries, including Nvidia, Alphabet, and Microsoft, operate profitable businesses with substantial existing revenues, unlike numerous internet startups whose valuations depended almost entirely on future growth.

Nvidia's latest quarterly results illustrate the contradiction. The company beat earnings estimates, reported record revenue and net income, and issued stronger-than-expected revenue guidance, yet its shares continued collapsing.

Richard Saperstein, chief investment officer at Treasury Partners, described the downturn as an "AI repricing" in comments to Reuters, citing slowing data center construction and tighter funding conditions. Lawrence Gillum, chief fixed income strategist at LPL Financial, offered a less alarming interpretation, saying the decline was "not a bubble bursting" and arguing that the AI investment cycle still has room to run.

Higher interest rates complicate both arguments. Investors are increasingly expecting the Federal Reserve to raise rates again in December, increasing financing costs for data centers while reducing the appeal of stocks whose valuations depend on profits years into the future.

The balance of evidence points to more than a routine bout of nerves, but less than a confirmed systemic bust. Stocks have repriced faster than the underlying technology has changed.

What happens next depends on whether actual demand for AI can justify the data-center spending spree—and whether the companies financing it can withstand tighter credit. The real test isn't simply whether Nvidia can keep selling chips. It's whether its customers can turn all that computing power into profits.

Right now much of the AI hype is just… hype. Markets are moving more by the perceived potential of AI in the future and not so much by what AI is actually doing right now.

Crypto investors have seen this before with ICOs, the TPS wars, the metaverse, the NFT fever and more. For many of them, the distinction may offer little comfort.

Bitcoin's slump shows how quickly markets can stop distinguishing between promising technologies and overpriced bets when confidence disappears. The dot-com crash didn't kill the internet; it killed the assumption that every internet company would make its investors rich.

AI may be the next world-changing technology to prove that being right about the future is not the same as making money from it.

#4889. Lucid CEO admits it ‘did not even know where cars were’ and lost orders

Lucid (LCID) CEO Silvio Napoli admitted on the company’s Q3 earnings call that, as recently as this summer, the automaker “did not even know where cars were.” Buyers placed orders, Lucid took up to two weeks to locate and deliver a car, and some of those buyers walked away.

That’s a rough admission from a company sitting on thousands of unsold vehicles. And it’s a problem Tesla ran into, and fixed, back in 2018 at more than ten times Lucid’s current volume.

Napoli made the comment when an analyst asked whether Lucid’s deliveries would go up or down in the fourth quarter. He said that at the start of Q3, the company had orders where it “would take sometimes two weeks to deliver to the customer, and so we would lose the order.”

He said Lucid now has visibility on which cars are being built, where they’re shipped, where they’re stored, and when they can reach a customer. A new commercial team also now sells the cars it knows are available on the day a customer wants one.

Lucid built 3,228 more vehicles than it delivered in the first half of 2026, including 5,500 built but only 3,093 delivered in Q1. Its inventory hit $1.38 billion on the balance sheet at the end of June, which is why we reported earlier this year that Lucid had to adjust production after a bloated Q1 inventory.

So Lucid had thousands of cars on lots and in transit, and it was still losing buyers because it couldn’t match a car to an order fast enough.

In Q3, Lucid cut production to 2,954 vehicles and delivered 3,806, down 7% year over year. Finished goods inventory fell by almost 15% during the quarter, according to new CFO Alexander De Bock. Inventory stood at $1.22 billion on September 30.

Tesla learned this lesson the hard way during the Model 3 ramp.

By Q3 2018, Tesla was making roughly 4,000 Model 3s per week. That’s more cars every week than Lucid delivers in a full quarter today. Deliveries tripled or quadrupled in some markets, and the bottleneck moved from the factory to the trucks.

Elon Musk tweeted in September 2018 that Tesla had “gone from production hell to delivery logistics hell.” A week later, he said Tesla was facing an “extreme shortage of car carrier trailers” and had started building its own car carriers to keep cars moving. Tesla also launched door-to-door delivery and asked existing owners to help at delivery centers.

It didn’t stop there. In November 2018, with the federal tax credit about to be cut in half for Tesla buyers, Musk said Tesla had bought trucking companies and locked in contracts with major haulers. Tesla moved cars east by truck instead of rail to cut weeks off transit times.

Tesla delivered 83,500 vehicles in Q3 2018, including 55,840 Model 3s, a record at the time.

When Model 3 reached Europe in February 2019, the first ship docked at Zeebrugge in Belgium and Tesla immediately had trouble processing the cars at the port.

About a week after the first ship arrived, Tesla ended its contract with the company preparing Model 3s at the port and took over the work itself. The target was 3,000 cars per week coming through one port.

It worked. By March 2019, Tesla delivered more than 5,300 Model 3s in Norway alone, making it the best-selling car in the country by a wide margin. A car that’s sitting at a port or on a lot doesn’t make anyone money. Tesla treated delivery speed as a sales tool, not a back-office job.

We later learned from Musk himself that this “logistic hell” was almost the nail in Tesla’s coffin. The CEO said that Tesla was at the brink of bankruptcy at the time.

I like to think people were relatively patient with Tesla's growing pains when Model 3 was ramping up in 2018, largely because there weren't many alternatives for a sporty, relatively affordable EV.

But it's 2026. Buyers have plenty of options now, and the patience that early EV adopters once had for these kinds of mistakes is long gone.

This is one of the most embarrassing admissions we’ve heard on an earnings call, and credit to Napoli for saying it out loud. Most executives would have blamed “demand” and moved on.

But it explains a lot. Lucid’s problem has never been the cars. The Air and Gravity are excellent products. The problem has been everything around them: service, software, and now we learn, basic inventory tracking. When you’re spending about $273,000 for every car you deliver, losing a buyer because you can’t find their car two weeks after they ordered is brutal.

Tesla solved this at 4,000 Model 3s a week while fighting for its own survival. Lucid is moving fewer than 300 cars a week. This isn’t a hard problem at that scale. It’s a discipline problem, and Napoli is right to call it that.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #4887. 10 Best Coffee Table Books to Buy in 2026, According to AD

Whether placed on a shelf or the coffee table in your living room, coffee table books can easily serve as pieces of decor. “I feel that books complete any room,” says global editorial director Amy Astley. “They are a record of your life and passions—I am profoundly connected to all of my printed matter!”

We’ve picked the best coffee table books from this year’s batch, and we’re thrilled to hole up with them until warmer days make their appearance. Here are our favorites from 2026’s bountiful assortment of debuts—many of them featuring AD-approved talents—all packed with invaluable design insights and gorgeous interiors past and present.

There are over 400 pages of inspiration-packed interiors to peruse in Amy Astley’s second coffee table book, AD at Home. Throughout the book you’ll find dreamy pictures and thoughtful essays pulled straight from our pages, including anecdotes from former cover stars like Marc Jacobs, Dakota Johnson, Gwyneth Paltrow, and more. “Even in a world of oversharing on social media, there is still such a frisson of entree in glimpsing the private world of a public figure, and AD will eternally share the fantasy,” Astley says in her editor’s letter. With the holidays approaching, we think AD at Home also makes a perfect gift for the collector, the design enthusiast, and anyone who’s just a little bit nosy about what’s going on behind a celebrity’s closed doors.

Created by AD France’s former editor-in-chief, Marie Kalt, La Bibliothèque de l’Amateur is a collection of compact, accessible monographs devoted to the great names of 20th-century design. The contents explore the work of acclaimed designers, including Alvar Aalto, Charlotte Perriand, Ettore Sottsass, while including emerging talents in the conversation as well. Each $30 volume pairs a brief introduction with about 60 images and detailed captions, offering an engaging visual primer on a designer’s life and work.

Global features director Sam Cochran’s debut coffee table book opens with the words, “Nothing is interesting unless it is personal.” His project, American Designers at Home, brings that idea to life beautifully. The volume takes you inside the homes of 20 leading designers across the country—including Andre Mellone, Kelly Wearstler, and Robin Standefer and Stephen Alesch of Roman and Williams—each reflecting its creator’s distinctive taste and personal passions. “As my dear friend Markham Roberts—one of the designers featured in the book—says, ‘If stuff makes you happy, you want it around,’ and this book makes me very happy,” says global interiors and garden director Alison Levasseur.

Levasseur says that Simon Upton’s most recent release with Rizzoli contains “beauty and poetry on every page.” The title is a nod to the iconic London A–Z: The detailed street atlas that every Londoner seemed to own in the 1980s and early 2000s, pre-Google Maps. Like its namesake, Upton’s book is an expert guide, specifically to a world of extraordinary interiors and visual pleasures. “Some of my favorite stories ever to appear in the pages of AD were photographed by Upton,” says Levasseur. “Many are gathered here, including those featuring Adwoa Aboah, Dakota Johnson, and Sienna Miller.”

This beautiful debut monograph not only showcases Chicago architect Joan Craig's elegant classical architecture, but it also spotlights the artistry behind it. “I love that Craig shares the spotlight with the often-unsung craftspeople who bring her interiors to life, including Brittany’s masters of straw marquetry and Bologna’s terrazzo artisans,” says senior digital editor Catherine Hong. “It's a tribute to design excellence and also the art of collaboration."

“Whether or not you’d ever dare to decorate with Sasha Bikoff’s over-the-top flair, this book is guaranteed to make a splash on your coffee table,” says Hong. Behind its trippy, hot-pink cover lies screaming color, clashing eras, and utter fantasy. The designer lives by her “Rule of Three Wows”—chronicled in the pages—and her book is, accordingly, loud, wildly fun, and a little bit insane in the best way.

Studio Valle de Valle’s debut monograph offers a refined window into the AD100 firm’s singular vision and creative evolution—surveying 10 projects from the past 10 years. Printed in Italy, with photography by Stephen Kent Johnson, the pages demonstrate the firm’s interior philosophy of creating spaces that feel personal, layered, and deeply inhabitable. “The edition isn’t just a tour de visual force, it’s an art object unto itself,” says Cochran. “I would expect nothing less from these brilliant auteurs."

“One of Meyer Davis’s greatest strengths is how cofounders Will Meyer and Gray Davis distill references, ideas, and desires into a highly refined vision,” says AD PRO senior editor Mel Studach. As client Jenna Lyons puts it in the book’s foreword, “They gave me clarity.” Beyond a printed body of work, The Language of Home is a testament to the collaborations that shape the studio's warm, reductive interiors. Designed with honest materials and a contemporary lens, the featured residences—a modern glass house in the Berkshires and an earthen dwelling in Puerto Escondido—reflect their inhabitants with a calm, clear ease.

The inaugural monograph from AD100 design duo Humbert & Poyet showcases their signature brand of eclectic French luxury, blending Art Deco glamour, midcentury modernism, and the decorative arts. Spanning residential and hospitality projects around the world, the book by AD contributing editor Gay Gassmann takes readers from a French Riviera villa to a Milan restaurant housed in a 17th-century chapel, with stops in New York, Paris, Monaco, and Seoul.

In New York: Atelier AM, Alexandra and Michael Misczynkski, the married couple behind the AD100 firm, shine a light on their New York projects, all beautifully captured by François Halard. Spotlighting a range of projects like elegant Manhattan residences and more relaxed Long Island retreats, contributor Ingrid Abramovitch says the book itself is “equally exquisite, with a cover resembling wood grain, and high-gloss pages printed in Italy.”

2026 AD New American Voices honoree Amber Guyton first burst onto the design scene by way of her hit blog, Blessed Little Bungalow. “Her debut book brings the same wit and spirit to the printed page in a kaleidoscope of color and pattern,” says Cochran. Senior shopping editor Nashia Baker adds that the pages “truly embody how to bring personality to any room.” Throughout the book, she makes a case for maximalism, and distills her approach into four pillars: joy, culture, layers, and intention.

“This book is perfect for cinephiles, design-lovers, or anyone with a case of wanderlust,” says senior digital editor Rachel Wallace. Film is one way to travel the world without ever leaving the sofa, and with her new project On Location, longtime AD contributor Cathy Whitlock takes readers on a global cinematic journey. The book goes behind the scenes of films that make you want to hop on a flight and never look back. Colorful Mexico City scenes from Frida and Marie Antoinette’s famous Palace of Versailles shooting location are both represented.

Organized by a series of chapter essays rather than specific projects, AD100 designer Dan Fink’s debut book explores bigger questions on how spaces, objects, and compositions move us across time. At the same time, the book spotlights several career-defining projects from his namesake studio, including previously unpublished interiors like Manhattan penthouses, Hawaiian beach retreats, Hollywood homes, and more.

One of the few completely fail-proof gifts is one of the best coffee table books of the season—weighty tomes whose thick, glossy pages feature stunning photographs and illustrations, to be specific. On a superficial level, they add ambiance, whether you place them in a library or a living room. But the best ones live in your home forever, while expanding your horizons far beyond. They also come across as far more memorable and thoughtful than the expected candle or bottle of champagne. Like works of fiction, there are limitless topics that can make deciding on an eye-catching hardcover (that doubles as home decor) seemingly impossible.

But that’s where we come in. Our team has gathered its favorite giftable recommendations for everyone from tennis enthusiasts to globe-trotters to interior design aficionados. From a book focused on hotels around the world to another that spotlights Tokyo style, there are plenty of ways to adorn the coffee tables of all the people on your holiday gift list. Most of them came out in 2024, but we’ve also included a few evergreen picks that make worthy gifts any year.

Plus, if you’re down to the wire on last-minute gifts, rest assured that most of these selections are available (and even marked down ahead of the holidays!) on Amazon.

Après-ski style needs no introduction in a season full of fuzzy Fair Isle sweaters, but it’s the cozy interiors of the chalets along the world’s most popular ski slopes that are worthy of a second look. From the wooden beams of an A-frame to the blazing fireplaces set within them, AD contributor Kathryn O’Shea-Evans pores over the finer details in this fun, wintery escape.

As associate digital editor Maya Ibbitson previously described it, “Alpine Style is a true compendium of resort luxury,” spanning spectacular chalets from Gstaad to Aspen and featuring the works of AD PRO Directory members such as Massucco Warner, Robbins Architecture, and Kara Adam Interiors.

Japan’s interiors are a perpetual reference for modern-day designers, and whether you’re a proponent of wabi-sabi principles or just looking for small space ideas, Tokyo Style will help you explore both.

Originally published in 1993, it’s since become a collector’s item and cult classic, and it was recommended by commerce writer Julia Harrison for anyone who would appreciate a more intimate glimpse of Tokyo’s dwellings: “Apartamento, an imprint that consistently delivers on elite design, has reintroduced Tsuzuki’s collection for us to enjoy in its full brilliance—that is, not a ‘fine’ condition original at $170 with $90 shipping from eBay.”

Prefaced with a foreword by Spike Lee, BLK MKT Vintage peers into the world of the Brooklyn antique shop’s endlessly curious founders Jannah Handy and Kiyanna Stewart. “This monograph teems with more than 300 studies of vintage ephemera and reads like a collage with context, or an I spy for your inner adult archivist,” writes Ibbitson of the book. For collectors, Sydney Gore pinpoints it as a must-have for your rotation.

Donald Judd’s considered, minimalist approach to design has fostered its own cult of acolytes who, these days, emulate his daybeds and chairs on Instagram.

For the true Juddheads, the Judd Foundation has produced a new book edited by his children Rainer and Flavin Judd that chronicles more than one hundred of his functional works produced between 1970 and 1992. “Mixed in are the juiciest bits: photos from past and present of those pieces in Judd-designed spaces in New York and Marfa,” Ibbitson writes.

AD’s Milan correspondent Laura May Todd penned a delightful homage to Italian living this year, one which spans from historic palaces in Venice to the homes of creatives like Luca Guadagnino along with midcentury Milanese apartments, so you can enjoy a small taste of la dolce vita on each page.

It was one of the AD PRO team’s favorite book releases this fall, one which should suit the person on your list who never got over their Italian study abroad or is manifesting a Sicilian villa in their future.

For the person on your list who delights in having people over, How They Entertain, released this fall, serves as a comprehensive guide to (and celebration of) the art of hosting.

Written by Pierre Sauvage, it’s chock-full of inspiration for your next dinner party, from recipes to design notes on arranging a tabletop to remember, as well as interviews with tastemakers like Laura Gonzalez about their particular philosophy on entertaining guests.

Considering this year’s smash summer hit Challengers and the enduring popularity of tenniscore (polos, skorts, and all), it’s clear that tennis is a lifestyle that has captured the imagination of many people, avid tennis players or not.

This book recommended by the AD PRO team earlier this year is dedicated to a more, uh, underserved cross section of the sport: the backdrops where athletes volley their way to tournament glory (clay, grass, asphalt, and beyond) in locales from Queens to Kenya.

AD100 designer Jeremiah Brent is no stranger to the pages of this very magazine, and his new book is a friends and family affair studded with stars, like Oprah and legendary choreographer Fatima Robinson, with whom Brent has formed close connections over the past 20 years.

We’ve previously described the book (layered with sentimental photos and new snapshots of contributors in their home) as “more scrapbook than design manifesto, a love letter to the spaces that people have inhabited and inherited over the years.” Read AD’s extensive interview with Brent for more backstory behind (and tidbits from) the book.

For art buffs with a fondness for the Harlem Renaissance—an era synonymous with greats like Aaron Douglas, known as the father of African-American art—this new book compiled by MoMA curator Denise Murrell is an excellent title to know. “Having this book is about as close as you’ll ever get to owning one of the original masterpieces featured in the Harlem Renaissance exhibition at The Met,” Gore says.

Self-interested plug here, but we really couldn’t publish a guide to coffee table books without mentioning our own, AD at 100: A Century of Style. For the design buff with an appetite for lush interiors and a healthy curiosity for how A-listers live, there’s no more satisfying holiday treat than this tome that delves into the homes of the Obamas, David Hockney, Diana Vreeland, and beyond. It also serves as a bird’s eye view of a century in design and architecture, including the works of Frank Gehry, David Hicks, Frank Lloyd Wright, and much, much more.

Architecture has a reputation for myopia when it comes to its foremost voices, but this book seeks to rectify the past with a fresh overview of some of its most brilliant underrepresented talents, including interviews with female architects around the globe such as Tatiana Bilbao, Liz Diller, and Julia Gamolina.

“100 Women offers a refreshing reframe of the built environment outside of the male-dominated Western canon, proving the benefits of sourcing alternative points of reference,” Ibbitson wrote of it in AD PRO’s guide to the best new design books out this past spring.

Colony founder Jean Lin has mentored and platformed some of the design world’s brightest rising stars, and in her first book out this year, she tapped a wide pool of creatives to explore the concept of collections that inform their work, from ceramicist Stephanie H. Shih’s cache of soy sauce bottles to Mira Nakashima’s stockpile of lumber inherited from her father, architect turned furniture maker George Nakashima.

That’s just one facet of the book Ibbitson previously described as an “encyclopedic, elemental study of artist studios, profiles, materials, and everything else under the design umbrella,” with plenty of wisdom imparted within its pages, from how-tos to styling tips. Read our interview with Lin for more of her notes on curating a collection of your own.

Luna Luna—a 1980s artist-led amusement park featuring works by icons of the art world such as Jean-Michel Basquiat, Keith Haring, Salvador Dalí, and David Hockney—is experiencing a resurgence as of late, what with a new retrospective at The Shed this year, along with a revival of the park itself in Los Angeles late last year.

This 2023 book takes a deeper dive into Luna Luna’s history blending fine art with the playful energy of a carnival—encompassing rides, interactive installations, and surreal sculptures. It comes recommended by associate visuals editor Shani Kravetz, who nods to the book’s breadth of archival media within, from photos to sketches, that are accompanied by behind-the-scenes stories.

For jet-setters who love to live large on vacation, Spencer Bailey’s Design: The Leading Hotels of the World (released just this month) is one sure way to sustain a sense of wanderlust. His new book takes readers on a visual journey to 80 countries around the globe, inviting them to mentally book into sumptuous spaces from The Okura in Tokyo to the Portrait Hotel in Milan. It’s rounded out by notable travel and design writers like Maria Cristina Didero and Mark Ellwood, who lend us their travel tips, on top of their reflections on the lodgings featured within.

#4884. Signal Calls OS-Level AI an 'Existential Threat' and Blocks Screenshots to Fight It

Signal Calls OS-Level AI an 'Existential Threat' and Blocks Screenshots to Fight It

Signal is moving to wall off its messages from the AI assistants now baked into smartphones, restricting screenshots so that systems like Google’s Gemini and Apple’s Siri can’t quietly scan and store users’ private conversations.

The encrypted-messaging nonprofit said Thursday it will block the latest agentic AI features built into iOS 27 and, on Android, automatically enable a “screen security” setting whenever it detects an AI assistant trying to read a user’s Signal screen.

The move extends a tactic Signal first used last year against Microsoft’s Recall, the Windows feature that captured periodic screenshots; the company blocked it to keep Recall from storing images of Signal messages by default.

The concern, Signal says, is that operating-system AI agents work by “scanning” whatever is on a user’s screen, which means Signal data gets captured and handed to those systems to process.

Once that happens, the content lands in whatever local database or remote server the AI is using, outside Signal’s control and beyond the encryption guarantees that have defined the app for a decade.

Users who still want to take screenshots can disable the protection in Signal’s settings, though the company warns that doing so could expose their messages, and their contacts’ messages, to the AI agents.

Signal also conceded the fix is incomplete: It doesn’t stop sneakier methods of access, such as pulling data straight from a device’s filesystem, and it can’t yet be implemented on macOS, where Signal says Apple lacks the documented tools to block screenshots reliably.

Signal also flagged third-party agents like Meta’s Muse, warning they exploit accessibility permissions meant to help people, not to serve as “a skeleton key” for AI.

The worry isn’t hypothetical: A journalist found that Muse had accessed his private iMessages despite his settings and then gave a false explanation of how it knew their contents, an episode that crystallized fears about how much agents can reach once granted deep device permissions.

Signal’s move lands amid a broader reckoning over AI agents accessing data they shouldn’t, from rogue agents breaching systems to banks scrambling to keep autonomous software out of sensitive accounts.

#4880. ECB to let banks pledge more tokenized securities as collateral

The ECB aims to start accepting securities issued on platforms licensed under the EU’s distributed ledger pilot program in June 2027.

The European Central Bank (ECB) approved a plan Monday to let banks borrow from euro area central banks against tokenized securities issued on platforms licensed under an EU pilot program.

The ECB plans to put the change into effect in June 2027, according to its announcement. The EU’s DLT Pilot Regime allows licensed platforms to handle trading or settlement of securities using distributed ledger technology.

Since March 30, banks have been able to pledge qualifying tokenized securities issued through central securities depositories that use distributed ledgers. Under Monday’s decision, a bank holding a qualifying bond issued on a licensed pilot platform could also use it to secure a central bank loan.

The securities must still meet the existing collateral rules of the Eurosystem, which comprises the ECB and the national central banks of the euro area. They must also be available to the Eurosystem through an approved link to a central securities depository that offers settlement on TARGET2-Securities, the Eurosystem’s securities settlement platform.

The Eurosystem expects to publish additional legal and operational requirements for the pilot platforms in spring 2027.

“A future-proof collateral framework must embrace new technologies without compromising on safety or the rigour of our risk assessment,” said ECB executive board member Isabel Schnabel.

The ECB said it is also exploring how banks could pledge securities issued and settled entirely on distributed ledgers. Through its Appia project, the Eurosystem plans to publish a blueprint in 2028 for how Europe’s tokenized wholesale financial markets could operate.

Related: EU taps industry to set standards for tokenized securities

#4879. Kia launches two new PV5 electric van variants in the UK

Kia opened orders for two new PV5 electric van variants, the PV5 Chassis and PV5 Cargo L2/H2, in the UK.

The PV5 is Kia’s first fully electric van from its Platform Beyond Vehicle (PBV) business. Built on Hyundai Motor’s dedicated modular skateboard platform, E-GMP.S, Kia can easily swap body styles to enable “limitless possibilities.”

So far, the PV5 has mostly been available in Passenger and Cargo body styles with several different variations.

Kia added the PV5 Chassis, an open-bed model that can be converted into just about anything. In the UK, Kia is offering conversions through TGS Automotive Group, including lightweight tipper, Dropside, and Box solutions.

The PV5 Chassis is available exclusively with the long-range 71.2 kWh battery in the UK, which Kia said could offer a potential WLTP range of up to 258 miles (416 km).

For reference, the PV5 Cargo L2/H1, which uses the same 71.2 kWh battery, offers a WLTP range of up to 258 miles (416 km). Other markets will also have a standard-range 51.1 kWh option.

The Chassis model is 4,610 mm long, 1,895 mm wide, and 1,935 mm tall, with a 2,995 mm wheelbase.

Its compact size and 5.5-meter turning radius, Kia said, make it easy to maneuver in city streets, construction sites, and other tight work areas.

Inside, the PV5 Chassis features two seats. Like other variants, it includes a 12.9″ navigation screen and a 7.5″ driver display with standard wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto.

The Chassis model supports DC fast charging up to 120 kW, enabling the battery to charge from 10% to 80% in about 30 minutes. Using an 11 kW AC supply, it takes about 6 hours and 30 minutes to charge from 10% to 100%.

Payload depends on the configuration. Kia said the Dropside offers a payload of up to 633 kg, while the Tipper can carry 561 kg.

In the UK, the PV5 Chassis is now on sale from £32,916 ($43,400) on the road, including the expected £5,000 ($6,700) UK Zero Emission Van Grant.

However, it’s an “incomplete vehicle,” as Kia describes it. The converted PV5 Dropside is priced from £40,110 ($53,500) on the road, including the ZEVG, while the PV5 Tipper starts at £41,310 ($55,000).

Kia also launched the larger PV5 Cargo L2/H2 in the UK. The new variant is 4,695mm long, 1,895mm wide, and 2,200mm high, with a 2,995mm wheelbase.

The taller roof opens up an internal cargo-area height of 1,815mm. It also offers a greater load capacity than the L1/H1 and L2/H2, with 5.2 cubic meters.

It’s available in three- and four-door configurations with the choice of two or three seats. The three-door versions have a maximum payload of 630 kg, while the four-door models have dual sliding doors and offer a payload of up to 600 kg. Both versions can tow up to 1,500 kg.

Like the Chassis model, the PV5 Cargo L2/H2 is available in the UK exclusively with the 71.2 kWh battery, which provides a WLTP range of up to 239 miles.

Inside, it features the same setup as the other PV5 variants, including a 12.9″ touchscreen navigation screen and a 7.5″ driver display.

The base PV5 Cargo L2/H2 Essential two-seat starts at £35,016 ($46,700), on the road, including ZEVG.

#4877. Fraudsters Used Fake Crypto Exchanges to Steal $37 Million, SEC Alleges

Fraudsters Used Fake Crypto Exchanges to Steal $37 Million, SEC Alleges

The Securities and Exchange Commission has charged eight entities with running a sprawling scheme that used fake crypto trading platforms and sham investment advisers to steal more than $37 million from U.S. investors.

The case targets eight Colorado-registered entities that the SEC says were likely operated by individuals overseas.

According to the complaint, filed in Colorado federal court, the defendants lured victims into believing they were working with a legitimate investment adviser and crypto trading platform, when both were entirely fake, “simply fronts to steal investor money.”

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The mechanics will sound familiar to anyone who has followed the rise of “pig butchering” scams. The SEC said the fraudsters advertised on social media to funnel investors into group and private chats, where operators posing as advisers first coached them to trade stocks on real U.S. markets before steering them toward a bogus crypto platform and pressing them to send as much money as possible. Victims believed they were buying real crypto assets; in reality, the funds were stolen.

To fake legitimacy, the operators went to unusual lengths, the SEC said, fabricating phony SEC certificates and even making filings with the SEC, FINRA and FinCEN, then pointing skeptical investors to those documents as proof they were real.

The scheme ran from August 2024 through May 2026 across four adviser-and-platform pairs, with the perpetrators abandoning one pair and spinning up the next every few months.

“The SEC continues to seek out and expose fraudulent investment scams that prey on U.S. investors,” said Laura D’Allaird, chief of the agency’s Cyber and Emerging Technologies Unit, urging the public to keep reporting such schemes.

The charged entities, carrying officious-sounding names like Convergent Wealth Advisors and Essential Crypto Exchange, face allegations of violating federal antifraud provisions, with several also accused of breaching the Investment Advisers Act.

The SEC is seeking injunctions, disgorgement and civil penalties. It credited cooperation from regulators in Hong Kong, Singapore, China and Switzerland, underscoring the cross-border nature of the fraud.

The case reflects a persistent threat, as fake platforms and social-engineering scams continue to drain billions from victims worldwide.

Chainalysis and others have documented how such operations, often run by organized crime from Southeast Asia, have scaled with the help of AI-generated personas and scripts, turning romance and investment cons into one of crypto’s costliest categories of fraud.

#4875. Tesla tuner Unplugged offers to take over Cybertruck production

Unplugged Performance, the biggest name in Tesla aftermarket tuning, wants to keep the Cybertruck alive. The company publicly offered on Tuesday to take the Cybertruck line off Tesla’s hands, funded by a consortium of investors.

It comes just four days after Elon Musk said Tesla would wind down Cybertruck production at Giga Texas around mid-2027, with the truck selling at roughly 12% of the 250,000 units a year Tesla planned for.

UP posted the pitch on X as “an open offer to Tesla.” The company says it fully supports putting Cybercab first and would run Cybertruck production “at no cost to Tesla’s floor space, headcount, or focus.”

In exchange, Tesla would collect a license fee plus FSD and Robotaxi network revenue on every vehicle built. UP even offered to let Cybercab get first call on battery cells. “We’ll wait our turn,” the company wrote.

UP founder Ben Schaffer made it clear that he supports Tesla’s all-in move to autonomy, but he wants to keep the Cybertruck program alive.

And it isn’t stopping at the pickup. UP says it would build the Cybertruck, a three-row “CyberSUV,” and an unmanned “CyberCargo” hauler. Two vehicles Tesla has sometimes referenced, but they were never unveiled.

The post passed 240,000 views within hours, and the replies are mostly Cybertruck owners and Tesla shareholders begging Musk to say yes. Neither Musk nor Tesla had publicly responded at the time of writing.

UP has a lot riding on this. It sells an entire Cybertruck product line, the UP Invincible lift and armor kits, and it has scored several contracts to turn Cybertrucks into police vehicles for law enforcement agencies. CEO Ben Schaffer once described the company’s purpose as doing “the things that Tesla maybe wants to do, but doesn’t have the bandwidth to do.” This is that idea taken to its extreme.

Musk framed the Cybertruck’s end as a space problem. He said Tesla needs to “make room for increased Cybercab production.”

If the Cybertruck program was successful, Tesla wouldn’t be killing it. Tesla also produces Model Ys at Giga Texas, and it is not shutting that down to make room for Cybercab.

As we reported when Tesla announced the wind-down, that explanation doesn’t hold up. Giga Texas has more than 10 million square feet of floor space, the Cybertruck line runs at about a quarter of its 125,000-unit installed capacity, and Texas had only a few hundred Cybercabs registered as of late October.

The real problem is buyers. Tesla delivered 8,295 “Other Models” in Q3, and we estimated 7,000 to 8,000 of those were Cybertrucks, a pace of about 30,000 trucks a year. That was a good quarter by Cybertruck standards.

Even that number flatters it. S&P Global Mobility data showed SpaceX bought 1,279 Cybertrucks in Q4 2025, about 18% of US registrations that quarter. Tesla also tried a cheaper RWD trim, killed it, launched a $60,000 AWD version, then raised prices by $5,000 in August. None of it fixed demand.

I’ll give UP credit for this: the offer calls Musk’s bluff. If the Cybertruck is really dying because Cybercab needs the room, here’s someone volunteering to take the line off Tesla’s plate. Problem solved.

Of course, it’s more complicated than that.

There are many ways this could fail or never happen in the first place, but there’s also a slight possibility that makes this possible.

First off, the backlash is real. If you look on social media and forums, yes, most of the discussion is about how Cybertruck is a failure, and Elon is lying about why Tesla is killing it, but there’s also a serious group of people, especially Cybertruck owners, who are genuinely upset that Tesla is killing the program because they love their trucks.

Most people have never driven a Cybertruck, but the steer-by-wire makes it a very addictive vehicle to drive, and even though the price is high, the value proposition for the specs is not bad compared to the very limited options for electric pickup trucks.

In other words, I wouldn’t necessarily bet against this effort, especially if they add an SUV variant and show the program some love with some tweaks here and there.

I could see a group of wealthy Cybertruck fans pooling money under Unplugged to make this happen, but getting Tesla to agree could be hard. It’s not unheard of for an automaker to sell a vehicle program to a dealer, like the Lotus Seven and Studebaker Avanti, but it would be a first at this scale.

Who is singing up to light their cash on fire? There is just no way that would work. Unless Tesla just gave them the designs and tooling. Then they are stuck the same problem with who is their market. There are 3 ev trucks coming that are cheaper better and more practical. Likey 3 or 4 more by the end of the decade.

You could make sense of it by looking at UP to Tesla as AMG to Mercedes. It’s not too big a stretch, considering UP isn’t just your average Tesla tuner. It even worked with the automaker on its own version of the Model S Plaid.

If UP were to become for Tesla what AMG has become for Mercedes, it could make some sense for the company to run a separate vehicle program that started under Tesla, but even as I write this, I realize that there’s very little chance this will happen.

But I wish them the best of luck, and I would be happy to see the Cybertruck live on.

#4873. Critics of Netflix’s FTX drama point to Obama’s producer credit

Series creator Graham Moore said he invented a scene in which Changpeng Zhao urges Alameda Research to trade ahead of FTX customers’ orders.

Critics who say Netflix’s new FTX drama favors Sam Bankman-Fried over Binance co-founder Changpeng “CZ” Zhao have pointed to former US President Barack Obama’s role as an executive producer.

DRW founder Don Wilson said Sunday that Netflix’s FTX drama The Altruists portrayed Bankman-Fried too sympathetically at the expense of Zhao.

In the fourth episode, Zhao’s character urges Bankman-Fried and Caroline Ellison to give Alameda Research advance access to FTX customers’ orders so it can trade ahead of them.

Series creator Graham Moore identified the scene as fiction in a Friday essay for Vulture. Moore wrote that he believed the pressure the pair faced was real, though “there’s no evidence that CZ ever specifically pressured FTX to front-run.”

Zhao called a clip from the series “complete BS” on Friday, a day after the eight-episode drama premiered. Investment research firm Farside Investors also singled out the front-running scene, saying the show cast Zhao as the villain.

Wilson called the clip an “epic fail by Netflix.” In a second post, he cited the former US president’s executive producer credit. Netflix lists both Barack and Michelle Obama as executive producers and names Moore and Jacqueline Hoyt as showrunners.

In a Sunday post on X, an account called StarPlatinum pointed to the Obamas’ producer credits and Bankman-Fried’s donations to Democrats. The post did not offer evidence that the donations influenced the show.

Related: Sam Bankman-Fried wrote to actor portraying him in Netflix series

Bankman-Fried made about $40 million in publicly reported political donations during the 2022 election cycle, mostly to Democratic candidates and groups. Federal prosecutors later said he also used FTX customer funds to make political contributions to candidates from both parties.

In November 2022, Zhao announced that Binance would sell its holdings of FTT, FTX’s token.

At Bankman-Fried’s 2023 trial, Ellison, Alameda’s former CEO, said she thought Zhao’s announcement “contributed to the situation.” She said FTX collapsed primarily because Alameda could not repay more than $10 billion in FTX customer funds.

Bankman-Fried was sentenced to 25 years in prison in 2024 for orchestrating a fraud that took more than $8 billion from FTX customers.

FTX’s bankruptcy estate sued Binance and Zhao in November 2024, seeking $1.76 billion paid in a 2021 share repurchase. The estate also sought damages over Zhao’s public statements. Binance called the claims “meritless.”

In July, US Bankruptcy Judge Karen Owens dismissed the claims over Zhao’s statements because FTX’s own misconduct barred them. She allowed the estate to pursue its separate claim over the buyback.

#4872. GTA 6 Leaker Dangles a PC Port Behind a $15 Million Crypto Pump

GTA 6 Leaker Dangles a PC Port Behind a $15 Million Crypto Pump

A notorious Grand Theft Auto 6 leaker is holding a potential PC version of the game hostage to a cryptocurrency payday, promising to release the final file needed to make a leaked build playable once its meme coin hits a $15 million market cap.

The leaker, who goes by Cyberleek, has released what it claims is a near-complete 209 GB Xbox build of GTA 6 from November 2026 via torrent, according to a report from PC Gamer.

The one piece withheld is the main executable, the file that would let modders begin emulating the game on PC, potentially bringing it to the platform long before Rockstar Games intends.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Here’s the crypto catch. Cyberleek says it will release the executable on Nov. 23 regardless, but will drop it early if its token, called CYBERLEEK, reaches a $15 million market cap. That’s half the $30 million target the leaker floated in October.

The pressure tactic doesn’t appear to be working: the token is trading around $0.002 as of the weekend, with a market cap of roughly $1.3 million, more than $13 million short of the goal. The coin is down some 20% on the week, but the numbers will fluctuate wildly given the very thin liquidity on a small cap coin.

The setup has drawn open skepticism that it’s a cash grab, or worse. Observers have warned it could be an attempt to pump the coin before a rug pull, the crypto term for abandoning a token after luring buyers in. Cyberleek has also claimed to hold a PS5 build as a fallback if the Xbox files can’t be made to work.

The episode sits at the increasingly busy intersection of crypto, piracy, and AI. Modern Xbox games are architecturally closer to PC software than PlayStation titles, and AI coding tools have accelerated the kind of decompilation work that makes fast ports plausible, the same dynamic behind a wave of AI-assisted game ports this year.

It also caps a chaotic launch window for GTA that crypto has repeatedly crashed. One trader banked around $66,000 on a GTA 6 meme coin amid ticker confusion, and the game’s disc-free, digital-only release has fueled broader debate, with GameStop’s CEO arguing that digital-only games have hurt consumers.

None of the leaked files have been independently confirmed as genuine, and there is still no official GTA 6 PC version; Rockstar, famously litigious, could respond with legal action rather than a port.
