#4827. Tesla confirms rare new delivery of 50 Semi electric trucks in Chicago

Tesla has confirmed a new batch of Tesla Semi deliveries, with its official Semi account saying today that “Chicago’s first 50 Semis” are on their way to the city.

It’s a rare data point. Tesla doesn’t disclose Semi deliveries, and it claims its new Nevada factory can build 50,000 of them a year.

The post, published on the @tesla_semi account on X at noon ET today, shows four Semis lined up at sunset under the caption “Hello Windy City.” Tesla didn’t name the customer.

Hello Windy CityChicago’s first 50 Semis en route for delivery pic.twitter.com/75kig8yWsb

But the only 50-truck Semi order tied to Chicago belongs to HMD Trucking. The family-owned carrier was founded in Chicago in 1998, runs more than 650 trucks (almost all diesel), and ordered 50 Semis right after Tesla’s production event in September.

HMD took its first truck on November 2 and says it’s the first Tesla Semi registered in Illinois. COO Michael Mahler said Tesla “has been transparent with us regarding the capabilities of Semi every step of the way.”

The company ran a Semi for more than 4,000 miles over the summer and averaged 1.74 kWh per mile, right in line with Tesla’s 1.7 kWh/mile claim. The first trucks will mostly handle local runs around Chicago, and HMD is installing its own chargers there since Tesla’s Megacharger network is still concentrated along the I-5 and I-10 corridors in the West.

Owner Henry Malukas told MotorTrend the deal is worth about $15 million. That’s roughly $300,000 per truck, in line with the ~$290,000 Long Range pricing we reported when the first truck came off the high-volume line and a long way from the $180,000 Tesla advertised in 2017.

Here’s the problem: it’s nearly impossible to tell how many Semis Tesla is actually building.

Tesla launched volume production at its Sparks, Nevada factory on September 24, after pushing the ramp out of 2025. It says the 1.8 million sq ft plant is built for 50,000 trucks a year. But it never gave a ramp schedule, and its quarterly production and delivery reports only count “passenger vehicles sold to customers.” The Semi isn’t in there.

So we’re left with proxies. Tesla says its Semi fleet has over 98% uptime, without saying how big that fleet is. Before this year, the largest Semi fleet was PepsiCo’s, at 86 trucks in 2024. California’s truck voucher program received 965 Semi applications between January 2025 and February 2026, which points to real demand but says nothing about output.

The order book is the other clue. Einride ordered 500 Semis in August, WattEV has 370 on order, IMC Logistics wants 50 for California, and the ZET SCALE alliance picked Tesla as main supplier for 2,500 trucks. And now HMD’s 50 are rolling into Chicago, one of the first Semi fleets that will have to deal with a Midwest winter. Wisconsin’s Paper Transport has also been piloting a Semi in the Chicago market since the summer.

None of that adds up to a production rate, but it does look like Tesla is slowly ramping up deliveries.

Tesla keeps the Semi numbers vague on purpose. It’s happy to talk about a 50,000-truck-a-year factory, and it posts hero shots when a batch ships, but it won’t give the one number that shows whether the factory is anywhere close to that.

I think the semi is going to be a big seller but its going to take some time to produce enough of them and for companies to gather enough data. Once the investment is justified, there will be customers and repeat customers. After that, it will only be a matter of time before other electric semi truck manufacturers start popping up with offerings and thats a good thing for everyone.

The 50,000 figure is what makes the Semi look like a real business to investors instead of a niche program that took nine years to get going. Tesla has a long history of quoting capacity long before it reaches it, so we shouldn’t treat “built for 50,000” as anything more than a ceiling.

Our read: Tesla is still far from that 50,000 rate. Fifty trucks for one customer is 0.1% of a year at full capacity, and if Tesla were shipping in large volumes, a single 50-unit batch wouldn’t get its own post. But the ramp has clearly started, the order book is real, and it’s plausible Tesla gets into the thousands by the end of the year.

Good for HMD, too. A 650-truck diesel fleet in a cold market is exactly the kind of customer the Semi has to win. Malukas admitted “we don’t have real numbers yet” on savings, and HMD’s own blog is fairly sober about cost and winter range. Local routes, depot charging and a Chicago winter is the right way to find out. It all sounds plausible for the Tesla Semi.

If you’re running an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. #4824. Tesla extends Cybertruck production to late summer 2027 amid fan backlash

Elon Musk now says Tesla will keep building the Cybertruck “through the end of summer 2027” to “work through more of the Cybertruck order backlog.” That buys the electric pickup roughly two to three more months, five days after Tesla first announced it was killing it.

The reprieve lands as a growing number of Tesla’s own fans push back on the decision, and on what it says about Tesla’s future as a company that sells cars to people.

On November 6, Musk said Tesla would wind down Cybertruck production at Giga Texas “around the middle of next year” to make room for Cybercab. On November 9, he wrote the line would shut “in about 8 months” and that Tesla would “have to return deposits for a lot of people.” Now it’s the end of summer.

In order to work through more of the Cybertruck order backlog, production will be extended through the end of summer 2027

The reasons have moved around just as much. First it was factory space, then “a very limited number of smart, dedicated people,” then the “opportunity cost” of the truck’s complexity. We went through each of them: Tesla is ending the Cybertruck as sales stall at about 10% of plan, Musk’s demand claim doesn’t survive the numbers, and Musk admitted Tesla doesn’t have the talent to run more than one vehicle program.

And the “backlog” is one Tesla built itself. Before the announcement, the truck was selling at a pace of roughly 30,000 units a year, about 10% of the 250,000 Tesla planned for, on a line with over 125,000 units of installed capacity. Telling people the truck is going away creates a last-call rush. That’s not demand Tesla was missing before.

Tesla also raised Cybertruck prices by up to 10% the same day and paused orders for the base Dual Motor. The cheapest Cybertruck you can order is now $92,490, up from $69,990 in early August.

Some of the people in that backlog have been waiting a while. Several cheering in Musk’s replies say they’re still waiting on the $59,990 Cybertruck Tesla sold for “10 days only” in February. One asked whether Tesla will prioritize Premium and Cyberbeast orders over theirs. Musk didn’t answer.

The most telling replies weren’t about the truck. Chuck Cook, one of the best-known FSD testers in the Tesla community, quote-tweeted Musk asking him to “change your mind,” adding: “Cybercab won’t haul my bees.”

Another longtime Tesla supporter, Phil Trubey, asked for “a vehicle roadmap post end of summer 2027,” followed by: “Will Tesla make any other vehicle?”

That’s the right question, and Tesla hasn’t announced a single new consumer vehicle to answer it. Tesla started 2026 with five consumer vehicle programs: Model S, Model X, Model 3, Model Y, and Cybertruck. It ended Model S and Model X production in the spring, citing “autonomy.” The Cybertruck goes next. By fall 2027, Tesla’s consumer lineup will be the Model 3 and Model Y, two vehicles that launched in 2017 and 2020.

Tesla’s answer to the affordable car it promised for years was stripped-down “Standard” versions of those same two vehicles. In China, its second-biggest market, Tesla turned Giga Shanghai into an export hub, and domestic retail sales crashed 66% in October to the lowest level in over four years.

Even Unplugged Performance, the biggest Tesla tuner around, offered to take the Cybertruck line off Tesla’s hands and pay a license fee. People want this truck built. Tesla doesn’t.

We’ve been warning about this for years, and for years we got called haters for it.

The pattern has been clear since Tesla swapped its affordable car plans for Robotaxi in 2024. No new consumer platform. Model S and X killed “for autonomy.” Cybertruck killed “for Cybercab.” A Roadster pitched as a thruster stunt nine years after its unveiling. Every product decision points away from selling cars to people and toward robotaxis and Optimus, two businesses that don’t generate meaningful revenue today.

I am beginning to suspect that Elon does not always tell the truth.

As long as we have an economic climate that allows massive amounts of money to be made off of speculation as opposed to actual product, future bots and robotaxis have a great deal of value whether they actually become viable or not.

A two-to-three-month extension doesn’t change any of that. It’s a gesture to the loudest part of the fan base, and it happens to let Tesla sell a few thousand more trucks at prices up to 10% higher than last week. Fine. But the people who bought into Tesla’s mission to accelerate the world’s transition to sustainable energy are now realizing that mission was quietly rewritten, and the word “sustainable” didn’t make the cut.

Tesla is becoming a company that sells two aging EVs to fund a bet on autonomy. That might work out for shareholders. We have our doubts, given Tesla is still behind Waymo by every public metric. But it’s a lot less exciting for the people who wanted the next great Tesla car.

The question for fans now: when the Model 3 and Model Y get their own “opportunity cost” post on X, will anyone actually be surprised?

If you’re buying an EV, Cybertruck or not, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4822. How market depth shapes the price of a weekend Bitcoin sale

Over 14 hours, Bitcoin traded on Kraken’s Canadian dollar market at an average 37.3% discount to a benchmark based on the exchange’s US dollar trades.

A narrow bid-ask spread can conceal the cost of selling 10 Bitcoin. If the best bid covers only part of the order, a desk seeking to sell the full amount immediately must accept lower prices for the balance.

From Friday evening to Saturday morning, 592.24 Bitcoin (BTC) changed hands on Kraken’s Canadian dollar market at an average of 51,886 Canadian dollars per coin. At the Bank of Canada’s Friday exchange rate, the benchmark was CA$82,798 per coin.

The price available to sellers varied even within a single second on Saturday. On the Canadian dollar market, the first of 104 fills fetched CA$31,000 per coin. Across the 10.42 BTC sold, the average was CA$25,505, nearly 18% lower.

Bitcoin’s Kraken anomalous discount chart produced by Cointelegraph with AI assistance.

Related: Bitcoin price drops to $60K as Anthropic drops to $87 in tech sell-off

Coinbase Institutional’s study of more than 40 exchanges found that weekday spot Bitcoin volumes in 2025 were two to three times weekend volumes. For a desk that divides a sale into smaller orders, the benefit of waiting depends on how quickly buyers replenish the bids and at what price.

#4821. Jensen Huang’s Wealth Rises $36 Billion as Nvidia Surge Burns Short Sellers for $12 Billion

Jensen Huang’s Wealth Rises $36 Billion as Nvidia Surge Burns Short Sellers for $12 Billion

Jensen Huang’s net worth surged by more than $36 billion on Monday after Nvidia’s board reinstated him as CEO, a dramatic reversal that sent the chipmaker’s stock rocketing and handed its short sellers their worst day in years.

Nvidia shares jumped more than 33% in Monday morning trading, to around $167, recovering the ground lost after Huang resigned 11 days earlier amid a scandal over extramarital affairs, including one with a woman U.S. officials reportedly suspected of spying for China.

The rebound added roughly $36.6 billion to Huang’s fortune, according to Forbes, lifting the co-founder, who owns an estimated 3.5% of Nvidia, to 13th on its global wealth ranking.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Nvidia with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

On the other side of the trade, investors betting against Nvidia were nursing about $12.2 billion in paper losses, their biggest single-day hit since at least 2019, according to data firm ORTEX cited by Reuters.

The reversal wiped out more than $10.5 billion in gains those short sellers had booked just last week. Still, ORTEX noted the bears remain up roughly $14 billion since Oct. 29, and with only about 1% of Nvidia’s float sold short, co-founder Peter Hillerberg characterized the move as a reaction to the news rather than a full short squeeze.

The catalyst was Nvidia’s board voting unanimously to bring Huang back, less than two weeks after his abrupt exit. The return followed President Donald Trump publicly pushing for it, with the president saying the board had sought “assurance on the national security threat” tied to the espionage allegations.

The reinstatement rippled well beyond Nvidia. The news lifted the broader market and dragged crypto up with it, with Bitcoin rallying as risk appetite returned, even as Anthropic shares sank.

The S&P 500 climbed about 5% and the Nasdaq rose nearly 7% on Monday morning, clawing back losses from a Friday selloff driven by anxiety over AI spending.

The episode caps one of the strangest corporate sagas in recent memory: a founder toppled by a spy-tinged scandal, replaced, then restored within days as the president intervened and markets whipsawed on every turn.

For Huang, it marks a remarkable personal recovery, his wealth and his role both snapping back almost overnight.

For Nvidia, whose chips underpin both the AI boom and the data centers increasingly entwined with crypto, the saga is a reminder of how much the market’s fortunes hinge on a single company, and a single person.

#4818. Kashmir’s Textile Legacy Inspires a Sumptuous New Line of Home and Fashion Accessories

Few artisanal traditions are so linked with a place that its geographical namesake winds up in the global lexicon. In the case of fine wool shawls from Kashmir—prized among 19th-century Europeans, including Joséphine Bonaparte—the word cashmere stuck. For Kadri Mujtaba, a fascination with these textiles came early. Born into a family of merchants and weavers in the region’s city of Srinagar, he observed a deep-rooted culture around hand-spun yarn and small wooden looms.

A bouquet of lotus pods held up against the brand’s Mountain blanket in pine

A Doorway Sozni twill blanket draped over a chair

“It’s the original work-from-home idea,” he says of the technique, passed down for centuries. Amid encroaching mechanization, Mujtaba has stayed committed to slow craft, cultivating a network of some 2,000 artisans over the past two decades. Initially he’d create custom pieces for designers and brands (Pierre Yovanovitch, The Row, Luis Laplace) and host high-end trunk shows. That is how Jonathan Renker—via his father, a longtime customer—first encountered the wares. Now, he and Mujtaba have partnered on Kadri: a new brand of cashmere accessories and home goods that marry Kashmiri refinement and streamlined design.

A Jaali Kaani blanket on the grounds of Pari Mahal garden

A wood swift of cashmere yarn at the Kadri atelier

Shawls and scarves from Kadri’s debut collection

The company debuts with a collection of scarves, throws, and blankets. The cofounders enlisted creative director Dan Thawley and textile designer Maeva Elfassi, who distilled the Kashmir Valley landscape into shades of tobacco, lime, vermilion, and—as a nod to Srinagar’s Dal Lake—dusty blue. Wool from Changthangi goats, which graze across the plateaus of nearby Ladakh, is spun by hand, resulting in airy, ultrasoft knits. More impressive still is the subtlety on display. A bouclé piece has the immersive pull of a Color Field painting, while a quilted scarf joins two paper-thin layers. Just one blanket requires months of work and a balletic dance of bobbins.

“What makes Kadri so special is this research-and-development approach,” says Renker, describing a push toward novel applications while holding fast to the handmade vernacular. “The only electricity that’s used are the overhead lights on a cloudy day.”

#4817. Perps and Swaps: How to Trade the News on Decrypt

Perps and Swaps: How to Trade the News on Decrypt

Readers have traditionally learned about markets in one place and traded them in another. A story about Bitcoin, a public company or a commodity might shape a view, but acting on it meant opening an exchange or a trading app and starting over.

Decrypt set out to close that gap in September 2026, when it announced The Information Exchange: a network that brings together news, data, market prices, probabilities and the ability to trade, around the subjects readers follow.

The Information Exchange sees Decrypt delivering both the reporting and the market infrastructure that links information to trading, while the Solana blockchain provides the network on which trades settle, including token swaps and perpetual futures.

This guide explains what each one is and how to trade it on Decrypt.

Everything on The Information Exchange runs through a Decrypt Money Account, which launched on Solana in October 2026.

A Money Account holds one balance that funds every product on Decrypt: swaps, perpetual futures and prediction markets.The account is self-custodial, meaning Decrypt does not hold users' private keys and cannot act on their behalf. It is designed to work without seed phrases, and network fees are covered, so users don't need to hold SOL, Solana's native token, to pay for transactions.

A swap exchanges one token for another at the current market price. Instead of placing an order on an exchange and waiting for it to fill, the trader sends one token and receives another in a single transaction.

Swaps on Decrypt are powered by Jupiter, a Solana trading platform that offers swaps, lending, borrowing, perpetual futures and portfolio tools. Jupiter finds prices across Solana's trading venues, so users don't need to choose where a trade is executed.

At time of publication, swaps cover a select group of major cryptocurrencies and Solana tokens, as well as tokenized stocks, commodities and other real-world assets.

As of November 2026, the Assets panel of the Portfolio page groups holdings into crypto, stocks, commodities and collectibles, and lists tokens including SOL, BTC, ETH, XRP and HYPE.

Perpetual futures, or perps, are contracts that let traders bet on whether an asset's price will rise or fall without owning the asset. Unlike traditional futures, they have no expiry date. A trader who expects the price to rise goes "long," and one who expects it to fall goes "short."

Perps are usually traded with leverage, which lets a trader control a larger position than their deposit would otherwise allow. Leverage magnifies gains and losses alike, and if the market moves against a position far enough, it can be liquidated, or closed automatically, wiping out the trader's margin.

Perps on Decrypt are powered by Phoenix, a fully onchain perpetuals exchange on Solana developed by Ellipsis Labs—making Decrypt the first media platform to offer perps trading alongside its journalism.

As of November 2026, Phoenix offers more than 100 perps markets on Decrypt, covering crypto, U.S. stocks, gold and pre-IPO companies. Markets trade around the clock, with leverage of up to 40x, according to Phoenix.

Decrypt plans to keep adding products to The Information Exchange, including Situation Rooms: pages that combine breaking news, live data, prices, probabilities and trading around a single subject.

Decrypt operates The Information Exchange, Decrypt Money Accounts and the swaps, perpetual futures and prediction market products described in this guide.

Perpetual futures are leveraged products and carry a high risk of loss. Availability may vary by region. Nothing in this guide is investment advice.

#4815. Cross River Bank enters Bitcoin-backed lending with $250K loans

The US bank is offering Bitcoin-backed loans ranging from $10,000 to $250,000 through LendingUSA, allowing borrowers to access cash without selling their holdings.

Cross River Bank and consumer financing company LendingUSA have launched a Bitcoin-backed lending program offering loans of up to $250,000, allowing eligible borrowers to access funds without selling their Bitcoin holdings.

According to a Monday announcement, borrowers can take out loans ranging from $10,000 to $250,000, using their Bitcoin (BTC) as collateral. They can borrow 30%, 50% or 70% of the value of their coins, depending on the loan terms. Eligible borrowers can either make interest-only payments during the loan term or postpone payments until the loan is due.

However, the loans carry risks if Bitcoin’s price falls. Borrowers could be required to provide additional collateral or repay part of their loans, with pledged Bitcoin subject to liquidation if collateral requirements are not met.

Bitcoin traded at $62,788 as of 4:13 p.m. UTC on Monday, up 3.03% over the past 24 hours but down 28.20% year-to-date, according to TradingView data.

The partnership marks Cross River’s first Bitcoin-backed lending program as a loan originator. Bitcoin pledged as collateral will be held by a qualified third-party custodian throughout the loan term.

Other Bitcoin-backed lenders include Ledn, which has offered crypto-backed loans since 2018, and Coinbase, which introduced Bitcoin-backed loans through Morpho in January 2025. US banks are also involved, including Column N.A., which originates some loans offered through Strike, and Fortis Bank, which participates in commercial lending arrangements with Unchained.

Cointelegraph is committed to providing independent, high-quality journalism across the crypto, blockchain, AI, and fintech industries.

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

#4814. 76 AD-Approved Interior Photographers in the Northeast

Grand staircases. Cozy breakfast nooks. Color-drenched walls. You name it, these interior photographers in the Northeast have shot it, in daylight and darkness, maximizing views of the park or the coast, the backyard or the city street far below. Their images are portraits of homes, capturing a space’s personality and highlighting the talents of its designer. Read on to meet AD-approved interior photographers in the Northeast (and, in many cases, beyond).

A beachfront home in Santa Barbara, California.

Classical proportions and carefully composed details give William Abranowicz’s interior photos the poise of a still life. His background as a fine art printer shows in images that reward looking beyond the statement furniture to the smaller objects around it.

This vibrant San Francisco home designed by Noz Nozawa is sometimes called “the Rainbow House.”

Brittany Ambridge makes colorful rooms shine and white rooms look luminous without losing the details that give them dimension. Her lighting approach draws on techniques learned shooting film, translating a home’s atmosphere whether the mood is airy and expansive, or bold and colorful. She has worked with talents including Jeremiah Brent, Nate Berkus, Charlotte Moss, and Gachot Studios.

Find your next creative collaborator using the AD PRO Sourcebook. Start exploring top interior photographers, stylists, decorative painters, millworkers, and more now.

The living room of Kate Upton and Justin Verlander’s penthouse in New York

Dramatic natural lighting is often heightened and accentuated in Gieves Anderson’s photographs, in both moody, highly patterned rooms as well as light and airy spaces. He knows just how to make the most of a perfectly cast shadow.

An interior at the Maison Brummell Majorelle in Marrakech, Morocco.

Trained in classical and modern dance, New York City–based Emily Andrews parlays that craft’s contrasting principles of line and weight, positive and negative space, into her photography work. The beautifully captured balancing acts have garnered the attention of AD, WSJ Magazine, The New York Times, and others.

Glassworks offers a kaleidoscopic moment in a moody dining room.

If you love the enviable homes of Athena Calderone—a.k.a. EyeSwoon—Jessica Antola is a name you need to know. Antola has captured Calderone’s Instagram-famous spaces, as well as countless others.

The minimalist Brooklyn apartment of Coco Romack and Mars Hobrecker.

Winnie Au’s photography brings out the color and personality in each space she so smartly captures, whether it’s a playful Palm Springs hotel, a boldly designed New York apartment, or the creative studios of artists and makers.

A dining room in Greenwich, Connecticut, designed by Alison Hammatt Home.

From Nantucket antiques to contemporary interiors, Jane Beiles approaches a room as a still life, balancing textures, shapes, and scale rather than letting one trophy piece steal the show. Based in Fairfield, Connecticut, she has photographed work by Gil Schafer, Katie Ridder, Stonefox, and Bennett Leifer.

Chloe Fineman’s West Village home, designed by Tali Roth.

Andrew Bui loves dynamic, expressive lighting and strives to create images with a timeless quality to them. He comes from two generations of award-winning restaurateurs, and his food photography is just as delectable as his interior shots.

Filmmaker ShawnLevy’s dining room in Manhattan, designed by AD100 designer Jeremiah Brent

Raised in Texas and now based in Brooklyn, Max Burkhalter doesn’t shy away from assignments in the Lone Star State, having shot projects like David Adjaye’s crystalline Ruby City contemporary art center. Still, Burkhalter reps hard for Brooklyn and has collaborated with brands including In Common With and Roll & Hill.

Inside a 19th-century Brooklyn town house renovated by Hatchet Design & Build.

Nicholas Calcott has worked on a variety of editorial, commercial, and self-assigned projects across topics including home and belonging, creativity, and the future. He’s of Arabic, Jewish, and British heritage and also speaks French, so he brings an international sensibility to assignments.

The daughter of an architect and a photographer, Anita Calero brings an artistic eye to her still-life photography, and is equally adept at shooting nature and interiors. A celebrated fine-art photographer as well, Calero works between home bases in New York, Colombia, and Miami, living her philosophy that beauty is everywhere and “nothing is ugly.”

Photographer Ethan James Green’s apartment in Brooklyn Heights

A regular contributor to AD, New York, and other publications, Seth Caplan documents spaces with a keen sense of presence, emphasizing warm tones and moody shadows. He loves a good portrait and also shoots images for homeware brands including Bed Threads, Framebridge, and Caraway Home.

Sean Davidson beautifully captures the stillness of an organically neutral interior.

A favorite of many AD100 and AD PRO Directory studios, Sean Davidson has translated his industrial design background into a successful career in photography, frequently working with top fashion brands and design publications alike.

Polished yet raw, Blaine Davis’s style and compositions result in interior shots and portraits that are crisp and authentic—not unlike the glossy pages of the magazines in which they appear. (Including AD, of course.)

A historic Fifth Avenue mansion, built by Stanford White.

A documentary background shapes Hayley Ellen Day’s interest in the relationship between people and their spaces. Her interiors range from ornate salons to playful bathrooms, with small details offering clues to the lives unfolding beyond the frame.

Inside a reimagined 19th-century farmhouse in Southampton, New York, designer Ariel Okin animates a historic New York refuge using color and character.

Donna Dotan’s obsession with clean lines and natural light is evident across her catalog of interior shoots, in which spaces are portrayed as sun-kissed and in dazzling color. Her work has graced the pages of all major design publications, including AD.

A New York City apartment for Casameu New York, styled by Rebecca Bartoshesky

Combining strong compositional elements with a sense of warmth and sensitivity, Pippa Drummond—a Kiwi photographer now based in New York City—has been commissioned by the likes of Dwell and Herman Miller.

Bovina Farm and Fermentory in New York’s Catskill Mountains.

Soft daylight and tactile finishes lend Sarah Elliott’s interiors an easy, unhurried feeling. Her photographs for Upstate invite a closer look at country living, while her work with Elizabeth Roberts, Nune, and others brings that intimacy to many homes.

A bedroom on the Upper East Side of New York, designed by The Interiors Concierge and styled by Pablo Olguin

The skill with which Patrick Fraser is able to portray history and character in his images is captivating. Born in the UK and recently relocated to Connecticut from the West Coast, he often takes inspiration from objects, landscapes, and people that have been forgotten or ignored.

A living room featuring a rug designed by Colin King for Beni Rugs.

Billal Taright’s eye gravitates toward sculptural objects, spare arrangements, and the space around them. Warm tones and considered framing lend even a shelf or mantel quiet presence. The New York photographer moves between interiors, fashion, and collectible design, with Ralph Lauren Home, Colin King, and Luke Edward Hall also among his clients.

A kitchen by Jenna Chused of Chused and Co. for Isla Porter’s Tastemakers series.

Christian Torres makes interiors feel inviting and lived in, drawing on a practice that also spans hospitality and travel. He can participate from concept and location scouting through styling and lighting, helping designers carry a cohesive idea through the shoot.

Pattern and pigment freshen a traditional sitting room

No design detail is lost on Lesley Unruh, a Tennessee native and alum of Domino. Unruh, who now lives in New York City, has worked with Anna Burke Interiors, Malene Barnett, and CeCe Barfield Thompson, among others.

The grand staircase in a historic Georgian home in Pennsylvania designed by Haldon House.

Jason Varney brings a painterly handling of light and a strong feel for color to restaurants, hotels, and homes. His food photography makes him particularly useful for hospitality projects where the atmosphere extends from the dining room to the table, but homes are always on the menu too.

An indoor/outdoor living area in Sag Harbor, designed by Garnett.DePasquale Projects.

Nicholas Venezia brings architectural training and eight years at Selldorf Architects to his photography. His process connects a project’s underlying ideas with its visual story, a valuable trait when a designer wants the photographs to communicate more than appearances.

A 1909 mansion in New Jersey belonging to director Ruben Fleischer, designed by Studio DB.

If Brooklyn brownstones and beautifully considered hotels populate your design daydreams, spend some time with Matthew Williams’s portfolio. His interiors photography moves between whole-room views and studies of furniture, materials, and smaller objects. His portfolio includes homes by Elizabeth Roberts and Studio DB, giving designers examples of how a project can unfold across a sequence of images.

Tour a strikingly modern home in East Hampton

A resident of New York City but available for work worldwide, Tim Williams captures a sense of life in his photographs of commercial and residential interiors. His images recently graced AD PRO’s coverage of David Thomas’s new Madison Avenue outpost for Manolo Blahnik.

The powder room of a West Village townhouse designed by Starrett Ringbom.

A veteran of the fashion industry, Regan Wood shifted her focus to architecture and interior photography some time ago. Still, she sees a connection between them in that both form an “outward expression of one’s inner life.”

#4812. BYD’s full-size luxury SUV has a wheel problem, and it’s fixing it for free

BYD is apologizing after a supplier error left some owners of its new full-size flagship SUV with the wrong wheels.

The Great Tang, or Da Tang as it’s called in China, is the first flagship D-segment SUV under its Dynasty series, the largest and most luxurious SUV from the lineup.

After opening preorders at the Beijing Auto Show in April, BYD said the high-end SUV secured over 30,000 preorders in 24 hours. Two weeks later, preorders reached over 100,000.

By the time it went on sale in mid-June, over 150,000 buyers had preordered the Great Tang, marking a new record for a single BYD model.

While it has been the top-selling pure electric large SUV for two months, with more than 10,000 sold since it hit the market, BYD issued an apology on November 11 for fitting the wrong front and rear wheel rims on some Great Tang models.

The apology comes after several owners complained that the wheels were improperly installed on their vehicles.

BYD said the issue stemmed from a coding error by the wheel mesh assembly supplier, which led to incorrect assembly on some Great Tang vehicles.

The company said it “immediately” launched an investigation and corrected the issue so newly built vehicles will not be affected. In addition, the supplier introduced a new visual error-prevention mechanism to limit issues like this going forward.

BYD said the issue does not affect the vehicle’s handling or pose a safety risk during driving.

The company said owners can take affected vehicles to their local service center for a free inspection and professional wheel rim replacement. It’s also offering a replacement vehicle or compensation for transportation expenses.

Effective immediately, BYD said, the warranty on affected Great Tang vehicles will also be restarted.

The Great Tang is on sale in China in pure electric (EV) and plug-in hybrid (PHEV) powertrains. The cheapest Premium EV variant starts at 239,900 yuan ($35,500).

All versions use BYD’s second-generation Blade Batteries. While the base Premium model uses a 105.79 kWh battery, the Flagship EV trim uses a larger 130.15 kWh battery that delivers up to 590 miles (950 km) of CLTC range. It starts at 269,900 yuan ($40,000).

The Great Tang is huge, measuring 5,263 mm long, 1,999 mm wide, and 1,800 mm high, with a wheelbase of 3,130 mm.

In comparison, that’s bigger than the Hyundai IONIQ 9 and Kia EV9. It’s closer in size to the Li Auto L9.

Based on a 1,000V architecture with BYD’s Flash Charging tech, the electric SUV can charge from 10% to 70% in five minutes. For a full recharge, or to go from 10% to 97%, it takes just nine minutes.

After launching in China, BYD executive vice president Stella Li said the flagship SUV will go on sale in Europe and other overseas markets by the end of 2026 or early 2027.

#4811. Solo Bitcoin Miner Scores Again, Hitting $191,000 Jackpot Against the Odds

Solo Bitcoin Miner Scores Again, Hitting $191,000 Jackpot Against the Odds

A lone Bitcoin miner has once again defied steep odds, single-handedly mining a block and pocketing a reward worth roughly $191,000, a windfall that normally flows to the industrial-scale operations dominating the network.

Blockchain data shows the miner solved block 977057 on Nov. 22, earning 3.12871270 BTC, made up of the 3.125 BTC base subsidy plus about 0.0037 BTC in transaction fees.

The block was found through Solo CKPool, a service that lets independent miners point their machines at the network and keep the entire reward if they get lucky, rather than pooling hash power and sharing payouts.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Solo miners command a tiny sliver of Bitcoin’s total computing power, and finding a block means being the first to guess a winning number against the combined might of massive mining farms running warehouses full of specialized machines.

The odds for a small setup are often likened to winning a lottery, which is why each solo success tends to draw attention.

And yet, it’s far from an isolated event, and solo Bitcoin miners have hit jackpots like this with increasing regularity throughout 2026. Scores worth around $300,000, $225,000, $210,000 and $200,000, as the price of Bitcoin fluctuates, have all landed with individual miners over the past year, some using only modest or rented hardware.

The frequency underscores that while the probability for any single miner is minuscule, the sheer number of people trying means someone occasionally beats the house.

The timing is notable against the backdrop of a strained mining industry. Bitcoin miners have been selling their holdings and pivoting infrastructure toward the AI boom, squeezed by thin margins, a lower post-halving subsidy and a Bitcoin price that has slumped well off its highs.

For large operators, those pressures have made mining a tougher business; for the solo miner who just won, it’s a rare, outsized payday.

What a solo miner does with the reward is up to them, and the win doesn’t change the economics for everyone else still grinding away. But these occasional long-shot victories remain a point of fascination in Bitcoin, a reminder that the network’s lottery, however improbable, still pays out to the little guy now and then.

At current prices, with Bitcoin trading around $62,000, the 3.13 BTC reward works out to roughly $191,000, though the dollar value shifts with the market.

#4807. 45 AD-Approved International Interior Photographers to Know for Your Next Project Abroad

They jet around the globe, camera in hand, capturing expansive views from clifftop villas and intimate reading nooks in private homes. The international interior photographers on these pages don’t just record living spaces; they tell stories and create moods, turning their eyes to details such as the light streaming through a red curtain or a marble statue waiting to welcome guests into a villa’s entryway. Each image is infused with a sense of place and the spirit of the people who pass through it. Read on to discover top talents working in Europe, Asia, Oceania, North Africa, South America, and Canada.

Born in South Korea, raised in Chicago, and currently living and working in Paris, Matthew Avignone brings a truly international perspective to his photography. His images often center around the collected objects that make a space personal and give it life.

A bathroom in Cap Martin along the French Riviera, designed by Studio Humbert & Poyet.

With Harry Nuriev, Festen, and Bernard Dubois among his collaborators, Ludovic Balay is no stranger to interiors with a point of view. The Paris photographer’s roster also includes Halleroed, adding another distinctive design voice to the picture.

The drawing room of a Notting Hill home designed by Olivia Williams.

Henry Bourne’s photographs open a window into creative people’s private worlds. The UK photographer’s interiors and portrait work includes subjects such as John Pawson, Faye Toogood, Luca Guadagnino, and Rose Uniacke.

A London townhouse designed by AD100 designer Leonora Hamill

Helen Cathcart brings a painterly, atmospheric quality to her work, which has resonated with some of the world’s hottest brands, designers, and publications. Cathcart’s photos of interiors, in particular, proffer serenity and calm through their tonality.

The Row, Lemaire, and a host of AD interiors: François Coquerel’s portfolio is in very good company. The Paris photographer moves between fashion and design, with Massimo Adario and Oitoemponto among his architectural collaborators.

Designer Luca Cipelletti delivers architectural precision and material opulence to an art collector’s Porta Nuova apartment.

Partners in life and work, Michael Depasquale and Martina Maffini count industry names like Dimore Studio, Nilufar Gallery, and JW Anderson among their clients. The duo splits their time between Paris and Milan and injects dynamism into each shoot that becomes evident in the final, glossy-ready results.

A villa in New Zealand by Patterson Associates.

Simon Devitt doesn’t stop at photographing architecture: fine art photo books, exhibitions, and workshops also fill his calendar. Based in Wellington, he has worked with Fearon Hay, Shigeru Ban, and Olson Kundig, documenting buildings across New Zealand and beyond.

Joseph Dirand designs rooms; his brother Adrien photographs them, including the architect’s Paris apartment for AD. Also based in Paris, Adrien contributed photography to the book Joseph Dirand Interior. A creative family, to say the least.

Inside the Paris headquarters of ACNE studios

Having started his career in fashion, Dol Maillot is as beloved for his street style shots and travel photography as he is for his interiors. Born in the South of France, the globetrotting artist now lives in Paris.

A Kelly Behun apartment at London’s Whiteley hotel is just one reason to linger over Veerle Evens’s portfolio. The London photographer moves between interiors, food, and travel, with HBA Residential, Owl Design, and Christian Bense among her collaborators.

A color-drenched home in the Canary Islands.

Some interiors tell you as much about their owners as a portrait might. Miguel Flores-Vianna seeks out those personal worlds, bringing his curiosity not just to interiors but also to his books, including Haute Bohemians and Haute Bohemians Greece. Armchair travelers, settle in.

Inside the Paris apartment of Yves Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé, as seen in AD France.

Among international interior photographers, Jérôme Galland ups the ante with his photos through a combination of striking contrasts, dramatic angles, and an appreciation of scale. French châteaus, Italian palazzos, and Greek villas all appear at their most grand through his lens.

Venetian interiors and Moroccan retreats make tempting subjects: Kasia Gatkowska’s portfolio travels well. The Amsterdam-based photographer has worked with Vincenzo De Cotiis, Vincent Van Duysen, Axel Vervoordt, and Massimo Adario, among others.

The home of the ABC Concept founders in Karuizawa, Japan.

Kenzo Takada’s Paris home is just one stop in Mitchell Geng’s portfolio. The Tokyo photographer also captured a colorful Hong Kong residence for Vogue Hong Kong and includes architecture, interiors, and hospitality in his photographic itinerary.

Film buffs may recognize Luca Guadagnino, the Italian director, producer, and screenwriter behind films such as Call Me By Your Name; design lovers will know his interiors too. Milan photographer Giulio Ghirardi has worked with the multihyphenate, shooting his interior design projects, and has also turned his lens to the work of Pierre Yovanovitch, Kelly Wearstler, Rodolphe Parente, and Augusta Hoffman.

A kitchen by AKB Design in Salaberry-de-Valleyfield, Quebec

Whether she is shooting portraits, food, travel, or interiors, Sylvie Li’s commercial and editorial images build a narrative. Just look at how she commemorates the marriage of old and new in this renovated Montreal apartment which appeared in AD.

The reception room of the barn on designer Jean-Louis Denoit’s watercress farm in France.

If Laura Gonzalez, Hugo Toro, and Pierre Yovanovitch feature on your list of design heroes, Stephan Julliard’s portfolio warrants a visit. The Paris photographer has worked with all three, as well as Jean-Louis Deniot, Kelly Wearstler, and Greg Natale.

Designer Meg Sharpe turns a traditional warren of rooms into an airy idyll where livability and flair are winsome bedfellows

Having shot for Dimorestudio, MacGuffin, Icon, and—hello, AD—Kyle Knodell has a knack for producing interior photos filled with life. At AD, we’ve tapped him to photograph Samantha Angelo and Peter Ostrega in their LES home.

The Paris apartment belonging to Gregoire Marot and Thomas Dupré de St Maur, designed with help of their friend the designer Eric Allart

Spain and Uruguay make appealing bookends for Ricardo Labougle’s practice. In between, his lens takes in interiors, gardens, and portraits, with the work of Jacques Grange, Vincent Van Duysen, Studio KO, and Michael S. Smith among its subjects.

A Stockholm apartment designed by Studio Escapist

From OMA’s architecture to Max Lamb’s furniture, Erik Lefvander moves easily between subjects, thrilling design enthusiasts. The Stockholm photographer’s collaborators also include Halleroed, Sabine Marcelis, and Sophie Dries.

“Uplifting” is the best way to describe Kensington Leverne’s photography, thanks to the softness and warmth that her images exude. Whether the interiors she shoots are palatial or cozy, Leverne is able to evoke a sense of intimacy within each space.

A bedroom in Stockholm’s Ett Hem boutique hotel.

Magnus Mårding takes a natural approach, whether the subject is a person, a car, or an interior. Based in Stockholm, he has worked with Ilse Crawford, Michael S. Smith, and Olson Kundig. Different subjects, one curious eye.

A New York townhouse designed by Elizabeth Roberts Architects, Grace Fuller Marroquin, and Leonora Hamill

Mexico City provides a fitting home base for Fernando Marroquin’s design-minded lens. His subject matter ranges from interiors to hospitality to retail, and he has worked with Héctor Esrawe, Manuel Cervantes, Ezequiel Farca, and Luis Laplace.

Gabriel Chipperfield and Laura de Gunzburg transformed their 19th-century London flat, blending contemporary softness with crisp minimalism.

Multiple shoots for AD and Vogue that show the English country manors and London town houses of the UK’s top celebrities and creatives fill Kate Martin’s portfolio. She adapts her eye for fashion editorial to interiors and portraits with ease.

Nicolas Matheus’s photos have graced the covers of countless international design magazines, depicting chic French apartments, sunny Spanish casas, coastal Cape Town villas, and more. His work in and around the Mediterranean is particularly gorgeous.

Still-life arrangements of smaller objects within larger spaces populate Alice Mesguich’s Instagram feed, showcasing her penchant for capturing styled vignettes as well as corners and niches she finds interesting.

Fans of Ashley Montgomery, Sam Sacks, and Ali Budd may already have Lauren Miller’s images on their inspiration boards. The Toronto photographer captures interiors and still lifes, with work appearing in AD and House & Garden.

Balmore House in North London, designed by Studio 163

Architecture buffs will find familiar names in Peter Molloy’s orbit: O’Donnell + Tuomey, Grafton Architects, and Carmody Groarke, for starters. The London photographer has also worked with McLaren.Excell, keeping the built environment firmly in the frame.

In AD100 designer Vincenzo De Cotiis’s 15th-century Italian palazzo, a breccia baixa marble was chosen for its resemblance to the palazzo’s original ceiling, shown in this bath along with a circa 1895 Carlo Bugatti chair.

Textured surfaces—plasterwork, fabric, timber, you name it—are the first thing noticed in any interior shot by Martin Morrell, making him a favorite of designers like Vincenzo de Cotiis and Studio Peregalli. His full list of both editorial and advertising clients reads as a who’s who of brand and media.

A dining area in the famed Casa de Canoas, a Brazilian modernist landmark designed by Oscar Niemeyer. Parente shot a campaign for Zara Home here.

Fran Parente travels his São Paulo–New York circuit with some excellent design company. Studio Arthur Casas, Studio MK27, Studio Mellone, and Isay Weinfeld figure among his collaborators, while hospitality, travel, and food broaden his view beyond architecture.

The pool area at the Desert Rock Resort in Saudi Arabia.

BIG. Kengo Kuma. Heatherwick Studio. Ema Peter’s collaborator list could double as an architecture lover’s hot list. Working between Vancouver and Los Angeles, she doesn’t just specialize in architecture; she also photographs residential interiors, including projects involving Studio Paolo Ferrari.

Proctor photographed the Brown Drawing Room at Blickling Hall in Norfolk as the house was being ‘put to sleep,’ a custom that involves draping the furniture when the visitor season ends, to allow the collections in the house to have some respite from the elements.

His intimate images of artists’ spaces and decorative arts workshops suggest Oskar Proctor enjoys looking behind the scenes. Working across London, New York, and Los Angeles, he has shot the work of Soane Britain, Vanderhurd, and Jermaine Gallacher, among other design-world names.

A penthouse at One Park Drive, the Herzon & de Meuron building at London’s Canary Wharf.

From the Aman New York to the Janu Tokyo, Robert Rieger’s portfolio makes a compelling case for checking in. The Berlin photographer also counts David Thulstrup, Vincent Van Duysen, and Herzog & de Meuron among his collaborators.

Designer Jacques Garcia’s 17th-century apartment in Paris.

Matthieu Salvaing’s book, Interior Voyages, lives up to its name, taking readers through homes as individual as their inhabitants. The Paris photographer’s travels encompass tropical retreats and historic residences, inviting readers to consider how different home can look around the world.

The bedroom of a New York townhouse designed by Greg Natale.

If your design tastes travel from Flack Studio to Kelly Wearstler, Anson Smart’s portfolio connects the dots. The Sydney photographer also captures interiors by Arent & Pyke and Greg Natale, alongside hospitality and lifestyle assignments.

An apartment in Naples, Italy, designed by FADD Architects.

A London apartment designed by Douglas Mackie.

If your design references lean British, put Astrid Templier on your radar. The London photographer’s collaborators include Guy Goodfellow, Douglas Mackie, Elicyon, and Rosanna Bossom—a roster that should resonate with fans of the English interior.

A private holiday home in Tuscany, Italy, designed by Vincenzo De Cotiis.

A room can tell a story, and documentary photographer Ambroise Tézenas has an ear for the details. Based in Paris, he has photographed interiors by Luis Laplace, Jacques Garcia, and Vincenzo De Cotiis, including commissions for AD France.

The kitchen in Nate Berkus and Jeremiah Brent’s home in Portugal.

Trevor Tondro’s command of color and mood brings a lively quality to his interiors photography. With early experience assisting both Philip-Lorca diCorcia and Martyn Thompson, he maximizes both artificial and natural light, always finding more than one way into a room.

Historic Castle Howard in North Yorkshire, London

#4806. Bitcoin rebounds 17% from $55K lows as Nvidia rally sparks $500M crypto liquidations

Bitcoin price upside hit $64,500 as risk assets surged on Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s reinstatement, while crypto liquidations topped $500 million four hours.

Bitcoin gained as much as 5.4% on Monday as cryptocurrencies and stocks rallied following news that Nvidia had reinstated its founder, Jensen Huang, as CEO.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD hitting local highs of $64,505 on Bitstamp, up more than 17% from a 26-month low seen on Saturday.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

US stocks opened markedly higher, led by Nvidia, which gained as much as 35% to add $1 trillion to its market cap.

Nvidia stock one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The company’s board confirmed Huang’s return shortly before the start of the US trading session, saying the decision was “unanimous.”

“The Board believes Jensen is the right person to lead NVIDIA, and that his reinstatement is in the best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers,” board chair Harvey Jones said in a press release.

The announcement followed comments on Friday by US President Donald Trump, who told reporters that he “would love to see” Huang return. In posts on Truth Social, Trump described the board’s move as an “absolutely great decision.”

“The Country needs Jensen’s leadership at NVIDIA in order to ensure the Success and Prosperity of not only the Company, but the American Economy,” he added.

The S&P 500 and tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index were up 4.8% and 6.8%, respectively, at the time of writing.

A notable exception was Anthropic, whose shares fell 1.7% on the day despite the broader relief that Huang’s return had brought to the AI trade.

Anthropic stock 15-minute chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

BTC price gains 12% from $55K: Three things to know in Bitcoin this week

The rally in stocks caught crypto short sellers off guard. Data from CoinGlass showed more than $500 million in total liquidations in the four hours leading up to 4:00 p.m. UTC.

Crypto liquidations vs. BTC/USD chart (screenshot). Source: CoinGlass

In an analysis posted on X, crypto trader and analyst Michaël Van de Poppe highlighted two trend lines as the next BTC price targets to reclaim: the 21-day and 200-day simple moving average (SMAs), at $69,014 and $72,240, respectively.

“The 200-Day MA would be a 30% rally from the lows and is a very, very likely relief rally resistance,” he forecast.

Cointelegraph previously reported that the two trend lines had formed a “death cross” for the first time since Q3 2023, when a similar pattern preceded macro lows of around $25,000.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 21, 200 SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#4802. Cuomo says rivals urged companies to opt out of OKXICE tokenized stocks

Former New York Gov. Andrew Cuomo said competitors encouraged companies to opt out of OKXICE’s rollout, while some issuers simply wanted more time.

Former New York Gov. Andrew Cuomo accused competing trading platforms of encouraging companies to reject OKXICE’s tokenized stock plans, saying the rivals wanted more time to launch their own services.

OKXICE is a joint venture between crypto exchange OKX and Intercontinental Exchange (ICE), the US financial markets operator that owns the New York Stock Exchange. Its platform launched Monday, giving eligible US investors access to around-the-clock trading in tokenized stocks.

Speaking during a Monday press briefing, Cuomo, who co-chairs OKXICE, said competitors had urged companies to opt out of the rollout, which has faced objections from 67 companies.

“Remember, also, we had competitors urging companies to opt out,” he said. “Why? Because they want companies to wait for them to have a system.”

The comments underscore the growing competition between traditional exchanges and crypto companies over tokenized stocks, which use tokens on a blockchain to represent ownership of or rights to an asset, such as a stock, allowing them to be traded around the clock.

OKXICE announced an initial rollout of six securities, including Coinbase, Circle, Robinhood, Strategy, BitGo and Securitize. Another 107 assets have been confirmed for a phased rollout, according to the company.

Related: Public companies object to OKX-ICE’s tokenized stock plan

While Cuomo alleged that competitors encouraged companies to opt out, he said some issuers simply needed more time to understand the platform.

Under the US Securities and Exchange Commission’s (SEC) temporary Innovation Exemption, issuers can object to having their shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party during a 30-day notice period.

Cuomo said the deadline gave some companies too little time to understand the technology and discuss the decision with their lawyers and boards. He added that OKXICE had received calls from companies planning to opt out for now but interested in learning more about the platform before making a final decision.

“The easiest decision was, no, but many were really saying, I need time, I want to talk about it,” he said.

JPMorgan Chase, McDonald’s, SpaceX and Coca-Cola were among the 67 companies that objected to having their shares tokenized on the platform.

Cuomo did not name the competitors he said were encouraging the objections or provide evidence for the claim during the briefing. Other companies developing tokenized stock trading services include Nasdaq, Kraken, Robinhood, Coinbase and Ondo Finance.

#4800. Base makes quantum security a 2027 priority as regulators warn of risks

Base creator Jesse Pollak says the upcoming Everest upgrade will help prepare wallets for future quantum threats, although further changes will be needed.

Base, an Ethereum layer-2 blockchain developed by Coinbase, is making quantum security a priority for 2027 as it prepares to protect its network against future threats from quantum computers.

Base creator Jesse Pollak told Cointelegraph in an exclusive interview that the upcoming Everest upgrade will introduce a new type of transaction designed to lay the groundwork for quantum-secure wallets.

The network is the second-largest blockchain by decentralized finance (DeFi) total value locked (TVL), with approximately $5.1 billion in assets deposited across its protocols, according to DefiLlama.

The upgrade will not make wallets quantum-resistant right away, but Pollak said it is an early step toward introducing further protections in the future. “That doesn’t mean we’ll be able to solve all of it in 2027, but [it’s] something the team’s going to be working on,” Pollak said.

The plans come as global regulators warn that quantum computers could eventually break the security systems used across financial markets, including blockchain networks.

Quantum computers process information differently from regular computers and could one day allow hackers to access crypto wallets and steal funds. However, many experts have concluded that today’s quantum computers aren’t powerful enough to do this.

One of Base’s first steps will come through Everest, its upcoming fifth network upgrade. Pollak said the upgrade introduces a new transaction type called Base Transactions, which he expects to support future improvements to wallet security.

“That network upgrade doesn’t specifically solve quantum security for these wallets, but it does put in place the framework,” Pollak said.

The current technical specifications for Base Transactions, outlined in EIP-8430, still rely on existing cryptography rather than quantum-resistant signatures. However, the proposed transaction format separates how a transaction is signed from how its signature is verified, allowing the network to introduce new security methods in the future without requiring users to change their wallet addresses.

Base has not set a timeline for introducing quantum-resistant protections, beyond making the work a priority for 2027.

Related: Ethereum dev targets 2028 for quantum resistance, a year early

On Thursday, the International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO) warned in a report that sufficiently powerful quantum computers could threaten the security of financial systems.

The international regulator said blockchain networks could be especially difficult to protect because making security changes would require cooperation from many participants. It also warned that these upgrades could create new security problems, raise costs and disrupt transactions.

Meanwhile, advances in artificial intelligence (AI) could make cyberattacks harder to detect and analyze, the report notes.

Governments are also preparing for the threat, with the US National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) introducing its first three security standards designed to protect against quantum attacks in 2024, and releasing further guidance in October 2026. The agency has set 2035 as the primary target for completing the transition to quantum-resistant cryptography across US federal systems.

Beyond quantum security, Pollak outlined four priorities for Base in 2027: expanding trading, payments and lending, while preparing for a future in which AI agents carry out more financial transactions.

Pollak pointed to growing demand for tokenized stocks, saying weekly trading volume on Base had reached $1 billion. He said much of the interest came from investors outside the US seeking easier access to American stocks.

AI agents are also expected to play a bigger role in the network. “Agentic payments was a big trend in 2026. I think that agentic finance is going to be one of the biggest trends of 2027,” he said.

Base’s latest network upgrade, Denim, was rolled out on Nov. 5 and reduced block times from two seconds to 200 milliseconds, allowing the network to produce blocks 10 times faster.

The upgrade was followed by transaction delays on Base. Pollak said Base reviewed the incidents, made fixes and introduced additional testing to help prevent similar problems.

Looking ahead, Pollak said he wants future upgrades to happen without users noticing. “Our goal in 2027 is to make it so that upgrades are non-event. So no one’s even talking about them,” he said.

#4799. Why Nvidia Needed Jensen Huang Back—And What His Return Really Changes

Why Nvidia Needed Jensen Huang Back—And What His Return Really Changes

Jensen Huang did not unveil a breakthrough chip on Monday. He simply got his old job back—and Nvidia shares surged more than 30%, while the Nasdaq jumped nearly 7% in midday trading.

The reaction revealed something more consequential than Wall Street’s fondness for a charismatic CEO: investors had spent 11 days confronting how much of the AI boom runs through one person.

Huang, who resigned November 12 amid revelations about extramarital affairs and an unproven Chinese espionage allegation involving a former customer executive, has returned as CEO, president, and director.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Nvidia with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Nvidia’s board says his reinstatement poses no national security risk, based partly on input from senior U.S. officials. Its independent investigation, however, is not over.

The company did not stop making chips. CFO Colette Kress became interim CEO, Harvey Jones took over as Nvidia’s first board chairman, and executives insisted the technology roadmap and customer commitments remained intact.

Kress presided over earnings that showed roughly $116 billion in quarterly sales and projected about $130 billion for the following quarter—staggering numbers that still failed to calm the market.

Inside Nvidia, the transition looked considerably less routine. During the first companywide meeting without Huang, staff had submitted about 700 questions, many concerning compensation and board transparency.

Kress promised pay increases and extra stock awards in December, months earlier than planned. Executives took turns assuring workers that the company could serve customers without Huang; some employees nevertheless discussed leaving if his absence became permanent.

The pushback extended outside headquarters. An online petition gathered roughly 200,000 names, though signers’ claimed employee or shareholder affiliations were not independently verified. Altimeter Capital’s Brad Gerstner and Atreides Management’s Gavin Baker, whose firms had disclosed about $2.1 billion in Nvidia shares, urged a deliberate succession process that kept Huang’s return possible. It was an unusual message to directors searching for a replacement: don't rush to replace the founder.

This letter was sent to Nvidia's board today.

Absolutely share @altcap belief that this letter outlines minimum acceptable standards. pic.twitter.com/ST2f5TMAa1

— Gavin Baker (@GavinSBaker) November 14, 2026

In other words, Nvidia couldn’t survive the scandal—not the one about Huang cheating, but the one about Huang leaving.

Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993 and turned graphics processors into the hardware behind modern AI. But his power also lies in the relationships surrounding those chips: cloud giants buying entire data centers of GPUs, developers locked into Nvidia’s CUDA software, suppliers building its next systems and policymakers deciding which markets it can serve.

Reuters reports that he has overseen roughly 60 direct reports and personally shaped major deals and product decisions.

He has also become a financier and ambassador for the AI ecosystem. Nvidia has invested in companies including CoreWeave and Nebius, which buy computing hardware to rent AI capacity to others.

That web of investment, sales, and financing is precisely why his disappearance unnerved markets far beyond Nvidia. After his outsting, Chinese customers delayed or canceled orders while leadership was unsettled; the whole Nasdaq index and the wider S&P500 crashed and odds for a bubble pop spiked to more than 70% on prediction markets.

Then, on Monday, minutes after his return, CoreWeave and Nebius shares each jumped more than 20% alongside Nvidia which had its best day in history—Huang also probably had his best day ever, too.

To have a grasp of Huang’s influence, one just needs to follow the money.

Elon Musk backed the reinstatement campaign; Y Combinator chief Garry Tan and Epic Games’ Tim Sweeney were among other prominent supporters. Bill Ackman celebrated Monday with “sanity prevails.” Trump pressed the case personally, later saying his intervention was “very important” and that the administration assured directors there was no national security obstacle to Huang’s return.

That backing helps explain the whiplash: On November 17, Jones said the board would not reinstate Huang, citing conduct inconsistent with Nvidia’s standards. Six days later, the same directors voted unanimously to bring him back. They say meetings satisfied them about his candor and leadership, but have not explained exactly what changed. Supporters see recognition of an indispensable founder; critics see a board yielding to powerful outsiders.

Bullish analysts argue that restoring Huang reduces execution and talent-retention risk. Dan Ives has framed Nvidia as a $300 stock with Huang versus $225 without him, while Melius Research upgraded the shares to “buy” with a $220 target.

Yet Monday’s roughly 30%-plus rebound followed a Friday close of $125.53; even near $165, Nvidia remains well below its October peak. Buying the comeback means paying much more for the same projected chip sales.

The other side is harder to price. Michael Burry criticized the reversal, and legal scrutiny of Huang, Nvidia’s export controls and its board continues. The company is establishing a board committee for national security and export-control oversight and recruiting a chief compliance officer.

None of that resolves the fundamental AI question: whether customers can earn enough on massive infrastructure spending to sustain Nvidia’s growth.

Huang’s return repairs a missing link between Nvidia, its customers, Wall Street, and Washington. The 11-day crisis showed how much confidence that link carries—and how vulnerable the AI trade becomes when it breaks.

What he has not yet won back is an unquestioned mandate to lead.

#4798. Kia just made history at Germany’s most prestigious auto awards

Kia became the first non-European brand to score a hat trick at the 2026 Golden Steering Wheel Awards, Germany’s most prestigious auto awards.

The Korean automaker won a triple crown at the 2026 Golden Steering Wheel Awards, with the EV2, K4 Sportswagon, and EV5 taking first in their class.

This is the first time a non-European brand has won three Golden Steering Wheel Awards in a single year in the prestigious award’s 50-year history.

The award is organized by AUTO BILD and BILD am SONNTAG with 90 new models nominated this year. Readers chose 27 finalists, three in each of the nine classes, which were then tested by a panel of celebrities and auto experts.

The judges tested the vehicles at the DEKRA-Lausitzring racetrack. Vehicles were judged on handling, comfort, ease of use, quality, and everyday practicality, as well as the sustainability and value they offer.

Kia won across three very different categories with the fully electric EV2 and EV5, alongside the gas-powered K4 Sportswagon.

The EV2, Kia’s entry-level electric SUV, won the award for the “Best Car Under €30,000.” Developed specifically for Europe, the compact EV is only 4.06 meters long, but offers up to 403 liters of cargo space.

It also offers a WLTP driving range of up to 453 km (281 miles), which is among the longest in its class.

Kia’s EV5 beat out the Audi Q3 and Cupra Formentor VZ5 in the compact SUV category. Launched in December, the EV5 is about the size of the Sportage.

It’s available with front- and all-wheel-drive powertrains and offers a range of over 550 km (341 miles). Depending on the configuration, the EV5 can tow up to 1,800 kg (about 4,000 lbs).

Lastly, the Kia K4 Sportswagon, launched in May, outperformed the new Volkswagen ID.3 Neo and Cupra Born in the compact class.

In Europe, Kia offers a full lineup of all-electric vehicles, from the entry-level EV2 to the EV3, EV4, EV5, EV6, three-row EV9, and PV5 van.

Sadly, Kia has no plans to sell the EV2 or EV5 in the US, but its popular EV3 (see our review) is now available. In 2024, the Kia EV3 won the Golden Steering Wheel award for “Best Car under €40,000.”

The 2027 Kia EV3 starts at $29,890, making it among the most affordable EVs in the US. It’s available in five trims and two battery packs.

The base Kia EV3 Light has a 58.3 kWh battery that provides up to 221 miles of range, while the Wind, Land, GT-Line, and GT use an 81.4 kWh battery for up to 321 miles of range.

#4797. Did Zendaya and Tom Holland Buy a $10.8 Million Home?

#4796. BTCS offloads $44M in ETH to reduce DeFi debt amid market downturn

BTCS sold more than half its Ether holdings to reduce leverage following the market sell-off, while BitMine added another 7,819 ETH to its treasury.

Nasdaq-listed blockchain company BTCS sold approximately 27,300 Ether on Friday to repay $43.8 million in loans on decentralized lending protocol Aave as cryptocurrency markets plunged.

According to a Monday filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission, BTCS sold the Ether (ETH) on Nov. 20 and used the proceeds to reduce its outstanding Aave debt to approximately $10.5 million.

The transaction reduced BTCS’s Aave borrowings by approximately 81%, as the company sought to limit liquidation risk amid heightened market volatility. Its remaining Aave positions had a loan-to-value ratio of approximately 32% following the repayment.

BTCS reported approximately $17.9 million in outstanding convertible notes, in addition to its remaining Aave borrowings, according to the company’s announcement. The company also said it temporarily exited liquidity pool positions in its Imperium DeFi business as part of its efforts to reduce exposure to further market volatility.

Following the sale, BTCS held approximately 24,245 ETH, with cash and digital assets valued at a combined $61 million as of Sunday. The 27,300 ETH sale represented approximately 53% of its combined pre-sale ETH holdings.

The move follows a similar reduction in Ether exposure by digital asset manager CoinShares, which disclosed last week that it sold its entire ETH treasury position and half its Solana (SOL) holdings in October to fund share buybacks.

Related: Bitmine edges toward 5% of ETH supply as treasuries report buys amid market slump

Meanwhile, Tom Lee’s BitMine Immersion Technologies continued accumulating Ether, purchasing another 7,819 ETH over the past week despite the market downturn.

According to a Monday announcement, BitMine now holds 6,085,564 ETH, representing 4.98% of the cryptocurrency’s total supply. The company said it has purchased Ether every week since adopting its treasury strategy in June 2025.

BitMine also reported $384 million in cash and marketable securities as of Sunday. The company has staked approximately 5.23 million ETH, representing 86% of its total Ether holdings.

BitMine accounts for roughly 75% of the Ether held in corporate and government treasuries tracked by CoinGecko, which listed 35 entities holding a combined 8.16 million ETH at the time of reporting.

The contrasting strategies come as Ether continues to struggle, trading at approximately $1,743 as of 6:35 p.m. UTC on Monday, down 37.7% over the past year and 24.8% over the past 30 days, according to CoinGecko data.

#4795. BYD shows what its new ‘Cloud Carriage’ intelligent body control system can really do

BYD’s new EV may be about the size of a Toyota Camry, but it’s playing in a whole different league when it comes to technology.

BYD launched the second-generation Seal 07 last week, calling it “the new choice for a class-leading luxury experience.”

It’s available in battery electric (EV) and plug-in hybrid (PHEV) powertrains, with prices starting at 139,900 yuan (about $21,000) for the plug-in hybrid and 149,900 yuan (around $22,500) for the fully electric version.

Both versions measure 5,080 mm long, 1,960 mm wide, and 1,495 mm high, with a 2,960 mm wheelbase, which is slightly longer than the Toyota Camry.

Don’t let the price fool you, however, the vehicle is packed with BYD’s latest breakthrough technology.

The new Seal 07 EV uses a 76.7 kWh second-generation BYD Blade LFP battery that provides a CLTC range of up to 481 miles (775 km).

Thanks to the second-gen Blade Batteries and BYD’s Flash Charging technology, the vehicle’s battery can charge from 10% to 70% in just five minutes. A full recharge, or going from 10% to 97%, takes just nine minutes.

The Seal 07 is also BYD’s first vehicle to debut with its new Disus-M Intelligent Magnetorheological Body Control System.

The setup features a front double-wishbone suspension and an enhanced rear five-link suspension. It works alongside BYD’s road-surface sensors that use LiDAR, plus its iTAC 2.0 Intelligent Torque Adaptation System.

Zhang Zhuo, the general manager of BYD’s Ocean sales division, offered a better look at how the system works in real life in the video shown above.

BYD said the system provides ultra-fast response times down to the millisecond. The advanced suspension system adjusts damping force across a wide range of 100 to 10,000 Nm.

It’s equipped on the all-electric Flagship Seal 07 variant, while lower-priced versions have the Disus-C intelligent body control system. The top-spec EV model with the Disus-M setup starts at 172,900 yuan ($26,000). Still, not too bad for what you get.

Inside, the new Seal 07 adopts BYD’s new interior design, including a 15.6″ floating center infotainment screen, a 10.25″ instrument cluster, and two rear passenger entertainment screens.

BYD added a few premium extras, including a built-in 6.8L smart refrigerator, zero gravity seats, and driver headrest audio.

#4794. North Korea Moves $247 Million of Binance Hack Loot Through Bitcoin Mixers

North Korea Moves $247 Million of Binance Hack Loot Through Bitcoin Mixers

North Korea’s hackers have now funneled at least $247 million from the record $6.4 billion Binance theft through Bitcoin “coinjoins,” a mixing technique designed to sever the trail between stolen funds and their origin, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said.

The figure, roughly a month after the Oct. 29 hack, reflects just the funds pushed into coinjoins, which pool many users’ Bitcoin into a single transaction so the coins can’t be traced.

Notably, almost all of it was coordinated by a single known individual: the operator of Kruw, the most widely used coinjoin coordinator for Wasabi Wallet users. That operator “refuses to block funds such as these,” Elliptic co-founder Tom Robinson told Decrypt in an email, which is why the firm expects the laundering to continue.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The mixing accelerated sharply in mid-November. On Nov. 17, the attacker emptied five of the nine largest Ethereum addresses tied to the theft, shifting about 488,000 ETH ($860 million) into fresh batches.

Those are being steadily swapped into Bitcoin through the Thorchain bridge and then sent into coinjoins, often within hours, the same playbook North Korea used after the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in early 2025.

The operation has spanned more than 27,000 wallets across at least 16 blockchains, roughly 55 services and some 90,000 transactions. Elliptic stressed it is clearly automated but found no evidence AI agents are directing it, pointing instead to conventional software, with fixed timing, set amounts and pauses that resemble human working patterns in Pyongyang.

Elliptic earlier found the hackers even minted their own worthless meme coins to shuffle funds through liquidity pools, one of several obfuscation tactics stacked together alongside cross-chain bridges, asset swaps and lending-vault hops.

The FBI has attributed the theft to the North Korean group TraderTraitor, and Binance has said it will cover all customer losses.

Still, little has actually been cashed out. About $2.85 billion has been frozen, and roughly $2.35 billion remains parked in theft-linked addresses, including the 18,270 BTC taken directly from Binance, which hasn’t moved since the night of the hack.

Elliptic expects the funds to be converted to Bitcoin, mixed and eventually cashed out through over-the-counter brokers over a period of months, the point at which they can still be intercepted.

With the Kruw operator declining to block the flows, though, the mixing shows little sign of slowing.
