#4793. Kalshi secures temporary win in New York gambling law dispute

The ruling allows Kalshi to continue operating as its legal battle with New York over sports-related event contracts moves toward a January hearing.

A New York state court judge on Monday denied the state attorney general’s request for a temporary restraining order against prediction market operator Kalshi, allowing the company to continue offering event contracts in New York as a legal battle over its sports-related markets proceeds.

In a statement to Cointelegraph, Elisabeth Diana, Kalshi’s head of communications, confirmed the ruling, saying the judge recognized the complex legal questions surrounding derivatives and federal preemption.

“The judge denied the State’s request for a TRO, recognizing that there are complex questions about derivatives and preemption. We remain committed to working with the State on a path forward that works for our 1.5 million customers and for the state of New York,” Diana said.

The denial of the temporary restraining order does not resolve the broader dispute over whether Kalshi’s federally regulated event contracts are subject to New York’s gambling laws.

The case will now proceed toward a preliminary injunction hearing set for early January, when the court will consider whether to restrict Kalshi’s event-contract operations in New York. Before then, both parties are scheduled to return to court on Nov. 30 to discuss potential restrictions on users aged 18 to 20, following concerns raised by the judge over the state’s minimum legal age of 21 for sports betting.

Related: CFTC halts some Kalshi Trump mention markets over manipulation concerns

New York sued Kalshi in July, alleging the prediction market operator was running an illegal, unlicensed gambling business by offering event contracts tied to sports, elections and other outcomes. The lawsuit seeks to halt the company’s operations in the state, recover alleged illegal gains and impose civil penalties.

The case followed an October 2025 cease-and-desist order from the New York State Gaming Commission, which prompted Kalshi to challenge the state’s authority in federal court. Kalshi argues that its status as a federally regulated derivatives exchange places its event contracts under the jurisdiction of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC).

In August, the CFTC warned that New York’s requested restraining order could prevent Kalshi from offering event contracts nationwide because the exchange is based in the state. The regulator also said New York was seeking more than $36 billion in damages.

In a separate federal court proceeding on Friday, US District Judge Lorna Schofield rejected the CFTC’s emergency request to block New York from pursuing its enforcement case against Kalshi, according to Reuters.

Kalshi isn’t the only prediction market operator facing legal action in New York. The state also sued Polymarket in September over allegedly offering illegal sports betting contracts, following similar actions against platforms operated by Coinbase and Gemini in April.

#4791. The Kids Are Outside: The Rise of the Adult Children’s ADU

When Diane and Steve Weaver of Castro Valley, California, shipped their son Sam off to college in 2013, they hoped he’d someday find his dream job and a nice home. And while Sam is thriving professionally as a sales rep with his own place, they never expected that his house would be in their backyard. But the nightmarish Bay Area real estate market made a 400-square-foot ADU (accessory dwelling unit) on his parents’ property, complete with a separate entrance and direct street access, seem like the best option for the 32-year-old. “It was becoming difficult for him to afford rent while also saving toward eventually buying a place of his own,” Diane tells AD. “He’s close by, but it really feels like he has his own home.”

While in-law suites and granny flats (a nickname for when ADUs house in-laws) have become popular among young, successful homeowners who want their elderly parents nearby, a growing number of Americans are now adding ADUs to their properties to house adult children threatening to move back in and reverse their status as empty nesters.

Diane and Steve Weaver on their property’s ADU

Blame the return of the flock on the home affordability crisis. These days a homebuyer needs an annual income between $110,000 and $113,000—well above the national average income of $64,220 per year—to comfortably afford a median-priced home in the US, which is currently priced at $434,900. While this has led some grown kids to move back into their childhood bedrooms, in cases where acreage allows, families like the Weavers are opting to be neighbors instead of housemates by setting up self-contained living spaces on the same lot as their single-family homes. In California, they’re certainly no outlier. The state has passed legislation specifically encouraging the construction of ADUs to address the housing shortage—which resulted in quadruple the number of ADU permits issued in the Golden State from 2017 to 2022.

Those in the ADU construction game say families like the Weavers make up an increasingly large slice of their business. Jeremy Nova, co-founder of the Colorado-based prefab ADU builder Studio Home, says ADUs for adult children are popular among his clients because the arrangement is mutually beneficial. The kids are afforded some independence and the ability to save money, “while for the parents, it can also be a long-term investment. The same space that houses an adult child today could eventually become a rental or a separate living space for a caregiver.” ADU builders on the opposite coast are seeing the same demand. Justin Olewack, president of Bolt Builders, says that ADUs for adult children are becoming a top request among his clientele in the DC, Virginia, and Maryland areas, where housing costs have risen 61 percent since 2016.

If it cuts costs and results in a sellable amenity down the line, perhaps moving back home isn’t the failure-to-launch scenario it was once seen as.

An ADU by Studio Home, built in the backyard of a Scottsdale, Arizona, family’s property.

Once a family’s adult child moves out of the property’s ADU, especially if it has a kitchen and bathroom, the structure can serve as a rental, providing passive income for retired parents. Tracy Noh, who hired John Brennan of BZBuild Co to build her daughter Eliza Bowen an ADU in Burbank, was thinking long-term with her compound planning. “An in-home apartment would require sharing the space of our 1,000-square-foot main home. And, as someone who works from home, the ADU seemed a better solution,” she says.

Noh and Eliza show off their Burbank ADU.

The living arrangement has also enabled Noh and her daughter to develop their own life paths. The structure gives her daughter a space in which she can become a young adult, and Noh the opportunity to step back from making the day-to-day household decisions. The former is learning how to manage her own household: planning meals, budgeting expenses, and making time for household chores.

“That change in dynamic also allows us both to remain much calmer in our interactions with each other,” Noh says. It’s not just because there’s a separate place for each of them to retreat to; Noh says she has “gained an enormous amount of respect for Eliza and her ability to manage her own home. It’s a fabulous opportunity to begin to build that adult-to-adult relationship with your child.”

The interior of Tracy Noh’s ADU by BZBuild Co

While modest ADUs serve single occupants just fine, more sizable ADUs allow a family’s adult children to bring their own kids in tow. Oren Amir, co-founder of SoCal builder GO Home Builders, points out that in his experience, the catalyst for aging parents to build an ADU for their adult children is almost never just the adult child themselves: It’s the grandchild they will be bringing with them. “The call comes a few months before or after a baby arrives,” he says. The grandparents don’t want to travel to visit their grandkids; they want them living nearby. Rent in Los Angeles is what stops people from saving, and early parenthood is when saving matters most, Amir says. An ADU in a grandparent’s backyard, therefore, lets the adult children stay in the neighborhood, raise the baby near help, and set some money aside for when the time comes to leave the nest for good. “Everyone gets what they need and nobody has to move in,” Amir says of the arrangement.

While the Weaver family ADU is Sam’s for now, it could grow up to be a granny flat someday. “It’s possible that Sam might live in the main house and we could move into the ADU as we age,” says Diane. “It gives us a lot of options for how the space can be used as our family’s needs change.”

#4790. Europe’s electric car sales jump 49% as global EV sales grow

Global EV sales climbed 8% in October, with Europe’s battery electric vehicle (BEV) sales jumping 49% and North America posting its first year-over-year increase in 2026. But that North American rebound comes with a big catch.

Drivers bought 2.09 million EVs worldwide last month, according to new data from Benchmark Mineral Intelligence. That’s down 3% from September, but it brings sales for the first 10 months of 2026 to 17.5 million – up 5% from the same period last year.

Benchmark’s figures include both BEVs and plug-in hybrids (PHEVs) in the passenger-car and light-duty vehicle markets.

Europe sold 510,000 EVs in October, up 36% year over year. Fully electric cars did most of the work: BEV sales rose 49%, while plug-in hybrids grew 7%.

That puts October’s growth ahead of Europe’s 31% increase for the year so far. The region has now sold 4.5 million EVs in 2026.

Sales fell 17% from September’s record, but September got a boost from the UK’s twice-yearly registration plate change. The year-over-year figures give a clearer view of how quickly the market is growing.

Germany’s EV share topped 45% of new car sales, its highest level since December 2022. That earlier peak came as buyers rushed to get cars before subsidy cuts took effect. Fully electric car sales in Germany rose almost 80% from a year earlier to more than 95,000.

UK EV sales increased 30%. That’s particularly notable because the Electric Car Grant was already available in October 2025, although fewer models qualified then. So this growth comes against a market that was already getting government support.

And in Portugal, EVs accounted for 55% of new car sales in October, following a sharp rise in their market share over the past few months.

North American EV sales rose 24% year over year to 130,000 in October, the region’s first annual increase of 2026. Sales were flat from September, while the year-to-date decline narrowed to 21%, from 24% a month earlier. The region has sold 1.3 million EVs so far this year.

US EV sales reached around 110,000 units in October.

But here’s the catch: October 2025 was the first month after the federal EV tax credit expired. The credit ended for vehicles acquired after September 30, 2025, and sales dropped sharply afterward.

October 2026 is therefore being compared with a month when sales plummeted. A positive annual number doesn’t mean US demand has returned to its pre-expiration level.

“October is the first month compared against a period without the US federal tax credit, and the US market has stabilized at a lower level rather than recovered,” said Charles Lester, Benchmark’s data manager.

The flat month-over-month figure helps tell that story: Sales have steadied, but the market is operating from a lower base.

China sold 1.14 million EVs in October, up 5% from September but down 12% year over year. That annual decline was smaller than September’s 18% drop.

Sales through October reached 9.1 million, down 11%, compared with a 13% year-to-date decline at the end of September.

Even with fewer EVs being sold, their share of China’s new car market reached a fresh high. That’s because the wider passenger car market is shrinking faster.

Chinese automakers are continuing to produce vehicles and look overseas for buyers. Passenger car exports of new energy vehicles – China’s category covering BEVs and PHEVs – reached nearly 600,000 in October. That’s another monthly record, up from 558,000 in September.

Those exports face more pressure ahead. The EU has agreed to cap imports of Chinese-built hybrids, and China will reduce its EV export VAT rebate from 13% to 9% starting April 1, 2027.

Outside China, Europe, and North America, EV sales rose 85% year over year to 300,000 in October. Those markets have bought 2.6 million EVs so far in 2026, up 93%.

Australia’s sales jumped more than 200% in October, and EVs have gained market share sharply this year. But that hasn’t translated into healthy dealer profits, according to Benchmark. More brands, many of them Chinese, are competing for buyers, spreading sales across more companies and squeezing dealer margins.

Brazil’s growth has also been huge, although Benchmark cautions that some of its drivers are temporary. Importers brought vehicles in ahead of phased tariff increases, a fuel crisis pushed more buyers toward EVs, and BYD increased production at its Brazilian factory.

Nothing like consumer economics to drive sales. Local story about consumer economics: Windturbines on land is not popular. However, the locals realised they could heat their home for $300/year (in Denmark(!)) with these wind turbines and then they were tolerated! Same with cars - people gravitate to cheaper alternatives despite the more frequent charging and initial higher prices.

As the tariff increases take full effect and fuel price pressure eases, Benchmark expects those boosts to fade. BYD’s local production, which will also supply Argentina and Mexico, is set to become the main remaining support for that growth.

#4789. US seeks to recover $1.1B in crypto stolen in North Korea-linked Binance hack

US prosecutors are seeking control of stolen USDC, while Elliptic says hackers have moved $247 million through Bitcoin mixers.

The US Department of Justice is seeking to recover more than $1.1 billion in crypto stolen from cryptocurrency exchange Binance in October by hackers linked to North Korea.

In a court filing Monday, prosecutors asked a federal court in Washington, DC, to permanently forfeit the funds, which are held in USDC – a stablecoin issued by Circle. The filing follows a court order obtained earlier this month allowing authorities to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion in assets linked to the hack. Since the attack, which occurred on Oct. 29, US authorities said they have helped to freeze, seize or return nearly $3 billion of the more than $6 billion taken from Binance.

“Following the Binance theft, the FBI and our partners moved quickly to trace and freeze stolen assets and share critical information with virtual asset service providers,” said in a statement Brett Leatherman, operations director of the Federal Bureau of Investigation’s (FBI) cyber branch.

Binance has also pursued legal action to recover the stolen assets. On Nov. 18, the exchange said it had secured a $6.5 billion worldwide freezing injunction from the Abu Dhabi Global Market Courts. The orders target unidentified attackers and allow legal notices to be delivered through blockchain transactions to wallets linked to the theft.

The filing comes as blockchain analytics firm Elliptic also reported an analysis updated Monday that hackers had moved at least $247 million in Bitcoin (BTC) through crypto mixers – tools that make stolen funds harder to track. The firm said another $2.22 billion remained in wallets linked to the theft, while $2.85 billion had been frozen or seized.

Elliptic noted that hackers had moved stolen funds through more than 27,000 wallets across at least 16 blockchains. However, most of the crypto had not yet been converted into cash, and the 18,270 BTC stolen directly from Binance had not moved since the attack.

US authorities have linked the attack to a North Korean hacking group called TraderTraitor. According to Binance, the hackers gained access to its systems after tricking an employee into downloading malicious software. Following the attack, Binance said it had committed more than $6.34 billion to cover the losses and reassured that customer funds were safe.

#4787. Why One Math Proof Has AI Experts Warning Humans Might Soon Lose Control

Why One Math Proof Has AI Experts Warning Humans Might Soon Lose Control

Meta Chief AI Officer Alexandr Wang warned Sunday that artificial superintelligence is approaching—and that humanity may be unable to control systems far smarter than itself.

In an essay on X titled “Superintelligence is Arriving,” Wang said the next year could determine how people live alongside machines capable of improving themselves. His catalyst was an extraordinary mathematics result involving Anthropic’s Claude.

“Superintelligence is arriving. How humanity acts over the next year will have great consequence on what future world we live in,” Wang wrote.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Wang called the proof of the Riemann Hypothesis a greater intellectual achievement than building the atomic bomb, which he described as a “homework problem” by comparison. The breakthrough, he wrote, came with help from an “alien mind that we don’t understand.”

The warning carries extra weight because Wang runs Meta Superintelligence Labs. Mark Zuckerberg brought him aboard in 2025 after Meta invested $14.3 billion for a 49% stake in Wang’s startup, Scale AI.

On Friday, Anthropic mathematician Levent Alpöge said he and Claude had proved the Riemann Hypothesis, a conjecture about prime numbers that had resisted mathematicians since 1859. The claim arrived a day after Anthropic’s massive IPO.

Alpöge released roughly 17 million lines of code in Lean, a system that checks mathematical proofs. On Saturday, the Lean and Mathlib communities certified that the code proves the correct statement; researchers are still working to explain its mathematics in conventional form.

For Wang, the result suggests raw reasoning power is no longer AI’s central limitation. He argued that the remaining hurdles involve specialist knowledge, experimentation, and the tools needed to apply intelligence in other fields.

That includes AI research itself. If machines can redesign more capable successors, Wang fears a cycle of recursive self-improvement could move faster than human monitoring or intervention.

“It is hard to imagine a world where humanity can maintain control over fully unconstrained recursive self-improvement,” he wrote. He also questioned human control over hypothetical systems “1000x smarter” than people.

Wang criticized the industry’s “lazy thinking” that superintelligence is inevitable and the only question is making it safe. “We should not be okay with a ‘handover’ of society to AI,” he wrote, advocating technology that strengthens human decision-making.

In a Monday follow-up, he said Meta had delayed its Muse launch for months over safety concerns and backed industry safeguards. Zuckerberg, he added, wants most computing power devoted to serving customers rather than unrestricted self-improvement.

Meta nevertheless has its own ambitions. Its Muse Wonder models, released in October, compete with systems from OpenAI and Anthropic, making Wang both a critic of the race’s direction and a leading participant.

Not everyone accepts his leap from mathematics to superintelligence. NYU professor Andrew Gordon Wilson argued that proving an existing conjecture is different from inventing new mathematical frameworks such as calculus or relativity.

“Nothing compares to proving the Riemann Hypothesis in terms of sheer intellectual might”. I disagree. The introduction of calculus, general relativity, and quantum mechanics, are more profound intellectual achievements. Creativity, taste, and intuition is the difference.

— Andrew Gordon Wilson (@andrewgwils) November 23, 2026

The Riemann result demonstrates a remarkable human-AI collaboration, but it does not prove that autonomous superintelligence exists.

Wang has urged the industry to change course while Meta continues developing the systems he fears could eventually exceed human oversight.

#4784. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Nvidia shares surged Monday after its board cleared Jensen Huang to return as CEO despite an ongoing intelligence probe. Meanwhile, OKXICE began offering 24/7 trading in tokenized US securities amid pushback from major companies, and a new analysis raised questions about foreign participation in US President Donald Trump’s second memecoin dinner.

Nvidia’s board unanimously voted to reinstate Jensen Huang as president and CEO after consultations with senior US government officials, concluding that his return posed no national security risk despite an ongoing investigation into an alleged foreign intelligence operation targeting him.

In a Monday announcement, Nvidia said its directors were satisfied with Huang’s candor and ability to lead following a series of discussions. The independent investigation will continue despite his reinstatement.

Nvidia shares surged 28.91% to $161.82 shortly after US markets opened on Monday, according to Yahoo Finance data.

Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress, who served as interim CEO, will return to her previous role, while Harvey Jones will remain chair of the board.

The chipmaker also said it had recently formed a National Security and Export Control Committee comprising independent directors to oversee compliance and security risks. Nvidia plans to appoint a chief compliance officer and said it would continue cooperating with government investigations.

OKXICE, a joint venture between cryptocurrency exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange (ICE), launched a venue Monday for round-the-clock trading of tokenized US securities, with 113 assets set to roll out in stages.

The lineup includes stocks, exchange-traded funds and other exchange-traded products, according to an announcement shared with Cointelegraph. OKXICE named Coinbase, Circle, Fidelity Investments and Robinhood among the issuers and asset managers taking part.

Before the launch, 67 companies, including Meta, Tesla and Netflix, sought to keep tokenized versions of their shares off the venue.

Each token represents a security entitlement backed one-to-one by an underlying share, OKXICE said. Holders are entitled to dividends and voting rights where applicable.

OKXICE said the venue operates under the US Securities and Exchange Commission’s Innovation Exemption. Access is limited to eligible US customers of OKX Inc., who must verify their identity, activate a self-custodial wallet and complete onboarding before trading through the OKX app.

More than half the wallets that qualified for Donald Trump’s memecoin dinner on Sunday received TRUMP tokens from crypto exchanges that bar US users, according to analysis from consumer advocacy group Public Citizen.

Trump is hosting his second dinner for his top memecoin holders at Trump National Golf Club in Sterling, Virginia, more than a year after an inaugural dinner in May 2025. The dinner offers up to 185 seats based on time-weighted TRUMP holdings.

Public Citizen said it traced 66 of the 130 wallets on the public leaderboard to exchanges including Binance (the global exchange), Bybit and BloFin — which are not permitted to serve US citizens. The list also included 15 of the 29 wallets that qualified for a VIP reception with the president.

“If those bans are enforced, many of the people vying for a seat at the president’s table don’t live in the United States,” said Public Citizen research director Zach Everson.

Trump’s memecoin events have drawn scrutiny over whether foreign buyers can essentially buy access to the president. Democratic lawmakers called on the White House to release the guest list for the first dinner in May 2025, while some joined a protest outside the golf club. They renewed the request again in October.

#4783. ‘The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping’: Inside the Psychedelic 1970s-Inspired Dystopian Sets

Francis Lawrence, director of The Hunger Games, has a tradition with author Suzanne Collins. Before bringing a new installment of her bestselling dystopian saga to the big screen, the two join forces to envision how the fictional world of Panem will take shape. “I go to New York each time, and we sit in a conference room at Scholastic,” says Lawrence, who has directed five of the franchise’s six films. “We go through the book page by page and beat out what we think the movie is going to look like.”

Lawrence’s latest adaptation, The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping (in theaters today), returns to Panem, a postapocalyptic nation where the ruling Capitol controls twelve districts through extreme systemic oppression—most notoriously the titular spectacle in which children are selected by lottery and forced to fight to the death in an arena. Set twenty-four years before the original The Hunger Games (2012) plotline, the prequel follows District 12’s sixteen-year-old Haymitch Abernathy (Joseph Zada)—the future mentor (played by Woody Harrelson) to Katniss Everdeen (Jennifer Lawrence) and Peeta Mellark (Josh Hutcherson)—as he is selected to fight in the Second Quarter Quell, a particularly sadistic rendition of the Games held every twenty-five years.

District 12’s female tribute Maysilee Donner (McKenna Grace) at the reaping.

Though the dystopia is set in a future North America, the previous prequel, The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes (2023), drew from the architecture of 1940s and ’50s postwar Berlin to depict evil President Snow’s rise to tyranny. Set forty years later, Sunrise explores Panem at a new stage of its evolution. Designed by returning production designer Uli Hanisch, the film follows Haymitch from his impoverished mining district, which barely evolves throughout the series’s sixty-five year timeline, to the opulent Capitol, whose extravagance is expressed through a 1970s-inflected visual lexicon.

“I realized the ’70s fall right smack in the middle between that movie and the originals, so we could lean into that,” says Lawrence. Hanisch and his team incorporated references to Andy Warhol, Studio 54, and the decade’s psychedelic color palette, appropriating its progressive spirit to convey the Capitol’s opulence and authoritarianism—and, particularly in the arena, using the aesthetic as a means of deception. “To turn the ’70s style from something progressive, liberal, young, and open-minded into something evil is a twist,” says Hanisch. “But that is the key to the entire film’s design.”

Haymitch and Maysilee as allies in the arena. The forest portion was filmed separately near Cologne, Germany.

The stylistic choice is also a poignant nod to Collins’s original inspiration for the book series. The 1970s marked a transformation in American television: as color TVs became ubiquitous in households across the country, sporting spectacles, variety shows, and beauty pageants flourished, establishing cultural touchstones that would help shape the contemporary reality programming Collins critiques and satirizes in The Hunger Games. “I was flipping through the channels one night between reality television programs and actual footage of the Iraq War, when the idea came to me,” Collins recalled in a 2018 interview with The New York Times. The juxtaposition inspired her to explore just-war theory for young people through an authoritarian dystopia in which mass entertainment is an instrument of political control.

From an abandoned rural steel mill to authoritarian monuments from postwar Berlin, the film draws on politically charged German sites to capture Panem at a pivotal stage in its political and architectural evolution.

Production Designer Uli Hanisch at the District 12 set. “District 12 is the most sentimental set to me because I’d known this site near my hometown my entire life,” Hanisch says.

To recreate the Appalachian-inspired, deteriorating industrial landscape of District 12 that was shot in Atlanta in the original series, the production utilized a sprawling abandoned steel mill in Duisburg, Germany, Hanisch’s hometown. “I wish we had it in the original movies, because it is an unbelievable space,” Lawrence says of the post-industrial, rural landscape, which also appeared in The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes, Hanisch’s first film in the franchise. “Everywhere you look is exactly how I have always imagined District 12.”

While District 12 was filmed near Atlanta in the original films, director Francis Lawrence’s fascination with German architecture brought production to Berlin for The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes. It was there that he began collaborating with Berlin-based production designer Hanisch, who proposed this steel mill nearby his hometown for the new location. Upon seeing photographs, Lawrence exclaimed, “Are you kidding me? ... Let’s go tomorrow,” and promptly flew out to see it for himself.

The production designer added modest huts at the edge of a nearby quarry to recreate the Seam, home to Haymitch, his girlfriend Lenore Dove (Whitney Peak), and fellow tribute Louella McCoy (Molly McCann), its humble dwellings contrasting the more prosperous merchant district inhabited by fellow tribute Maysilee Donner (Mckenna Grace).

“Unlike the Capitol, District 12 doesn't evolve stylistically throughout the films. Nobody is investing in it, and everything is gradually deteriorating,” Hanisch explains. The vivid purples and oranges of the Capitol’s ‘70s palette are notably absent, only appearing prominently in propaganda decorating the reaping square, including Hunger Games campaign materials and posters portraying Snow as Panem’s number-one Peacekeeper.

Druscilla in the Capitol before the tribute interviews, which is broadcast to all of Panem.

In the Capitol, however, the architecture takes on a distinctly ‘70s flair. Rooted in the Roman concept of panem et circenses, or “bread and circuses,” from which Panem takes its name, the architecture reinforces the government’s manipulation through spectacle. In the background is Berlin's International Congress Centrum, a colossal, aluminum-clad convention center that opened in 1979, which Lawrence describes as “Cold War-era Berlin showing off in a modern way.” As West Berlin’s most expensive postwar building, the futuristic complex was publicly subsidised to showcase economic prosperity and technological advancement—an evocative backdrop for the Capitol’s own display of authoritarianism.

“It’s a time of peace for the Capitol with no rebel attacks, so Snow's had time to build the city up in a way that is an extravagant show of power,” Lawrence explains. The chariot parade, which introduces the tributes before the Games, similarly unfolds against the historically-charged Berlin Olympiastadion, which was designed by Werner March for the 1936 Olympics under Nazi Germany to project state power and an illusion of timeless permanence.

The upstairs library in Plutarch’s home, which he inherited from the Heavensbee dynasty. Reflecting the Capitol’s classic style, it contrasts the Plutarch’s modern downstairs salon which features Warhol-inspired portraits, including one of director Lawrence.

The Capitol's grandeur is also exhibited inside the mansion of Plutarch Heavensbee (Jesse Plemons), a cameraman with historic ties to Panem’s ruling class. “This was one of my favorite sets because of the scope and scale” Lawrence says, noting how its excess reflects the dynasty's immense wealth. Filmed at Berlin's late-1960s modernist Congress Center, the circular residence required twenty-five vans of retro-modern furniture to fill it. The salon, decked in vibrant 1970s furnishings and Warhol-inspired art, showcases Plutarch's modern sensibilities, in stark contrast to the upstairs library inherited from his family. “We wanted to distinguish Plutarch from the dynasty he comes from,” Lawrence explains, reflecting on how the expansive volumes must be a facade of intellectual sophistication.

The set decoration department spent weeks hand-folding and assembling thousands of hollow cardboard books, interspersed with real volumes, including a collection of Edgar Allan Poe’s poetry, which Plutarch references to Haymitch. “We imagined Plutarch might actually be the first Heavensbee to read some of those books,” Hanisch says. “He even says the library is one of the few worthwhile things his ancestors accomplished.”

The library's custom-built curved bookshelves envelope the characters, symbolising the insularity of Capitol society. “Circles represent both inclusion and exclusion,” Hanisch says. “If you are in the circle, you're in. And if you're not, you're out.” As fans of the franchise will know, Plutarch (originally played by Philip Seymour Hoffman) later reveals himself as a double agent and leader of the underground rebellion against Snow’s regime.

The aspect ratio expands as Haymitch ascends into the eye-shaped arena, emphasizing the landscape’s immersive scale and beauty. While scouting locations, Lawrence drew inspiration from the dramatic landscapes in Terrence Malick’s A Hidden Life. “I had that in my head, only it was a little trippier. The Asturias were perfect,” he recalls. The kaleidoscopic design also inspired the official logo of the 50th Hunger Games, extending the arena’s psychedelic imagery into the Capitol’s propaganda.

The Quarter Quell arena, in particular, embodies the surreal, fantastical ethos of the 1970s that permeates the film, illustrating how the Capitol exploits beauty to disguise its underlying manipulation and brutality. “As I was reading with Suzanne, I said there was an Alice in Wonderland-like element to it, which made me think of the song ‘White Rabbit,’ which made me think of the late ’60s, early ’70s,” Lawrence recalls. “That kind of broke something open for me.”

Designed in the shape of an eye, the eight to ten mile arena serves as a metaphor for the Capitol's omnipresent surveillance. A golden Cornucopia forms its pupil; vibrant meadows its iris; and the surrounding mountain ranges its whites. In the book, Haymitch describes the especially lethal arena masquerading as an idyllic landscape as a “poisonous beauty of an arena.”

Lawrence and Haymitch in the arena. The physical flowers were concentrated in the foreground, while visual effects extended the colorful landscape into the distance

Seeking to capture as much natural landscape as possible, Hanisch and location manager Klaus große Darrelmann discovered a remote valley in Asturias, Spain, surrounded by the Cantabrian Mountains. Transforming the isolated valley into a psychedelic paradise proved an enormous undertaking.“The arena was our nemesis,” says Hanisch, recalling how the production had to bring extensive resources to the remote terrain. “You can't even imagine how far away it was or how hard it was to get to.” Crews spent weeks individually installing hundreds of thousands of artificial flowers throughout the meadows and forests.

Despite the arena’s dreamlike beauty, water sources, flowers, and vegetation conceal deadly poisons. “It’s the most colorful and playful dystopia you’ll ever see,” producer Nina Jacobson says. Perhaps more than anywhere else in Panem, the arena encapsulates how the Capitol weaponizes the electric zeitgeist of the ‘70s as an instrument of control. “To have something so overwhelmingly beautiful and seductive but poisonous and deadly is very cruel,” Hanisch says. “That contradiction captures the mentality of Panem at this point in its history.”

For Actors Dichen Lachman and Maximilian Osinski, a Temporary Move to London Turned Into a Colorful House Renovation

#4782. OpenAI Will Pay You Up to $8K to Preach the AI Gospel to Small Businesses

OpenAI Will Pay You Up to $8K to Preach the AI Gospel to Small Businesses

OpenAI wants people to spread the gospel of ChatGPT to their local businesses. And it is willing to pay them up to $8,000 a month to do it.

The company launched OpenWorks, a program that recruits AI-savvy individuals and small consulting teams to help neighborhood businesses figure out what to do with the technology. The idea is to move beyond telling a restaurant owner that AI will change everything and actually build something useful for the restaurant.

OpenAI will pay selected partners a monthly stipend of up to $8,000. In exchange, applicants must commit to four full days of free, in-person work with local businesses each month, plus one community workshop. It's not an $8,000 salary just for being good at prompts.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That said, this isn't a full-time job with an automatic $96,000 salary. The company advertises a maximum monthly stipend and asks partners to spend four full days each month providing free, in-person assistance, plus hosting one community workshop.

The application asks candidates to describe a real problem they've solved using AI, what changed afterward, and which businesses they'd help first. Knowing how to give ChatGPT clever instructions apparently isn't enough; experience in coding, automation, marketing or operations helps.

Small businesses pay nothing for the service. They pick a problem, work with a partner on a solution, and get three months of ChatGPT Business at no cost. OpenAI says the pilot covers independently owned U.S. businesses, but leaves out startups backed by venture capital, private-equity-owned companies, and software firms.

The company has some examples to sell the idea. In Fargo, North Dakota, the owners of Tiny Things, a gift shop selling miniatures, used ChatGPT with an OpenWorks partner to build an inventory dashboard. One owner estimated that commissioning a similar tool would have cost about $50,000.

In Charleston, a microgreens grower used the chatbot to find restaurants and write personalized pitches to chefs. He estimates his response rate went from 20% to 75% and says he landed 10 new restaurant customers in four months. Those are the businesses' own accounts, not independently audited results.

OpenWorks is starting in 20 communities, including New York, Chicago, Los Angeles, and Fargo. OpenAI is inviting applicants elsewhere, too, under the slogan "Help us reach every community in America."

The push comes as the company works to turn consumer enthusiasm into recurring business usage. As Decrypt reported in April, enterprise customers already accounted for more than 40% of OpenAI's revenue, according to the company.

Competition also provides an incentive. Anthropic overtook OpenAI in one measure of business AI adoption earlier this year, even as ChatGPT remained a major consumer destination.

Getting ChatGPT into more neighborhood businesses gives OpenAI another route to paying customers, though the company hasn't said what happens after the free three-month offer. It also follows an October effort with America's Small Business Development Centers to train advisers and reach at least 1,000 businesses through workshops.

OpenAI has not announced a general application deadline or a fixed stipend for everyone accepted. The form is open now, and its most important question is pretty straightforward: Can you actually show up four full days a month and help somebody run a business better?

#4780. In the Berkshires, an Intensely Yellow Kitchen Is Anything but Mellow in This Lakeside Cottage

“I personally would not want this yellow in my kitchen,” says Jess Cooney. It’s a cheeky comment about the beaming hue she herself chose for clients in the Berkshires—a color that defines their entire lakeside home, one she calls the diva of the whole project, requiring (no, demanding) decisions in every square foot to balance out its intensity.

Cooney’s clients didn’t blink at the bold idea. Unusually carefree for a couple with high-powered jobs, they gave the AD PRO Directory designer free license to create. The bungalow was purchased as a weekend retreat from life in New York, but even before paint swatches were discussed, the kitchen was a sticking point. “The kitchen was crazy—it was this tiny little makeshift kitchen,” says Cooney.

Cooney’s clients weren’t initially convinced by reclaimed brick flooring in the entryway, but Cooney’s team proved convincing—they believed the space needed a worn palette to counterbalance the intensely yellow kitchen. Much to their clients’ delight, it worked beautifully. A Croft House Morro Credenza with leather-front doors anchors the space, topped with a vintage lamp from Millerton Antiques Center and set beneath an unknown artwork from Brimfield Flea Market.

Cooney opted to stain wood-paneled walls in custom blue and brown stains from Rubio Monocoat, allowing rustic grains to shine through. Atop a Jaipur Hazan rug, an upholstered George Smith Emma Ottoman anchors the room and is accompanied by a set of armchairs and a velvet mordoré sofa from Lawson-Fenning. A 20th-century oil painting by Paul Berg hangs above the reworked fireplace, while in the corner, above a Charles Dudouyt side table, hangs a vintage painting sourced from The Little Store in Massachusetts.

“We just decided to kind of lean into it and go almost in a maximalist way,” says Cooney of the dining room’s focal point, a painting by K.G. Narendra Babu that inspired the home’s many colors. For the inside of a Four Hands Camila Sideboard, they commissioned artisan Joel Haynes to hand-paint a leafy pattern. A Hubbardton Forge Disq fixture hangs above a custom Bossi dining table surrounded with Tribute Modern Dining Chairs from Craft Associates.

The husband, an English lawyer, craved a small cabin in the woods, while the wife, an Italian who loves cooking, wanted a home large enough to host intimate gatherings. “This house was a happy compromise,” says the husband. But while the home spans three levels and around 4,500 square feet, the main floor measured just about 1,000. The kitchen, in a cramped corner, didn’t cut it

Cooney’s team reconfigured a few walls. They collapsed oversized closets in the entryway, shrinking the space to a slimmer mudroom while letting the entire layout exhale, expanding the footprint of the semi-open cooking area. The room is now the proper heart of the home, and its bright yellow cabinets aren’t shy about it.

Cabinets with glass windows were fabricated by Westek Architectural Woodworking and feature an array of white porcelain that punctuates and calms the intensely painted millwork; the pieces were all sourced from shops in the Berkshires, including Millerton Antiques, All that Jazz, and Montage Antiques.

“It’s the diva of the project,” says Cooney of the yellow Benjamin Moore paint used throughout the kitchen, a choice that influenced nearly all palettes throughout the home. Ceilings, walls, and upper cabinets were painted in Farrow & Ball’s New White.

The kitchen island is topped with a wooden counter and is paired with Amber Interiors Cade Counter Stools in walnut. A set of Matthew Fairbank Allegro Pendants hang above the island.

The saturated hue, Benjamin Moore’s Glen Ridge Gold, wasn’t a choice made on a whim. Like many colors peppering the home, it was inspired by a painting by Chennai-based artist K.G. Narendra Babu that now hangs in the couple’s dining room. The artwork was given to the couple by a family member who worked in India, and it features an array of folk and musical motifs.

“The whole point of the project is that it’s bright and energetic and deeply relaxing at the same time,” says Cooney. “That was the trust that they put in us—to believe that both could happen.”

The architecture itself lacked a definitive style. Light traces of Arts & Crafts style gave Cooney a point of view to reinvent existing character. A fireplace in the living room is now reimagined with glazed brick Clé tiles, their dappled surface cladding the surround with clean lines and a crafted look; a custom stain allowed wood-paneled walls to bear rustic grains but in modern blue-green tones.

To expand the kitchen’s footprint, Cooney reclaimed space from unnecessarily large closets in the entryway. Her team moved the wall to install smaller, but equally useful, cabinets that turn the space into a mudroom. The custom millwork, crafted by Westek Architectural Woodworking and painted in Farrow & Ball’s Blue Gray, complements a vintage rug and a built-in bench.

A custom walnut vanity injects warmth as an alternative to a colder stone. Cooney’s team used a boat sealer to coat and protect the walnut, giving the material a high-gloss finish. A fabric shade made with Eric Kuster’s Micha 002 textile occupies the space that might traditionally feature a mirror. “You can kind of break a rule and have it be playful and fun, especially in a powder bath,” says Cooney, noting that a mirror hangs on the side wall instead. The wallcovering is Pierre Frey’s Uberon print.

Cooney describes an almost cottagey feel in the primary, thanks to a walnut Flora Bed by Jacob May, which was locally made and turned by hand, layered with cozy and textural textiles like Parachute Home’s Hand Stitched Cotton Quilt. Gentle patterns add to the mix through a Scandinavian kilim with geometric design from Rug & Kilim, and light drapes made with a Best Buds textile by Kerry Joyce.

Making the most of existing features, light touches to the bathroom reimagined the space, like painting existing wainscoting in Farrow & Ball’s Tanner’s Brown, and covering the walls in a botanical House of Hackney Herbarium wallpaper. For a touch of texture, Cooney also layered vintage rugs and set an antique Victorian bamboo chair in the corner below the window.

With a view over the living room, Cooney says the lofted floor is like a little escape within the small cottage. Leaning into the cozy feel, the team went with a layered look. A brass chest anchors the space, paired with a black armchair owned by the clients, which was reupholstered with a burgundy fabric from Abraham Moon & Sons, and a Lawson-Fenning Morro Sectional in a linen blend.

The clients’ only real hesitation was a bit of a surprise: the reclaimed brick Cooney specified for the mudroom. It is laid in a herringbone pattern with colors that appeared to shift across the surface. That yellow kitchen? Totally fine. But the expressive bricks? Too dramatic. “We relied on the team’s judgement, and this feature is now one of our most favorite parts of the home,” says the husband, noting how guests often compliment the mudroom.

“I feel like this house is kind of us unchecked,” says Cooney of her clients’ trust. “No one checked us, so we were allowed to do the fullest extent of what we wanted to do.” Her clients could easily have staged their own diva moment after the cabinets were painted in screaming yellow, before everything else around it fell into place around it; but they let the vision come into full focus.

As if cantilevered over the hill, a screened-in porch is the best of both worlds. “You don’t have to have everything be indoor-outdoor in a covered porch—you can really pull the inside out there and not worry so much,” says Cooney, nodding to choices like Henning Kjaernulf dining chairs from the 1960s purchased from Lone Fox. And while most might use a lighter color in this space, Cooney chose a deeper Farrow & Ball Hopper Head paint for the walls and window frames, which she says helps punctuate the view in a beautiful way.

#4778. Honda’s $2,000 electric moped is a winner. So why can’t Americans buy it?

Honda has been steadily expanding its electric motorcycle lineup, but one of its most interesting new models isn’t a flashy high-performance machine. It’s a modest little electric scooter called the Honda UC3, and with prices now dipping close to $2,000 in local markets, it’s the kind of affordable electric two-wheeler that many Americans would probably love to get their hands on.

Unfortunately, that’s probably not happening anytime soon.

We first covered the Honda UC3 earlier this year when the company announced plans to launch the electric scooter in Thailand and Vietnam. Designed as an electric equivalent to a 110cc gasoline-powered scooter, the UC3 is built for practical everyday transportation rather than weekend joyrides.

But don’t let its modest appearance fool you. The UC3 packs a 6 kW (8 hp) electric motor capable of reaching approximately 50 mph (80 km/h), making it more than capable of handling typical urban commuting.

Its 3.2 kWh lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery provides up to 76 miles (122 km) of range under WMTC testing. Unlike Honda’s other electric scooters that use removable batteries, the UC3 has a permanently installed battery that charges directly on the bike.

There’s also a full-color TFT display, smartphone connectivity, three riding modes, reverse gear, and a generous 26-liter under-seat storage compartment.

And perhaps most importantly, it comes from Honda, a company with decades of experience building reliable, affordable motorcycles.

The UC3 originally launched in Vietnam with prices starting around 78.5 million Vietnamese dong, or roughly $3,000. However, recent reports from Vietnamese dealerships show incentives bringing prices down to around 54 million dong, equivalent to approximately $2,100.

That’s a pretty compelling price for a 50 mph (80 km/h) electric scooter from one of the world’s largest motorcycle manufacturers.

But despite its appeal, Honda hasn’t announced any plans to bring the UC3 to the United States.

In much of Southeast Asia, scooters are everyday transportation, with millions of riders using them for commuting, shopping, and practically everything else. In the US, motorcycles are much more commonly recreational vehicles, and the market for small, practical scooters is comparatively tiny.

I'm hoping scooters like this take over the world, the sooner the better. Anyone who's travelled in any cities in Asian, India, or most of Europe, there's always a constant background whine of 2-stroke scooters, day and night.

I would have thought these kinds of markets would have already transitioned to EV - in addition to the annoying sound, those 2-stroke engines spew out an order of magnitude more pollution than most ICE vehicles. I guess it's been a cost issue - this scooter should be cheap enough to take over the world.

Add in the costs of US regulatory certification, establishing parts and service support, and navigating a market where many buyers expect motorcycles to handle highway speeds, and the business case becomes considerably less attractive.

That’s unfortunate, because the UC3 looks like exactly the kind of electric two-wheeler that could make a real difference in American cities.

It’s affordable, practical, fast enough for most city streets, and backed by a manufacturer that isn’t going anywhere.

Now if only Honda would give Americans a chance to buy one.

#4776. China's Z.ai Says Its New AI Model Can Think Like One Four Times Its Size

China's Z.ai Says Its New AI Model Can Think Like One Four Times Its Size

Chinese AI developer Z.ai released GLM-5.4 on Monday, introducing a new architecture that it claims delivers the reasoning capabilities of models nearly four times its size without requiring nearly as many parameters.

The Beijing-based company says its new 750-billion-parameter model can compete with systems containing two to three trillion parameters. It also supports a one-million-token context window, accepts images and video, and can generate up to 128,000 tokens in a single response, according to its technical documentation. Parameters are the internal values a model learns during training, while tokens are the chunks of text it processes and generates—roughly equivalent to words or parts of words.

The release comes as American companies including OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google dominate the highest positions in AI performance rankings. Chinese developers, meanwhile, have increasingly focused on making powerful models cheaper to run, particularly for coding and autonomous agents.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Z.ai has been among China's strongest competitors. As Decrypt reported in August, its predecessor GLM-5.3 was already competitive with leading American coding models, although it still trailed the best proprietary systems on several software engineering tests.

GLM-5.4 introduces what Z.ai calls a Loop Transformer, which processes information through the same 28 neural network layers twice. Traditional Transformers generally pass information through each layer once, so reusing layers effectively increases computational depth without increasing the number of stored parameters.

The company combines this approach with gated updates, which regulate how much information changes between passes, and a hybrid memory cache that avoids storing an entirely separate copy of previous calculations. Sparse attention also helps the model focus its computing power on relevant information rather than processing everything equally.

In controlled experiments on a smaller 30-billion-parameter model, Z.ai reported that its looping technique increased accuracy on AIME 2025, a mathematics examination benchmark scored by the percentage of correctly solved problems, from 19.17% to 30.42%.

GPQA Diamond, a graduate-level science question-answering test also scored by correct-answer percentage, improved from 47.58% to 56.26%. The experiments required approximately 10% additional training compute, while reducing generated reasoning tokens by 10% to 14% on several tests.

Those results concern the experimental architecture, not independently established performance for GLM-5.4 itself. Z.ai has not published a comprehensive flagship benchmark comparison demonstrating that the new model matches Claude or GPT.

The company is also promoting what it calls Generalized Coding: using programming to accomplish tasks beyond software development. Its demonstrations include building a video game, creating 3D environments, and designing a six-axis robotic arm using engineering software.

The company's previous flagship costs $1.40 per million input tokens and $4.40 per million output tokens through its API. By comparison, OpenAI's GPT-6.2 Sol costs $2 and $10, while Anthropic's Claude Opus 5.5 costs $4 and $20, respectively.

At those published rates, GLM-5.3 is considerably cheaper than its American counterparts, although GLM-5.4's own API pricing has not been announced. Its actual performance-per-dollar advantage therefore cannot yet be calculated.

China has other serious contenders. Alibaba's Qwen 4 Max recently topped Chinese competitors on Arena's agent leaderboard, while Moonshot's Kimi K3 and DeepSeek V4 have challenged American developers on coding performance and API pricing.

The performance gap remains contested. A U.S. government evaluation placed DeepSeek roughly eight months behind the American frontier, although researchers questioned its methodology.

Z.ai itself remains on the U.S. Commerce Department's Entity List, following its January 2025 designation over concerns that its AI research could support Chinese military modernization. The restrictions limit access to controlled American technology, but have not stopped the company from releasing new models.

#4775. Mauviel's Copper Cookware Is 25% Off For Black Friday

There are a few covetable luxury goods you’ll see pop up again and again in AD’s home tours: a Moccamaster coffee maker, a Nordic Knots rug, and Mauviel copper cookware, to name a few. As seen in this Sag Harbor home, this Parisian pied-à-terre, this Westchester residence, and this Connecticut country retreat, Mauviel is something of a status symbol in kitchens. Much like a Le Creuset Dutch oven out on display, spying a few copper pans from the French brand hanging from a rail signals that “Great Taste Lives Here.” And this week marks a good opportunity to join the club, with Mauviel sets and individual pieces up to 30% off for Black Friday at Williams Sonoma and on Shop.

I’m a recent convert; I bought my Mauviel frying pan mostly to elevate the aesthetic of my kitchen, with a suspicion that the tool would also function as a fantastic piece of cookware, particularly for my beloved morning ritual of frying up an egg for breakfast. Of course it does. Unlike some of my ceramic pans that catch and dry out an egg, the Mauviel has given me no trouble, delivering me the most perfect eggs to date I’ve ever cooked. Even against my stainless-steel pans (which I do love very much), these heat a bit more quickly and evenly.

That excellent cooking capability is largely due to the construction and craftsmanship of the goods, which have a long history as a French culinary staple. Mauviel has made copper cookware since 1830 in Villedieu-les-Poêles, a town in Normandy with an enduring history of copper manufacturing; the pieces are still handcrafted there. As far as their makeup, the M’150 B pieces are, for example, 1.5 millimeters thick and constructed of a 90% polished copper exterior and a 10% stainless-steel interior. They heat evenly; are immediately responsive to heat adjustments; and work on gas, electric, and ceramic cooktops.

The pans partners with a copper kettle on the stove in this Portland manse by Jessica Helgerson.

The copper treasures do, however, require care. They’re not nonstick pots and pans, so you need a little oil or butter to get any slip. They’re hand-wash only, and Mauviel advises against using them for long stretches on high heat or leaving them empty on a hot burner. I don’t polish mine, simply because I don’t own any copper polish. The wear shows quickly, and I like that, though if AD were shooting my house, maybe I’d like it less.

The Black Friday markdowns run across the line: my copper fry pan is going for $280 in the 10-inch size (25% off), a 2.5-quart sauce pan is $450 (29% off), and a 15-piece cookware set is $3,000 (32% off). For anyone who cooks every day, it’s a hard gift to beat. I, for one, now can’t imagine frying my eggs any other way.

#4773. Latest Canadian budget packs funding for 55,000 new EV chargers

Canada’s government wants to make it easier for citizens to drive electric, and they’re putting more than $4 billion in funding for electricity infrastructure and grid modernization that includes more than 55,000 new EV chargers.

The proposed federal budget set aside $4 billion (CAD) for grid improvements, with a $~635 million carveout specifically for EV charging infrastructure with $587 million of that set aside to renew the Zero-Emission Vehicle Infrastructure Program (ZEVIP), which funds the development of new public EV charging stations.

“The charging industry is really encouraged by the multi-year investment in the budget,” explains Travis Allan, president and CEO of the Canadian Charging Infrastructure Council (CCIC) told Electric Autonomy. “We’ll be looking to make sure that we have great programs in place to deal with the barriers faced by people living in condos and apartments, public fast charging, which needs to cover major corridors and metropolitan areas, and then also charging for fleets and medium- and heavy-duty vehicles, which make up a major share of transportation emissions.”

The rest of the $4 billion will be spent on electricity infrastructure expansion over the next 10 years, starting in 2027 and running through 2036. Because it’s Canada where nearly 60% of electrical generation already comes from renewable hydro and wind power, renewable energy is a de-facto priority, with carveouts for the Smart Renewables and Electrification Pathways Program, new data and modeling efforts to guide grid planning, and $1.6 billion to modernize power systems in northern and remote communities.

The charging infrastructure program highlighted here, ZEVIP, would help charge point operators by covering up to 50% of eligible project expenses for most projects, and up to 75% of projects led by indigenous developers with caps of up to $2 million per project.

Canada continues to take steps to separate itself from the wild swings and economic chaos caused by America’s random tariffs, trade policies, and dumb oil wars – and building out an infrastructure that makes it easier for its citizens to give affordable Chinese EVs a chance while strengthening its relationship with the US’ biggest economic rival?

I sincerely hope this promotes the idea of installing fast chargers along routes with lower populations. I live in Ottawa, driving to my home town in northern Ontario has pretty much a single fast charger, a Tesla one with short cables at the mid point about 2.25h in. 6 stalls. If that goes down, there is nothing for about 400km of a decent charging speed. They need to have alternatives to Tesla (I've been very reliant on Tesla stations and don't own a Tesla) and more stations in between the current Tesla stations. Southern Ontario for example is very well covered with fast chargers. But the market is there so companies install them. Northern Ontario, the trans Canada highway needs these chargers badly.

If Washington doesn’t see that for, “Go pound sand, eh?” that it is, then it’s truly lost the plot – but even if the “blue wave” last week was enough to shake a little sense into the folks running the show, it may already be too late to patch things up.

#4770. Police charge another Tesla driver caught asleep in rush hour as cases pile up

A Tesla driver caught asleep behind the wheel on Ottawa’s Highway 417 during Tuesday’s rush hour now faces a careless driving charge from the Ontario Provincial Police. The photo OPP shared shows a new-generation Model Y in traffic.

The charge carries a $490 fine and six demerit points on conviction. It’s at least the fifth time this year Canadian police have gone public with a sleeping Tesla driver, and it’s not a coincidence that it’s happening as “Full Self-Driving” gets better.

OPP East Region posted about the stop Wednesday morning. Police didn’t name the brand or the driver-assist system, but the message was clear: “even though your vehicle has an autonomous driving feature, drivers are still required to pay due care and attention when behind the wheel.”

According to OPP, the officer could see the driver’s eyes were closed and hands were off the steering wheel. Instead of lighting up the cruiser, police pulled in close to capture body camera footage first.

Careless driving isn’t a parking ticket in Ontario. The $490 is the set fine, but if the case goes to court, the Highway Traffic Act allows fines up to $2,000, up to six months in jail, and a licence suspension of up to two years.

Ottawa is the latest, but British Columbia has been dealing with this all year:

This isn’t new in Canada either. Back in 2020, Alberta RCMP went further and criminally charged a Tesla driver caught sleeping on Autopilot at 150 km/h with dangerous driving.

The U.S. has the same problem with less enforcement. In September, Colorado State Patrol got a report of a sleeping Tesla driver on I-70 near Georgetown and never found the car. The same month, Rep. Raja Krishnamoorthi pressed the DOT after NBC News found 43 videos of drivers apparently asleep in moving Teslas, 17 of them posted in 2026 alone.

And in October, a driver told CHP he was asleep on FSD when his Tesla drove into a closed crash scene and hit a patrol car in Fairfield, California. Two officers were seriously injured.

A few years ago, FSD needed an intervention every few miles, so nobody was napping. You couldn’t.

Now FSD can handle long highway stretches without a takeover, and that’s exactly when supervision breaks down. A human asked to watch a system that’s right 99% of the time stops watching. That’s automation complacency, and it’s one of the most studied problems in aviation and driving research.

Waymo learned this the hard way. In 2013, Google let employees use its highway driver-assist prototype and caught people falling asleep at highway speed. It decided a system that works most of the time but still needs a human to catch its mistakes was the worst of both worlds, and it went straight to driverless.

Tesla went the other way. It sells a Level 2 system called “Full Self-Driving” that’s good enough to lull people to sleep, and it puts the full responsibility on the human who’s now asleep.

Since FSD v12.4, Tesla has relied mainly on the cabin camera above the rearview mirror to make sure the driver is watching the road. But look at what Tesla’s own owner’s manual says about it: the camera “does not require full visibility of the driver’s eyes in order to monitor attentiveness,” and the system “is still active, for example, if the driver is wearing sunglasses.”

In other words, it’s often judging head position, not whether your eyes are open. When it can’t see your hands, it falls back to asking for a bit of torque on the wheel, which a sleeping driver resting a hand on the rim can provide.

The penalties are soft too. Five “strikeouts” suspend FSD for a week, and each strike is forgiven after three and a half days.

And Tesla has been moving in the wrong direction. Musk said in December 2025 that FSD v14.2.1 lets drivers text “depending on context of surrounding traffic.” In May 2026, he said v14.3.3 “nags less too.” When the car does detect drowsiness, Tesla’s alert reads “Drowsiness detected. Stay focused with FSD.” That pushes a tired driver to lean harder on the system instead of pulling over.

Compare that with GM’s Super Cruise, which uses a steering column camera with infrared LEDs to track head and eye position, and only works hands-free on pre-mapped highways.

The Ottawa driver had their eyes closed and their hands off the wheel in rush hour traffic, and the car was still driving when an officer pulled up beside it. Whatever Tesla’s camera was checking from a foot away, it hadn’t stopped the car before a cop in the next lane did.

We’re glad Canadian police are treating this seriously. A $490 fine and six points is a real deterrent, and OPP’s message is the right one: no matter what Tesla calls it, you’re the driver.

People have been falling asleep at the wheel since driving started; many, if not most, have ended up in tragedy. At least with the self-driving cars, there is a margin of safety, especially for other drivers. These cars should monitor the driver better and pull off the road when it happens.

But police can’t ticket their way out of this. These drivers aren’t edge cases anymore. They’re the predictable result of a system that drives well enough to make people relax, sold under a name that tells them relaxing is fine.

The fix is on Tesla’s side, and it isn’t complicated. Detect closed eyes, even behind sunglasses, which is what infrared eye-tracking is built for. When a driver doesn’t respond, slow down and pull over immediately instead of escalating warnings for minutes. Stop telling drowsy drivers to “stay focused with FSD.” And make the strike system actually sting.

NHTSA already forced a recall of over 2 million Teslas in December 2023 over weak Autopilot monitoring. Three years later, people are still sleeping through rush hour on FSD.

If you’re driving an EV, charging it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4769. 15 Best Places to Buy Bedding, Editor Picks (2026)

Sheets get slept in every night and washed every week (in theory) which means it doesn’t take long to learn which ones hold up under use. Every retailer here comes recommended by an AD editor or contributor who has lived with the bedding at home for a long stretch: flannel kept on the bed through May, linen hauled to a communal laundromat and back, or a sheet set that survives a dog digging in every night.

Each brand earned its spot for a reason, whether that’s price, a cooling fabric, an unusually wide color range, or embroidery done by hand. Rest assured: they’re all comfy.

For bedding basics that won’t damage the budget, Quince is our first stop. Its European Linen Sheet Set earned our pick for the best affordable linen sheets, and senior shopping editor Nashia Baker has recently added the brand to her list of favorites. “The price point is unbeatable, and I love that they offer bedding sets in beautiful colorways and a variety of fabric options,” she says. “The quality is also impressive, especially considering the affordability.”

Made from 100% European flax, the sheets cost a fraction of what competitors charge, thanks in part to Quince’s direct-to-consumer model which skips traditional retail markups. “I love its airy nature along with the gorgeous Bordeaux color that complements the rest of my decor,” says Baker. The bedding range extends to quilts and coverlets, , and the brand’s down comforter also made our ranking of the best comforters.

Anyone who has paused a Nancy Meyers movie to study the bedroom should start with Serena & Lily. The brand is based in Sausalito, California, but its textured, breezy bedding and decor would look right at home on any coast, Nantucket included. The range covers lightweight linen and crisp cotton percale in pale colors and easygoing prints.

Of all the bedding Baker has tested, the Nantucket Stripe Sheet Set ranks near the top for its light weight and “worn-in texture,” which keeps the linen soft instead of scratchy. The ruffle trim keeps her bed from looking too plain. “The striped mink print does give a seaside vibe, but it’s been neutral enough to layer with any color bed cover without clashing with the rest of my minimal, romantic decor over the few months I’ve tested the set,” she adds. For a top layer, there’s the scalloped Baywood Quilt, which Alpert likes as a light blanket for “when you want to throw something over while you read your favorite Emily Henry book in bed.”

Boll & Branch sheets arrive feeling as if they’ve already been through several wash cycles, without the fraying or pilling that usually comes with that. Contributor Rachel Fletcher tested the brand’s percale, Supima cotton, flannel, and linen, but its signature sateen stood out most for its long-staple cotton and a soft, smooth finish. “That extra-soft feel, paired with the lovely, cooling properties, make them feel like the luxury sheets they are,” she says. Converts can outfit the rest of the room too, with Boll & Branch’s mattresses and bedroom furniture.

Brooklinen started out selling two things, cotton percale and sateen sheets, and has since added cashmere, brushed flannel, and a long list of prints. Pieces come in bundled sets or one at a time, and prices land between the budget brands and the luxury labels. Our editors keep coming back to the linen sheets, which they use in every season.

“Since I don’t live my life on a Greek island, I thought white linen sheets year-round might be a slightly bold move, but the nature of the fiber is versatile and temperature regulating,” says AD contributor Madeleine Luckel, who tested the Core Sheet Set. Fletcher sleeps on the Luxe Sheet Set and credits it with keeping her cool. “I’m a hot sleeper with sensitive skin, and they’re really cool on my skin at night, which is a huge relief,” she says. “I love the breathability of these sheets.”

Few bedding brands have tied their name to sustainability as closely as Coyuchi. Its linen sheets are made from certified organic French flax grown without synthetic fertilizers or pesticides, and the brand also offers percale, sateen, and flannel sheets. The earthy color palette is a selling point on its own.

“I love the color, which is what drew me to these sheets in the first place,” says Sam Cochran, global features editor. “They’re the perfect brown—a tad auburn, ever so slightly timeworn in palette.”

Shopping writer Julia Harrison and contributor Kate McGregor agree: Garnet Hill is their “secret spot” for bedding. Harrison is devoted to the flannel sheets and admits she leaves them on the bed through May because “they’re so soft, I’d rather suffer in my own sweat than switch them out.” The ticking stripe is “perfectly classic—like a true vintage piece, but not actually vintage…that might be tough sell in the bedding department.” Alpert also loves her flannel pick, a duvet cover that “signals a cottagecore moment without going over the top.” Paired with her Ostermoor duvet insert, it’s “like climbing under a layer of meringue.” Garnet Hill specializes in vintage-inspired designs, patchwork quilts especially, made in 100% linen and 100% cotton options at reasonable prices.

Cozy Earth makes breathable bedding for people who run hot at night, and its signature bamboo bedding, made of 100% viscose, are the brand’s best-known example. “I honestly thought these sheets were silk,” says contributor Megan Wahn. “That’s how smooth and soft they feel.” Wahn tested them mostly in winter, and despite feeling cool to the touch, they kept her warm. “I’m especially eager to see how they perform in the summer,” she adds. “Something about their smoothness makes me think the summer-night sweats won’t stand a chance.”

Parachute is where we send anyone after classic, well-made cotton bedding. Its sets and bundles are designed with sustainability in mind, and the percale and brushed cotton are made by artisans at the brand’s factory in Portugal.

“We love [Parachute] because the bedding is designed to provide a hotel-like experience, offering a perfect balance of softness and crispness,” says Stephanie Kraus of Stephanie Kraus Designs, whose clients favor the brand’s linen bedding sets. “They become softer with each wash while maintaining their durability and are super soft to sleep in every night.”

Fletcher also praises the brand’s commitment to organic, ethically sourced materials; the Organic Cloud Cotton Duvet Set, for one, is made of Oeko-Tex-certified 100% cotton. “I try a lot of bedding in this line of work and the more I do, the more I appreciate ethical and organic production, even if the price tag is a little higher,” she says.

GreenRow makes charming, long-lasting bedding from recycled and sustainably sourced materials, and it has won over several people on the Shop AD team. Contributor Yelena Moroz Alpert calls the Helena Floral Sheet Set “without a doubt, the softest sheets I own.” She compares the cotton satin to velvet, and the butter-yellow florals and green micro-gingham ruffle make the set prettier still. McGregor, meanwhile, is partial to the reversible patchwork quilt. “It’s the kind of blanket that feels substantial without trapping heat or feeling stuffy,” she notes. “I feel cocooned without a down comforter, but I’m not smothered when I sleep sans AC in the warmer months. It’s a year-round bedding piece for me, especially since the color palette can lean moodier or more cheerful depending on my sheet selection.”

Rebecca Udall is a newer name and one worth knowing. Her namesake bedding line is built on romantic, careful details, such as soft-colored decorative embroidery along the sheets. McGregor’s current favorite is this scalloped percale sheet set. “The feel is like a washed percale, crisp but forgiving, and I find that the subtle color on the detailed edge gives the perfect amount of visual interest to my all-white bedscape,” she says. If you want less ornament, try the Georgiana duvet cover, with faint ladderstitch detailing hand-embroidered by artisans in Northern Italy.

Hommey, an Australian brand founded in 2020, first caught on with its boldly striped robes and nightwear. It has since carried those designs into cotton and linen sheets and duvet covers. Across both fabrics, there are more than two dozen colors and prints to choose from. For maximalists who want to try color-blocking, the brand’s bolder colorways are a great place to start.

Baker tested the brown sugar linen duvet cover and found the stonewashed, Oeko-Tex certified flax smoother and more broken-in straight out of the package than the Quince version she already owned (above). “I won’t get ahead of myself and say it feels like pure silk, but I can confirm that it’s soft and breathable almost like an organic cotton duvet after a few washes,” Baker says. She pairs the chocolatey shade with burgundy linen sheets for a moodier bed, though it also works as a neutral with lighter sheets in oatmeal, which pick up the cream trim. The one drawback is that the cover has no interior ties, so the insert can shift around inside.

Australian brand Cultiver makes linen sheets and duvets with an easy, beachy look. Its Oeko-Tex certified pieces are an investment, and a worthwhile one. Coverlets are generously sized so they drape loosely, and the envelope-back pillow cases hold their shape without looking stiff. Fletcher has put her set through heavy use and many wash cycles. “Linen is my bedding fabric of choice pretty much 365 days a year, and Cultiver’s high-quality sheet sets do it for me in a way no other brand has. Out of all the sets I’ve tried, this one has stood the test of many washes, my dog digging in them to get cozy at night, and surviving across seasons without feeling worn or threadbare,” she says.

Erica Tanov’s bedding is handmade by artisans in Jaipur, in 100% linen with embroidery drawn from traditional Indian textiles. Harrison calls hers “a lifetime set” that “draws more compliments than anything else in my apartment.” The linen is soft and tough: She hauls this sheet set to her neighborhood laundromat, and it survives the communal machines without damage. It’s the first linen set she’s found to be “city-friendly,” as she calls it. She hasn’t tried the quilts or throw blankets yet, though one already tops her birthday wishlist (this one).

It depends mostly on how you like your bed to feel and how reliable your thermostat is. If you like the crisp coolness of hotel sheets, try percale. If you like a soft, organic feel, try linen for a breezy texture, or bamboo sheets for something similar to silk. Cotton sheets (like Supima sets) are often the most durable over time, especially after many, many washes. Your warmest options are generally flannel sets, and your coolest are generally bamboo and percale. For a basic setup, we recommend a full sheet set (flat sheet included, people) plus a duvet insert with a cover. The cover protects the insert and keeps it clean, so you’ll generally only need to wash the insert every six months rather than weekly like your sheets. Harrison likes to go one step further with a bed blanket underneath her duvet and above her sheets for a bit of weight and additional warmth. If a fluffy duvet isn’t your thing, a quilt or coverlet can take its place and add a little more weight.

Start with the fiber content on the label. Search for 100% cotton, linen (the more organic, the better), and steer clear of polyesters or more plastic fibers, as they’re likely to pill more quickly and break apart in the washer and dryer.
