#4767. Navigating the Unpredictable Ride of COPD

When COPD flare-ups affect breathing and health-related quality of life, ask about other treatment options.

HOOVES THUNDER AGAINST packed dirt as the bull bucks to the left and the rider leans right, tightening his grip on the rope. Just as he finds his balance, another sudden buck or turn can change the course of the ride.

The unexpected twists and turns of a bull ride may feel familiar to people living with inadequately controlled chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD). Not knowing when breathing challenges or a flare-up—a period when breathing becomes more difficult than usual that can last for several days—might occur can make everyday life feel unpredictable.

Mark H., 60, has experienced the challenges that can come with COPD since he was diagnosed in 2010. “COPD isn’t easy to navigate, and it can be a frightening condition to face,” he says. “I tried to ignore my diagnosis early on because I didn’t want to believe it, and I didn’t want to think about how it might affect my life. But ignoring it didn’t stop my COPD from making itself known. Shortness of breath was really impacting my day to day.”

With support and clinical intervention, Mark was able to better manage his COPD and get back to doing more of the things he loves. Now, he wants others living with COPD to know help is available to manage their condition.

COPD IS A complex and progressive disease that can make breathing more difficult over time.

COPD can be caused by inflammation in the airways that causes damage to parts of your lungs. Patients with COPD may also have chronic bronchitis and/or emphysema.

People with COPD can experience persistent cough, excessive mucus production, wheezing, chest tightness, and shortness of breath. Understanding COPD is important, and recognizing when the disease may not be adequately controlled is critical. Living with uncertainty around flare-ups can carry an emotional weight as well, says Corinne Young, nurse practitioner, pulmonary disease specialist, who is the Founder/Executive Director of the Association of Pulmonary Advanced Practice Providers.

Flare-ups, also known as exacerbations, are the worsening of COPD symptoms over several days. They can happen to anyone with COPD. During a flare-up, difficulty breathing, wheezing, and coughing can intensify and take longer to resolve. Even one flare-up can lead to permanent lung damage.

Young, who in addition to seeing patients, competes in team roping at rodeos, knows the importance of paying attention to changing conditions—a perspective she brings to helping patients recognize when their COPD may be inadequately controlled.

“The biggest predictor of a flare-up is a previous flare-up, and they start to get closer and closer together,” Young explains. For example, someone may experience a flare-up or exacerbation once every two years, then once a year, then every six months, then every three. “Inflammatory responses in the body drive these exacerbations, and that may lower lung function,” Young adds. That decline can increase the risk of additional flare-ups, perpetuating the cycle.

AS MARK’S COPD became more difficult to manage, it increasingly interfered with the life he was used to living. Shortness of breath and frequent flare-ups made it harder to work, travel, and do as many of the things he enjoyed. Eventually, he made the difficult decision to retire early from his job as a field service engineer.

Even after stepping away from work, Mark continued to struggle with his COPD. “I remember feeling like, ‘Well, I guess this is my life now,’” he says.

IN THE FALL of 2024, Mark underwent a pulmonary function test, bracing for bad news. The results confirmed that his current treatment plan was no longer adequately managing his COPD, prompting a discussion with his pulmonologist about what to do next. His doctor explained that Mark’s high eosinophil count could be a sign of type 2 inflammation. With this information, Mark and his doctor explored other treatment options, including Dupixent® (dupilumab).

Dupixent is a prescription medicine used with other medicines for the maintenance treatment of adults with inadequately controlled COPD and a high number of blood eosinophils (a type of white blood cell that may contribute to your COPD). Dupixent is used to reduce the number of flare-ups and can improve your breathing.

Dupixent is not used to relieve sudden breathing problems and will not replace an inhaled rescue medicine.

Do not use if you are allergic to dupilumab or to any of the ingredients in Dupixent.

Dupixent can cause serious side effects, such as allergic reactions, including skin reactions, that can sometimes be severe, eye problems, and joint aches and pain.

Please see additional Important Safety Information below.

Mark noticed a difference after adding Dupixent. “I could breathe better,” he says. Now, Mark feels less winded, and he’s doing more of the things he loves. “I’m really grateful that I can enjoy my hobbies more without constantly thinking about my COPD,” he adds.

For Mark, better breathing started with an honest conversation. Young encourages anyone living with COPD to be open with their healthcare provider, who can explain the full range of treatment options available. “It’s not just inhalers anymore—there are a lot of options,” she says.

Like bull riders who stay alert to changing conditions, people living with COPD can watch for signs that their disease may be inadequately controlled and talk with their doctor about their treatment options. With the right support, they may be able to enjoy more of the moments that matter to them—including, for some, time spent in the rodeo stands.

IMPORTANT SAFETY INFORMATION & INDICATION

Dupixent is a prescription medicine used with other medicines for the maintenance treatment of adults with inadequately controlled COPD and a high number of blood eosinophils (a type of white blood cell that may contribute to your COPD). Dupixent is used to reduce the number of flare-ups and can improve your breathing.

Do not use if you are allergic to dupilumab or to any of the ingredients in DUPIXENT®.

Before using DUPIXENT, tell your healthcare provider about all your medical conditions, including if you:

Tell your healthcare provider about all the medicines you take, including prescription and over-the-counter medicines, vitamins, and herbal supplements.

Especially tell your healthcare provider if you are taking oral, topical, or inhaled corticosteroid medicines or have COPD and also have asthma. Do not change or stop your other medicines, including corticosteroid medicine or other asthma medicine, without talking to your healthcare provider. This may cause other symptoms that were controlled by those medicines to come back.

DUPIXENT can cause serious side effects, including:

The most common side effects in patients with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease include injection site reactions, common cold symptoms (nasopharyngitis), high count of a certain white blood cell (eosinophilia), viral infection, back pain, inflammation inside the nose (rhinitis), diarrhea, stomach problems (gastritis), joint pain (arthralgia), toothache, headache, and urinary tract infection.

Tell your healthcare provider if you have any side effect that bothers you or that does not go away. These are not all the possible side effects of DUPIXENT. Call your doctor for medical advice about side effects. You are encouraged to report negative side effects of prescription drugs to the FDA. Visit www.fda.gov/medwatch, or call 1-800-FDA-1088.

Use DUPIXENT exactly as prescribed by your healthcare provider. It’s an injection given under the skin (subcutaneous injection). Your healthcare provider will decide if you or your caregiver can inject DUPIXENT. Do not try to prepare and inject DUPIXENT until you or your caregiver have been trained by your healthcare provider.

Please see accompanying full Prescribing Information including Patient Information.

Learn more about Dupixent at Dupixent.com/COPD.

DUPIXENT is a registered trademark of Sanofi or an affiliate.

#4765. The 7 Best Electric Razors for Women in 2026, Tested by Beauty Editors

It's time to ditch the disposable before your holiday party.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

My interest in hair removal started in elementary school when I snuck into my mom's medicine cabinet and dragged her razor across my leg like I was coloring with my favorite box of markers. Needless to say, I've learned the right way to shave since then (namely to apply shaving cream and gently glide my razor toward the grain with light pressure).

I've had a lot of help perfecting my technique—as a beauty editor with a decade of testing experience, I have access to the best dermatologists and aestheticians. Yet, no matter how on-point my new shaving technique is now, most women's razors never get my legs quite as smooth. And other hair removal methods are super painful, ripping strands out at the root. Ouch!

But then, I upgraded to electric razors—and I've never turned back.

“A closer shave can feel nice, but because the surface of our skin is not perfectly smooth, it inevitably results in removal of some skin cells,” says dermatologist Caroline Robinson, MD. This can lead to microscopic nicks and irritation. “Electric razors can help avoid this. They’re typically less sensitizing,” says Dr. Robinson, because the shave they provide is not as close. At-home laser hair removal devices, waxing, sugaring, and threading are all great options for hair removal if you’ve got the time (and the coin), but if silky-smooth skin sans irritation is what you’re after, the answer is electric.

Over the past two years, Women's Health editors have been testing popular electric razors to determine which seven are worth the investment. Ultimately, we found that the Philips Beauty Series was the best multi-use product due to its five-blade razor and dual-sidedness that allows for a super close shave. Meanwhile, the Remington Smooth & Silky On-the-Go Shaver delivers on quality for quick touch-ups while being budget- and travel-friendly. Read on to see our other winners from our testing process.

A favorite of our editors, this hair removal device hits all of the targets we're looking for when buying an electric razor. For starters, it literally has 40 micro-grip tweezers in its motor to provide a super close shave, keeping your skin smooth for weeks at a time. While that might seem intimidating, it upgrades your in-shower shaving routine in a major way.

You can hit even the trickiest spots on your body. From your underarms and belly down to your ankles, this electric razor has you covered quite literally from head to toe. And with an impressive 50-minute runtime you can eliminate all of my unwanted hair with this device at least a couple of times before recharging it for just an hour. You’ll also appreciate that it's lightweight but not flimsy like it'll slip out of your grip. Plus, it's waterproof so you can shave in the shower without worrying about your new razor cranking out mid-shave.

An upgrade from the original Braun model, the more expensive but higher-tech Flex comes with a flexible head (hence, the name) that can uniquely adapt to your body's curves and contours—even the complicated corners of your bikini line. In addition to the epilator, the bundle also comes with a body exfoliation brush, skin contact cap for additional comfort, and more—definitely a bargain.

"The face and body brushes are especially useful, and I love how you can interchange the heads depending on what type of cleansing you want to do," says Women's Health contributor Isabel McMahon.

She credits the device’s built-in light, called the "smartlight," as a great option for women who like dimmer lighting while showering but still want to see what they're doing.

We previously tried and enjoyed the Philips Lady Shaver Series 6000, but it's no longer available. Thankfully, the brand upgraded to the 9000 model—and we love the changes.

The different blades of the Philips electric razor are designed to target specific areas of your body, like the more compact bikini trimmer comb and head versus the larger trimming comb for your legs. It does an excellent job of trimming coarse body hair without any irritation or tugging.

Regardless of which attachment you employ, they all ensure that you can shave safely, even in the tightest contours of your legs and underarms. Whether you shave wet or dry, the ergonomic handle design will make sure nothing slips through your fingers. For this reason, we think the Philips is an incredible value. There are other, less expensive options on our list, but they're not quite as versatile in terms of where you can use them, so that's why this one took the top value spot.

It’s shaped like a lipstick tube and features a hypoallergenic head that glides over rogue chin hairs and your upper lip with ease. Though it lacks a built-in mirror or LED light so you can conveniently see and remove even the smallest facial hairs, this device is only $20 compared to the $47 Philips model. With those savings you could easily treat yourself to a light up makeup to ensure you see every unwanted hair.

Pro tip: You should try using it before your skincare routine to help your products to really penetrate the skin for a major glow. Just don't use anything with too many active ingredients like retinol right after as that can cause sensitivity.

Amazon reviewers can’t get enough of this affordable electric razor. It boasts a casual 4.2-star average from nearly 15,500 Amazon reviews. "While it's too big to remove hair on my brows, it's awesome for those larger areas of the face when you're short on time and want to look smooth and stubble-free," she says.

Pro tip: The large shaver head does tend to get clogged after a few uses, which can be prevented if you wipe it down with alcohol after every use. Taking care of your device will ensure you get the closest shave and that it lasts for years.

You’ll appreciate how beginner-friendly the Philips razor is. The instructions are so clear and easy to follow, which make your experience a breeze. I enjoyed my bikini line shaving experience enough to use this device on my underarms and that's when I realized it heats up relatively quickly, which may be something to keep in mind if you're going for a longer shave sesh. Unlike the sold out Gillette razor we featured, Philips is rechargeable, so you won’t have to buy batteries once it dies out.

With the multitude of options available on Amazon, Ulta, and Dermstore, there are a couple of features to consider the next time you're in the market for a fresh shave:

More is not always better. In fact, a larger number of blades can sometimes increase irritation, says dermatologist Lauren Ploch, MD. On the other hand, fewer blades mean more passes over the skin, which can also cause irritation. It's about finding a happy medium that works for you. This may take some testing, so remember to try out your razor on a small patch of skin to see how you react before going all in.

Think about how well a razor feels and fits in your hand. You want something that’s well-balanced and not too heavy for the most comfortable shaving experience. Also consider if it will shave wet or dry. Not all electric razors can be used in the shower, Dr. Ploch points out. So, if you're a shower shaver, make sure to grab something waterproof and cordless.

You also want to make sure your razor head can contour to your body's curves, especially when you're targeting areas like the bikini line, underarms, toes, and even legs. Some of the razors on this list, like the Panasonic Close Curves Electric Razor, can uniquely adapt to the body's curves with their rotating heads.

If a razor is rechargeable (big plus already), make sure to check how long it takes to charge and the battery life. If you don't want to deal with a cord or annoying charge time, some of the options on this list—hi, Philips Series 5000 Electric Facial Hair Remover—are battery-powered, which may be more convenient while traveling.

Some razors come with trimmers, blade guides, and changeable blade heads for different areas of the body. If you're looking for more of a Swiss Army knife-type, multi-tool situation, take a look at the available attachments. Then, see if those are compatible with the parts of your body you want to tackle.

Believe it or not, many razors have a warranty, so if you're unsatisfied with your purchase, you can most likely return it for a longer period than you might think. Most razors on this list have a 30-day warranty, which gives you ample time for testing.

Our team tests a wide range of products and services to provide our readers with reliable, honest, and trustworthy information. It's our belief that our readers deserve the best, which is why our recommendations have been thoroughly tested and vetted by our very own editors. For the past year, Women’s Health editors, including myself, and contributors Jasmine Gomez and Sabrina Talbert, tested the bestselling, most popular, and top-rated electric razors. Our team also consulted four dermatologists, and then evaluated thousands of customer reviews and ratings to narrow down the seven best electric razors for a wide range of needs and preferences.

Ready for smooth, bump-free skin? Look no further than these editor-tested and expert-approved electric razors for women, below.

If you struggle with bumps and irritation, an electric razor might be your new best friend. Of course, you still need to make sure you use that electric razor properly. Dermatologists say you should follow the below best practices for a smooth shave every time.

Make sure your skin is well hydrated before shaving because using an electric razor over dry, irritated skin can make matters worse, says dermatologist Joshua Zeichner, MD. Dr. Robinson adds that you should also use a gentle cleanser prior to shaving. And, avoid fragrance or products known to cause irritation immediately before and after shaving, she says.

And please, do so in the direction of the hair growth, says Dr. Zeichner. Repeated strokes over the same area increase the likelihood of irritation and ingrown hairs.

Hydration is key. "I advise patients that they do not need a dedicated post-shave or aftercare lotion—a gentle moisturizer is just fine in these areas," says Dr. Robinson, who likes light, fragrance-free formulas. Aside from that, Dr. Robinson recommends making sure you follow any specific instructions on the product package—in other words, you should actually read the directions.

At the end of the day, everyone's going to have their personal preference, and it's not worth, ahem, splitting hairs if you prefer one type of razor over another. That being said, if your skin is prone to irritation, it might be time to ditch the traditional razor. With an electric razor, you’re not scraping against the skin really hard like a traditional razor does, says dermatologist Cameron Rokhsar, MD. This means that you won't see a lot of the cuts and burns that sometimes result from a manual blade, says Dr. Rokhsar. There are also areas of the body that might be better suited to shave with an electric razor. "Most women's electric razors are designed for our curvy areas," says Dr. Ploch. "I prefer electric razors around the ankles because manual razors cut this area easily."

Keeping your electric razor clean is incredibly important, says Dr. Rokhsar. Many derms will recommend changing a manual razor every time you shave, but you can't do that with an electric one, which makes running the blades under warm water after use a crucial step. This will keep it free of hair, shaving cream, and other residues.

Dr. Rokhsar also recommends swiping over the blades and the outside of the razor with a rubbing alcohol-soaked cotton ball regularly. "Especially if it sits in your bathroom moisture, bacteria will accumulate on the blades," he says. "It's an easy entry point for bacteria to go down the hair follicle." That's a big no-no if you want to stay free of irritation or infection.

Our team of editors follow Dr. Rokhsar's advice and it hasn't led us astray. And despite evaluating some of these razors for close to a year, we are pleased that we haven't noticed any rusting after using the alcohol-soaked cotton ball.

Another tip is to actually read and follow the maintenance instructions of your device. (Admittedly, we threw ours away since we've been using these for several months and like to keep the limited space of our beauty closet as streamlined as possible.) You can also prioritize using devices like the Philips Series 5000 Electric Facial Hair Remover that's easy to clean, as the head can easily be removed so you can dump shaved hair and dead skin cells straight into the garbage.

#4763. Ginger Rogers at Home: 10 Photos of the Golden Age Icon’s Glamorous SoCal Life

While most people race to the hospital to give birth, Ginger Rogers’s mother, Lela, insisted that her baby girl’s first breaths be taken in their Missouri home. “The little four-room house at 100 Moore Street was the perfect choice” for the future icon’s July 1911 welcome to the world, Rogers concluded in her 1991 memoir, Ginger: My Story. The star would go on to live with her extended family in Kansas City at 3306 Bellefountain Street; with her stepfather in Fort Worth at 1510 Cooper Street; and once she became a Hollywood sensation, a sprawling Beverly Hills estate. That joy and sense of peace she found in her childhood residence became a defining feature of her California pad. “It was a fun house, a happy house,” Rogers told Architectural Digest in 1994. “I was never angry when I didn’t have to work, because I liked to stay home so much.”

Decades after her death in 1995, Rogers is poised to return to the big screen by way of Sabrina Carpenter, who’s set to portray the late actor in the forthcoming, yet-to-be-titled Fred Astaire biopic. Tom Holland will star as Astaire. Read on for a glimpse inside the Oscar winner’s off-set life in the places she cherished most.

After Rogers separated from her second husband, actor Lew Ayres, in 1936, she and her mother moved in together. “I asked Mother if she would like me to build her a house, and if she did, could I live in it with her,” Rogers wrote in her memoir. “The next day she scoured the area for a suitable place to build. She found a wonderful lot high in Beverly Hills on 2.75 acres on Gilcrest Drive in Coldwater Canyon. It had a view that didn’t stop; when the fog burned off late every afternoon, it was possible to see Catalina.” Rogers is pictured at the stunning estate in this 1938 photo.

The actor with her home ice cream fountain.

The Swing Time star added many personal touches to the home, one of which was an elaborate soda fountain. In this 1940 photo, she’s shown whipping up a sweet treat. In her memoir, Rogers recalled all the amenities she installed. “The building took over a year because of labor strikes and material shortages,” she wrote. “When the house was finished, it proved to have been worth waiting for. It had everything I wanted or needed in a house: swimming pool, tennis court, projection room, and my very own soda fountain. Who could ask for anything more?”

Rogers opened her home tennis court to championship hopefuls so they could get practice matches in.

Rogers was an avid tennis player. Though she’s pictured here in 1950 at her Malibu beach house, the actor also had a home court installed at Gilcrest Drive. “On the way to the pool and tennis court were eight sculptured bay leaf trees. Next to the blue-tiled pool was my tennis court. This was my pride and joy,” she wrote in Ginger: My Story. With the knowledge that Rogers had the only en-tout-cas court in the area, the Tennis Association “begged” her to invite young hopefuls for some practice before they went on to championship matches at Forest Hills, New York. “I was delighted to open my court to competitors like Pancho Gonzales, Gussie Moran; and, later, Billie Jean King, Jennifer Hood, and Alice Marble slammed a few over the net too,” Rogers said.

Rogers was known for hosting her Hollywood star friends at home in Beverly Hills.

The Top Hat actor’s Beverly Hills abode was also a hot party spot for Rogers’s A-list friends. “My home was made for entertaining and I gave party after party,” she wrote in her memoir. “Most of them were informal. I’d serve spaghetti and we’d play some night tennis. Alcohol was never served, just cold drinks. One, a mixture of finely crushed ice, 7-Up, and concentrated orange juice, was a real favorite with the tennis crowd.”

The star had a close relationship with her mother, Lela.

Rogers loved living with her mother, with whom she’s pictured in the circa 1940s portrait on the piano in this 1954 image. The triple threat pushed back against the term “stage mother” in Architectural Digest; she considered her mom to be “my guide and my professor… she was my encyclopedia. She knew what route to take: She helped Lucille Ball along hers too, you know. It would have been hard to have a career without her.” About their Beverly Hills pad, she added, “we both liked the look of a French farmhouse. And I wanted certain specific things: a helpful bath, a tennis court, and my own projection room, where we had eight or ten director’s chairs, so that friends could come over to watch movies.”

Rogers with a furry friend at home in the 1950s.

The home featured a main house with bedrooms for Rogers and her mom, as well as a guesthouse for visitors. “The house was very bright,” she told Architectural Digest. “I don’t like dark things; even in my clothing, I prefer to be light and colorful—Springtime for Henry has always been my motto,” she explained, referencing the 1934 comedy. In her memoir, Rogers added, “The style was an American farmhouse with Dutch doors opening to cool breezes. The large bay window in the corner of the living room had a view of Beverly Hills and a bit of Los Angeles. Off the living room was a patio for barbecuing on a flagstone area under a beautiful Australian pepper tree. It was a wonderful place to entertain on crisp California nights.”

Rogers’s home also included a solarium, and bedrooms with scenic views. “On a lower level was an outside entrance to the playroom,” the actor noted in her memoir. “This room had big windows and comfortable cushions as did the solarium in the hallway to the patio. I read many a book, with apple in hand, buried in those downy cushions.” About her bedrooms, she added, “There were two bedrooms leading from the solarium, one for Lelee [her mother] with a four-posted bed and one for me. My room looked out on the view of Beverly Hills, and I had a beautiful hand-carved mantel for my fireplace.”

In addition to the stage talents for which she became a star, Rogers was also a painter. Her guesthouse also doubled as a studio. “My studio, which could double as a guesthouse, held all the paraphernalia of my hobbies: painting materials, canvases, paintbrushes, turpentine, pencils, and so on,” she wrote in her memoir. “Whenever there was a lull on set, I’d take out my pencil and sketch,” Rogers added. “I had been drawing for a long time and it seemed to come naturally to me. I found it challenging and relaxing. I never studied drawing or painting, I just did it.”

In her memoir, Rogers recalled a time when she brought her paintings out for a gallery show. “When I’m not working or traveling, my main source of relaxation comes from my painting,” she wrote. “Gallery owners have wanted to have a one-person show of my work, but I never had the time to organize my paintings. Well, now was the time. Gregg Juarez, a gallery owner and friend of mine, arranged for a show and sale at his gallery in Palm Desert. As we were hanging the paintings for display, I realized these were my children—I couldn’t sell them! Gregg told me I had to sell a few so the customers wouldn’t get mad. I managed to put a few up for sale, but couldn’t part with the rest. So now they are joyously hung in my home for all to see.”

Rogers was 83 years old when she died in 1995 at home in California.

Rogers remained at her home on Gilcrest Drive until 1970, when her mom urged her to sell while she was away working in Europe. “I had been renting it out more than living in it lately, and Mother thought it was financially sound to put it on the market,” the actor wrote in her memoir. “Besides, she said it needed a new roof! She handled all the details of the sale…. This was one time I’m not sure Mother knew best. I adored that house on Gilcrest Drive and have regretted selling it ever since.” Rogers died decades later, on April 25, 1995, at her home in Rancho Mirage, per the Los Angeles Times.

For Actors Dichen Lachman and Maximilian Osinski, a Temporary Move to London Turned Into a Colorful House Renovation

Julianne Moore Trades Her Manhattan Town House for a Serene, Light-Filled Duplex Atop a 1920s Building

That being said, there is a bit of extra fabric around the crotch, which is supposed to give that area more breathing room, but might be too baggy for some wearers.

Women's Health food producer Tina Martinez touts these Hey Nuts leggings for their smooth, light feel and ability to stay in place. She loves that they offer ample support, and that they feel like they're holding you in the right places. Martinez also loves their great compression, the fact that they don't roll down, and how opaque they are (no panty lines here!). They're also super stretchy and comfortable, so you can wear them at home or to the gym.

These leggings have the most inseam options on this list, ranging from 25 inches to 31 inches if you're on the taller side. In terms of sizing, our testers find that these leggings run totally true-to-size, which, if you're an avid Amazon shopper, you know is hard to come by.

We had very few cons about these leggings, but we did agree that the butt seam felt a bit too low, so they're not the most flattering option on this list.

Professor Preeti Arya, PhD, specializes in textile development at the Fashion Institute of Technology. She says that the right fabric for leggings depends on your needs. But if you're worried about maintenance, "you can go for something that is petroleum-based because care is easy," she says. Petroleum-based fabrics include spandex, polyester, and nylon. These options are also typically much cheaper, according to Arya, but you can also opt for wool or silk, which sometimes feel better on your skin since they're natural fibers. Heads up: these fabrics are a bit harder to care for, though, and require special attention on laundry day.

Rise is really up to your personal preference, but higher-rise leggings usually have tummy control, which is a plus when you want to feel snatched at the gym. However, tummy control typically comes with a bit more compression, which may not be great for lower-impact activities like yoga or casual walks.

Again, length is up to your personal preference, but the most flattering cut is typically just above your ankle. For most people, that means a 7/8 style of leggings. Full-length leggings, or ones that extend all the way to your feet, might be better for barefoot workouts like yoga. Other lengths, like capri leggings, are really up to your personal style.

This final list includes something for everyone—from the best high-waisted leggings, to the best leggings for workouts. #4760. The Man With No Butt

My flat ass has haunted me since high school. So I did what any man would do and sought out some wisdom from the Internet. This is the story of my midlife butt crisis.

I REMEMBER THE first time I became aware of it. Or should I say lack of it.

I grew up in the mid '80s. A magical time. MTV actually played music videos. New Coke was a thing people cared about. And a ginger-headed German teenager named Boris Becker—Boom-Boom, to his millions of fans—became, at age 17, the youngest player ever to win Wimbledon.

While other kids my age were collecting Garbage Pail Kids cards or trying to solve Rubik’s Cubes, I was rewatching VHS recordings of Becker’s matches. I practiced his signature serve motion in front of my bedroom mirror. I begged—actually begged—my parents for the complete Boris Becker starter kit: the Ellesse tennis outfit, the Puma half-top sneakers, and the holy grail itself: the Puma Super tennis racket.

The problem was, no matter how hard I tried, no matter how much Hi-C I consumed, I could never quite achieve the same explosive spring that made Boom-Boom so spectacular. Then I noticed something about Becker’s serve—the one that earned him his nickname. Most players bent their knees to generate power. Becker did something else.

He stuck out his butt. Like, really stuck it out. His serve motion involved this pronounced posterior thrust that helped him achieve that devastating topspin and pace.

I went to the bedroom mirror. Turned sideways. Attempted the same motion. And that’s when it hit me with all the force of one of Becker’s 130-mph serves: I didn’t have a butt to stick out. Where Becker had curvature, I had...continuity. My back just sort of continued into my legs.

Reality set in. No bum-bum, no boom-boom.

By high school, I had pivoted to more cerebral pursuits like creative writing and a burgeoning interest in art history. Art history seemed safe—just looking at old paintings and Roman sculptures, right? Wrong. It became immediately clear that every civilization in human history had one thing in common: great asses.

One fateful morning during my sophomore year, I was standing at my locker wearing a pair of saggy-bottomed Gap khakis when I heard giggles behind me. The distinctive sound of teenage girls discovering something hilarious and vaguely cruel.

“He has no butt!” another voice announced with the confidence of a scientist making a groundbreaking discovery. More giggling. Then came the death blow that would echo through the halls of my high school for the next two and a half years:

Sure, it wasn’t the most creative nickname, but it had just enough alliteration to spread like a pre-internet meme. From that day on, I knew “they” knew.

I RECENTLY TURNED 50. I’m happily married and have two amazing kids. Life is good, but I’d be lying if I said the ghost of No-Butt Bender doesn’t haunt me. As I’ve aged, what little I had has somehow continued to erode. My wife loves me despite my anatomical deficiency but remains perpetually baffled by the lack of rear topography. And what was once giggles from teenage girls is now full-throated hysterical laughter from my 13- and 11-year-old children when they catch a glimpse of me in anything form-fitting.

Recently, Instagram’s insecurity algorithm served me up an ad for Perk men’s clothing. Their website is a gallery of flattering middle-aged bum-shots, but what really stopped my scroll was the pitch for their pants: “Our chinos magically turn even a flat butt into a compliment magnet. Whether you’re 25 or 65, that never gets old!”

You have to understand—when you have no butt, no pants are flattering. So when Perk starts advertising pants that promise to be so bootylicious you’ll receive compliments everywhere you go, I dared to dream. I did what any man starved for posterior validation does—panic-ordered chinos in every damn color.

The moment the package arrived, I tore it open. I slipped them on, turned toward the mirror, and—holy hell—was that…something? An actual curve? A protrusion? I squinted. I turned. And I practically sprinted to the living room where the family was waiting and struck a pose.

Crickets. Then a full collapse into hysterical laughter.

“I’m sorry,” my wife gasped between laughs, “but those pants are working so hard and getting nowhere.”

I was tired of feeling like the only person other than Hank Hill to be cursed with diminished gluteal syndrome (DGS). It’s a very isolating condition. But I figured if Perk could build an entire company around flat-figured men, there had to be others out there, living their lives in quiet resignation.

Close to midnight on a Wednesday, my face lit only by the glow of my computer, I did the obvious: Googled “men with no butts.”

The search results led me to a Reddit post titled, “Gentlemen, how do you live with having no butt?” This guy laid it all out: approaching 29, finally accepting his glute-free reality, enduring constant jokes from his girlfriend, the whole nine yards of tush-less trauma. But what really got me was his closing line: “Please help.”

I stared at those two words. They contained decades of my own experience—the loneliness, the confusion, the desperation. This post was five years old, but I didn’t care. I had to reach out.

I woke the following morning to find his response waiting like a message from a long-lost brother: “You seem legit I guess. Sure why not.” We were already bonding!

I asked Ross (names have been changed to protect the in-ass-cent) what motivated him to publicly post about his crisis on Reddit, and he said, “It was a genuine problem and if someone’s got an answer that helps, I was going to be all ears.”

Ross first became aware of his DGS when a friend at the gym dropped the bomb: “Dude, you have no ass whatsoever.” The keen observation sent Ross into a spiral of photographic evidence review. He started examining pictures of himself and came to the only possible conclusion: “Huh, I guess I don’t.” It was his own bedroom-mirror moment.

As for the wisdom he received from the Reddit hivemind, he said it was mostly people prescribing the usual suspects: sprints and squats: “I was doing a shit ton of those back then and still do,” he told me. Five years later, he reports modest gains: “It’s no dump truck but I’ve got a little bit of cushion for comfort back there.” He eventually broke up with the ass-teaser (good riddance) and has been dating a new lady who actually pinches it every now and then—a gesture that, for men like us, feels like winning the Mega Millions.

But the struggle continues. Despite his Stairmaster devotion, sweatpants still “kind of sag” making it look like he may have taken a small dump in them. Still, Ross gave me the faintest glimmer that improvement was possible. Maybe a guy with no butt could transform it into a bona fide ba-donk-a-donk.

I phoned two lifelines from my past—Jonathan Botts, MD, a college friend who’d become an orthopedic surgeon at the Los Angeles Orthopedic Institute in Sherman Oaks, California, and Derek DeGrazio, Britney Spears’s former physical trainer, who had whipped me into the best shape of my life for my long-ago wedding and now is a partner at Barry’s Bootcamp Miami.

Dr. Botts (who inexplicably escaped high school without a single butt-related nickname) broke down the anatomy: the butt consists of three muscles—the gluteus maximus, medius, and minimus—but it’s the maximus that does the heavy lifting in filling out a man’s pants.

When I asked about the possibility of maximizing my non-existus, he didn’t sugarcoat it. “Here’s the problem: You can’t go from what you are now to just working on your butt and developing a huge butt,” he told me. “People are doing lots of protein intake and protein shakes and bulking up like that. You could get bigger and your butt would get bigger with you. Muscularly. But I don’t know. I don’t think you could take huge leaps with just doing squats and lunges.”

So basically, I’d need to become the Hulk to grow a butt.

Derek offered an alternate strategy with what he calls the Bender Big Three™. “I can’t say for certain, but I think No-Butt Bender can become Mild-Butt Bender with squats for foundation, Romanian deadlifts to pop your glute a little bit, and a nice hip thrust,” he said.

Mild-Butt Bender. It did have a certain je ne sais quoi.

Derek, who freely admits he was born with “a great Italian ass,” is my gluteal bizarro from an alternate universe where only perfect butts exist. He told me his seat meat gave him confidence in high school, something I could only imagine by taping Springsteen’s Born in the USA album cover to the back of my pants. Derek played baseball, a sport notorious for its revealing tight pants. “I don’t know if they came to watch baseball or if they came to watch us in our uniforms,” he recalled, misty-eyed.

I hated him. I loved him. I wanted to be him.

But then—plot twist!—just as I began fantasizing about playing shortstop, Derek dropped a bomb: “I started to realize that what was a blessing at one point became also my Achilles heel at the same time. If there is a downside, I wasn’t the fastest guy on the team.”

Wait. There was a downside to having a butt?

Dr. Botts, who describes his own posterior as “bigger than average,” delivered the final revelation: Having a bigger butt doesn’t automatically mean you have a stronger butt. “If you have a small butt, does that mean you have weak glutes? It doesn’t at all. You can have a big butt and have really weak glutes.”

All these years, I’d associated my small butt with weakness. With inadequacy. With being less than. What if I was wrong? What if, beneath my perpetually drooping pants lurked a superhero-strength posterior more powerful than even Derek’s celebrated Italian ass?

Unlikely. And who am I kidding? It’s not like if I had said to those girls in high school, “Oh no, you don’t understand—it’s really strong!” they would have stopped laughing and made out with me.

But hey, this new knowledge gives me something to cling on to. Wish I could say the same for my pants. Still, I am what I am. I am No-Butt Bender. And if that makes my kids laugh and my wife give me a warm, “It’s okay. I love you anyway” hug, I’ll take it.

Mike Bender is a #1 NY Times Bestselling author of "Awkward Family Photos," screenwriter of "Not Another Teen Movie," and an award-winning children's book author at Random House Children's Books. He recently published a picture book for kids inspired by his health journey called "The End Is Just The Beginning''.

#4758. Volkswagen’s new electric SUV is taking over where the ID.4 left off

Volkswagen is turning one of its best-selling nameplates into an electric SUV. The new ID. Tiguan is now rolling off the assembly line and will replace the ID.4 in 2027.

The ID.4 and ID.5 are on the way out, and the new ID. Tiguan will replace them in Volkswagen’s EV lineup next year.

Following the ID. Polo and ID. Cross, which are now on sale in Europe, the ID. Tiguan is the next model to go on sale as a “True Volkswagen,” as the company calls it.

The new electric SUV adopts Volkswagen’s best-selling nameplate, the Tiguan, as part of its shift away from the numerical system used on the outgoing ID.4 and ID.5.

Volkswagen announced on Thursday that series production of the electric ID. Tiguan has now begun at its Embden Plant in Germany.

“The Tiguan is our best-selling model worldwide, and the ID.4 and ID.5 are among our most successful electric cars,” VW Brand CEO Thomas Shafer said last month after unveiling the new SUV. He added that “The ID. Tiguan brings these two success stories together.”

The ID. Tiguan will be a step change from its predecessors, the ID.4 and ID.5, featuring Volkswagen’s “Pure Positive” design language, which includes an overhauled interior and exterior design and updated tech.

Volkswagen said the ID. Tiguan’s “hallmark feature” is the completely redesigned exterior. The new design remains closer to the gas-powered Tiguan, with a boxy, traditional SUV look.

Unlike the ID.4’s aerodynamic design, the new ID. Tiguan features a wide, chunky front end and traditional pull-style door handles instead of the flush ones.

In Volkswagen’s words, the new ID. Tiguan electric SUV “looks like a vehicle from the premium segment.”

Inside, the updates are just as evident. The overhauled interior features Volkswagen’s new infotainment setup, including a 10″ Digital Cockpit and a 15″ center display. There’s also an optional 12″ passenger screen available.

Another highlight is the return of physical buttons. After listening to customer feedback from current drivers, Volkswagen added physical buttons under the center display and on the steering wheel for climate control and other vehicle functions.

To spark a bit of nostalgia, VW added a fun new Retro Mode feature that transforms the Digital Cockpit and center display to mimic the style of iconic models like the first-generation Golf.

Drivers can also choose from six classic VW models to display in a virtual “garage” on the infotainment screen: Golf I, Golf II Country, Golf IV, Tiguan I, Race Touareg, and Beetle.

The new Volkswagen ID. Tiguan will be available with two battery packs and three performance levels:

Depending on the configuration, the ID. Tiguan offers a “new benchmark” for Volkswagen’s electric SUVs in Europe with a WLTP range of up to 377 miles (607 km).

Volkswagen opened orders for the ID. Tiguan in Germany and a few other European markets in October, starting at €42,695 ($47,800). It’s available in three trims: Trend, Life, and Style.

After it launches in Europe through early 2027, Volkswagen said the ID. Tiguan will go on sale in North America to replace the ID.4.

Apparently, the new ID Tiguan has a slightly better Cx (Drag Coefficient) at 0.26 versus 0.28 on first gen ID4 and 0.27 on 2nd gen. According to VW, it’s achieved using front air curtains, a minimal air intake, an enclosed underbody, and a longer roof spoiler.

Volkswagen said prices and other details will come in “due course.” The current VW ID.4 uses an 82 kWh battery that provides an EPA-estimated range of up to 291 miles in the RWD version. The AWD model offers around 263 miles.

Since the European model offers close to 25 miles (40 km) more WLTP range than the outgoing ID.4, the ID. Tiguan is expected to offer over 300 miles on the EPA scale.

Official US prices have not yet been announced. The outgoing ID.4 has a starting MSRP of about $45,000, so the ID. Tiguan will likely be priced similarly.

His enthusiasm for electric vehicles, primarily Tesla, is a significant reason he pursued a career in investments. If he isn’t telling you about his latest 10K findings, you can find him enjoying the outdoors or exercising

#4757. 8 Best Miami Airbnbs to Get You in the Vacation Mindset (2026)

Miami is a hotbed for avant-garde fashion, design, and art, which is why people flock to the tip of the Florida peninsula every year to navigate the crowds at events like the upcoming Art Basel Festival and Design Miami. History buffs can explore architecture from the 1920s at the Vizcaya Museum and Gardens, stroll the Deering Estate house museum, or study up on Miami’s urban and environmental design at the Coral Gables Museum. There’s plenty of nightlife to speak of, too, but if you’re aiming for something more low-key, catch a live performance at the Historic Miracle Theatre, or head over to ArtecHouse Miami to immerse yourself in interactive art.

You could even devote a day or so to scouring the food scene. May we suggest a Cuban food tour of Little Havana or noshing at trendy spots like the playful, pastel-hued Casadonna? Local favorites like the News Cafe, the historic restaurant where Gianni Versace used to venture to pick up his daily newspaper, and newer openings like cocktail bar Little Torch, with its Golden Milk Punch, are not to be overlooked as well.

Just know whether you’re traveling solo or going in with a group, there are countless Miami airbnbs to set you up for an epic trip to remember. #4755. The Best Barbour Jacket Alternatives, According to Fashion Editors

You don’t need a designer price tag to pull off one of menswear’s most iconic outerwear styles.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

I’M A SUCKER for a good Barbour jacket. I’ve always considered the British heritage brand’s waxed-cotton outerwear the perfect finishing touch to a nicely put-together fall-winter outfit. Founded in 1894, Barbour originally made weather-resistant jackets for sailors, fishermen, and outdoor workers before becoming a staple of British countryside dressing. Over the years, its association with the royal family, including Princess Diana and King Charles III, helped give the rugged outerwear an upper-class appeal. But in the past five years, Barbour has seen a major resurgence, thanks to the barn-jacket boom on runways at Prada, Loewe, and Bottega Veneta, along with a growing appetite for timeless pieces over fast fashion. Buzzy collaborations with designers like Gucci and Paul Smith, plus the popularity of British countryside style in shows like The Crown, have also introduced the look to a whole new generation of guys.

But as much as I love Barbour jackets, dropping several hundred dollars on one isn’t exactly in everyone’s budget. Some of the brand’s most iconic styles, including the Bedale, Beaufort, and Ashby, will set you back anywhere from $415 to $450. And that’s before you dive into the designer collabs. Take the latest J.Crew x Barbour Beaufort jacket. It’s a fresh, reimagined take on the brand’s signature outerwear, dressed in a bold apple-green shade with a weather-resistant finish and tartan lining. Personally, it has me in a chokehold. At nearly $500, it’s a pretty steep investment, especially if you’re just looking to dip your toes into the barn-jacket trend.

Thankfully, you don’t have to spend that kind of money to capture the same British countryside charm. Plenty of menswear brands have put their own spin on the classic barn jacket, offering similar silhouettes, canvas fabrics, and utilitarian details at more digestible prices. I’ve rounded up some stylish Barbour jacket alternatives from brands like Bonobos and Quince that look just as sharp, whether you’re getting dressed up or keeping it casual.

Of all the Barbour jacket alternatives I’ve tested, Quince’s version comes close in comparison to the Barbour x Paul Smith collaboration I already own. That jacket runs north of $800, while Quince’s costs just over a hundred bucks but with nearly all the same bells and whistles: a two-tone brown colorway, contrasting corduroy collar, and snap-button storm throat flap that helps seal out drafts of wind. There’s also a two-way zipper that lets me open the jacket from the bottom when I need a little more breathing room.

It’s made from 100% organic cotton and fully lined with soft cotton flannel, which adds a welcome layer of warmth on chilly days when I can skip wearing a bulky sweater underneath. The cut hits just below the waist, with a roomy fit that leaves plenty of space for layering without feeling overly baggy. I’ve even worn it through a few light rain showers, and the fabric didn’t get soaked. I also appreciate how the classic design makes it easy to dress up with trousers and an Oxford shirt or keep things casual with jeans and boots.

Uniqlo makes one of the best affordable Barbour alternatives I’ve come across, and at just $80, it looks and feels more expensive than it actually is. The lightly padded cotton-nylon fabric provides just enough warmth for temps below 60 degrees. What I especially like is the clean, sleek design that comes with a concealed front closure that hides the buttons and zipper, giving the jacket a sharper, more polished look when fully fastened. There’s also a classic checked lining and two-way flap pockets that can be accessed from the top or sides.

Fit-wise, it has a relaxed, boxy silhouette that hits right at the waist and leaves enough room to comfortably layer a chunky knit over a dress shirt. And unlike some barn jackets that lean heavily into the rugged workwear aesthetic, this one feels a little more understated. It comes in versatile neutrals like olive, khaki, black, and dark brown, so finding one that works with your wardrobe shouldn’t be difficult.

If you’re looking for something that puts a slightly different spin on the traditional barn jacket, Bonobos has you covered. Its Canvas Ranch Jacket trades the usual waxed-cotton construction for a sturdy cotton-elastane blend that has a little stretch, so I’m not feeling boxed in. But what really caught my attention are the angled patch pockets across the front. They’re a subtle departure from the usual straight-cut or flap pockets you see on most field jackets, giving the whole design a more distinctive, modern feel.

The cut extends past the waist and lands around the upper thigh, offering a little more coverage than your typical trucker jacket. I also like that Bonobos keeps the color options simple with midnight navy and forest green, both of which go nicely with denim, chinos, or tailored trousers.

For guys who want the classic Barbour look with a little more protection against the elements, consider this insulated version from L.L.Bean. Unlike some of the lighter options on this list, this one is packed with 60 grams of PrimaLoft Black insulation, a lightweight synthetic fill that helps keep it toasty inside without the bulk of a heavy winter coat. The 100% cotton exterior has a water-resistant waxed finish that will keep you dry in light rain. It also has a quilted exterior that gives the traditional field-jacket silhouette a slightly more outdoorsy vibe.

It’s slightly fitted, so guys who are super jacked might have to size up. But the regular-length cut makes it easy to wear over everyday layers and not feel overly snug. This is a great option for construction laborers, landscapers, and other guys who spend long hours outdoors. It’s more practical than fashion-forward, but you get the timeless look of a heritage field jacket with added insulation and weather protection that make it useful beyond just completing an outfit.

Flint and Tinder’s waxed trucker jacket famously appeared on Pedro Pascal in HBO’s The Last of Us, and we’ve been major fans ever since. This is a burly, no-nonsense trucker jacket made from Martexin waxed sailcloth that’s treated on both sides to help block wind and repel light rain. The antique-finish metal buttons and earthy color options give it that rugged, worn-in look that only gets better with age. On the inside, a full-length blanket-style lining keeps you cozy during 50-degree mornings.

One thing worth mentioning is that the waxed fabric feels pretty stiff at first, so don’t expect it to have that soft, broken-in feel right out of the gate. But that’s also part of the appeal of this type of outerwear. After three wears, the fabric gradually softens and develops more character. Compared to Barbour’s longer field jackets, this one has a more traditional trucker silhouette, so I can pair it with jeans, boots, and other casual staples.

Not every Barbour alternative needs to look like it belongs in the English countryside. Case in point: AYR’s The Club, which takes a more refined approach. It’s made from weatherproofed waxed cotton that offers a water-repellent finish. The fabric is also light enough to wear across three seasons, making it a versatile option for those in-between days when a heavy coat feels like overkill.

I can throw it over a tee with jeans for the weekend or wear it with a collared shirt and trousers to the office. I wore it in light showers, and it didn’t soak through to my clothes. And at a little over $300, it’s certainly not the cheapest alternative on this list. Still, if you’re after something that feels more stylish than rugged, this one makes a strong case for itself.

As the Style and Reviews Editor for Men's Health, Joseph covers all-things fashion and grooming. Previously, Cheatham was a commerce editor for Spy, and he has written for Travel + Leisure.

Christian is the Style and Reviews Director at Men's Health and Women’s Health, where he oversees shopping and style content for both brands. He started his magazine career as an accessories assistant at Vogue, and has since held editorial roles at Tatler Asia and The Manual. When he’s not online shopping, you can find him on long walks with his two corgis.

#4753. BYD’s new 5-seat luxury SUV brings big screens, big range, and starts at about $40,000

BYD launched the Denza N8 EV in China, a five-seat luxury SUV that promises more than 1,000 km (621 miles) of CLTC range, a tech-filled cabin, and plenty of premium extras.

Another day, another new BYD vehicle is now on sale in China. After opening preorders for the Denza N8 EV a few weeks ago, BYD held the grand launch event on Thursday.

The new N8 EV, or “the king of large 5-seater pure electric vehicles,” as Denza called it, is available in six variants, including four battery-electric (EV) and two plug-in hybrid (PHEV) powertrain options.

The all-electric Denza N8 starts at 269,900 yuan, or about $40,500, with prices ranging up to 329,900 yuan ($49,500) for the Flagship model. The PHEV models start at 300,900 yuan and 32,900 yuan, respectively.

Measuring 5,150 mm long, 1,999 mm wide, and 1,820 mm high, with a 3,075 mm wheelbase, the Denza N8 EV is slightly longer than the Kia EV9 and Hyundai IONIQ 9.

Inside, the interior is pretty over the top with a huge 30″ panoramic infotainment screen that stretches nearly the entire passenger-side dashboard. There’s also a new 43″ (1.1 meter) panoramic head-up display (PHUD) at the bottom of the windshield.

BYD added a few premium extras like front and rear zero-gravity seats, a drop-down ceiling entertainment screen, ambient lighting, and wireless phone chargers.

The Denza N8 is offered with a single rear-wheel-drive (RWD) or a tri-motor all-wheel-drive (AWD) powertrain. The RWD variant uses a single motor that delivers either 320 kW (429 hp) or 370 kW (496 hp), depending on the configuration.

The tri-motor Denza N8 debuts BYD’s e3 Evo technology. The setup includes three electric motors: a 270 kW (362 hp) front motor and two 310 kW (416 hp) rear motors, for a combined output of 890 kW (1,193 hp).

BYD’s Denza said the N8 EV boasts an all-electric CLTC range of 1,003 km (623 miles), “making it the world’s longest-range mass-produced all-electric SUV.”

To put it in perspective, that’s as much as the outgoing N8 plug-in hybrid (PHEV) it’s replacing, which offered a combined CLTC range of 1,003 km (623 miles) on a full tank of gas and a full charge.

It’s available with two second-gen BYD Blade battery packs: 105.792 kWh and 130.15 kWh, delivering CLTC ranges of up to 885 km (549 miles) and 1,003 km (623 miles).

With BYD’s second-gen Blade Batteries and Flash Charging tech, the Denza N8 EV can charge from 10% to 70% in five minutes. Charging from 10% to 97% takes just nine minutes.

Like many of its new luxury vehicles, the Denza N8 is equipped with BYD’s premium tech and features, such as its DiSus-A intelligent air suspension system and God’s Eye 5.0 intelligent driving assistance system.

BYD also sells the extended Denza N8L in China, which went on sale earlier this year, starting at 299,800 yuan ($45,000).

#4752. 12 Best Early Black Friday Mattress Deals, Vetted by AD (2026)

Black Friday isn't until November 27, but that hasn't stopped mattress brands from getting a head start on the holiday savings. If your current bed has seen better days or you're waking up with more aches than you went to bed with, there's no need to wait until a designated shopping day to save big on an upgrade. We've spotted the 12 best Black Friday mattress deals to explore ahead of all of your Thanksgiving festivities.

Brands like Saatva, Birch, and Beautyrest are already offering up to $1,000 off on hotel-worthy innerspring mattresses, cooling hybrids, and organic options. Whether you're a side sleeper in need of a little extra cushioning for your hips and shoulders or someone who prefers a firmer, more supportive surface, there's something worth considering before the holiday rush kicks into high gear.

Below, you'll find AD-tested mattresses, complete with reviewer insights, notable features, and details about the current promotions. Get ready to find your next bed without losing sleep over the price tag.

Saatva is giving shoppers who have “luxury mattress” on their Black Friday wish list a head start on savings. The brand is taking over $600 off its popular Classic queen. Designed to deliver hotel-worthy comfort at home, the innerspring mattress features a dual-coil construction; a plush, three-inch Euro pillow top; and targeted lumbar support that helps promote spinal alignment. It also comes in three firmness options—plush soft, luxury firm, and firm—making it a versatile choice for different sleep positions. Reviewer Erin Kaplan, a combination side-and-stomach sleeper, praises the mattress's supportive feel, durability, and temperature regulation. “Overall, I get a better night’s sleep on a more consistent basis since I switched mattresses,” she says. Complimentary white-glove delivery, a 365-night sleep trial, and a lifetime warranty round out the perks of this high-end buy.

Another name brand with an impressive 180 years of history, Stearns & Foster has a reputation for hotel-quality bedding you’ll find in luxury hotel chains like the Ritz-Carlton. They’ve already kicked off their Black Friday sale, which promises up to $1,000 off select adjustable mattress sets and a $300 Visa gift card with all mattress purchases. Senior visuals editor Melissa Maria raves about her Stearns & Foster Lux Estate Mattress, noting its seamless white glove delivery service was a game-changer. The level of firmness required an adjustment period for her, but the Tempur-Indulge memory foam layer and added pillow top gave it a more plush feel. “I’m a very light and finicky sleeper and it always takes me time to adjust to any sort of change, but my husband remarked at how well he slept and how much better his back felt after the first couple of nights sleeping on this mattress,” she says.

Beautyrest’s long-lasting mattresses are powered with advanced pocketed coil technology, helping couples with motion isolation and providing zoned support. The brand’s Black Friday sale is underway, and you can now save up to $1,000 on select mattress and adjustable base sets. Audience development manager Emma Tubbs recommends the Beautyrest Black Series One Mattress, which she says has the “perfect firmness.” “As someone who continuously changes positions throughout the night, the quilted innerspring construction nicely accommodated my tossing and turning,” she writes in her review. “I often fall asleep on my stomach and move to my side and even my back as I fall deeper into REM—I feel supported by this mattress in each position.” Plus, the motion isolation has worked well for her, as she continues sleeping through her husband’s 5:30 a.m. wakeups.

Over the past century, Serta’s become known for making comfortable, solidly priced mattresses. As it stands, the brand is offering the lowest price we’ve seen in months on the Serta Five-Star Mattress. Shopping writer Julia Harrison had this mattress-in-a-box delivered downstairs, and managed to haul it up with the help of her roommate. She refers to it as relatively lightweight (thanks to the gel-infused foam versus a heavier gel bead layer), exactly medium-firm, and made without any frills. “Nothing about it feels too hard or too soft, which I know primarily because I never think about the density or firmness of this mattress (a good sign!) and because I’ve had no back or neck problems since sleeping on it,” she says.

Selecting a firmer mattress doesn't mean you’re opting for less comfort. That’s especially true with the original PurpleFlex bed-in-a-box, currently $100 off ahead of Black Friday. Unlike traditional memory foam beds, the PurpleFlex features a two-inch GelFlex Grid designed to relieve pressure while maintaining a supportive, responsive feel. Beneath that layer, a durable foam base provides stability, making the eight-inch mattress especially appealing to back and stomach sleepers who prefer sleeping on top of their bed rather than sinking into it. AD tester Chapin Rockwell particularly likes the firm construction, reporting “less back pain and stiffness” after switching to the PurpleFlex.

The WinkBed is a luxury hybrid mattress that caters to your preferred sleeping style, and it’s currently 30% off as part of the brand’s Veterans Day sale. What makes the WinkBeds particularly versatile is its four firmness options: Softer, Luxury Firm, Firmer, and Plus (the last level, Plus, is designed for sleepers who need additional support). Beneath its plush yet supportive Euro pillow top, pressure-relieving gel foam and individually wrapped coils work together to provide cushioning, targeted support, and motion isolation. A moisture-wicking Tencel cover also helps promote a cooler sleeping environment. But don’t just take WinkBeds’ word for it. Contributor Mark Burka notes that the mattress "provides strong edge support and wicks away heat," two features that help distinguish it from other hybrids. Moreover, it also comes with free shipping, a 365-night sleep trial, and a lifetime warranty, offering plenty of time to determine whether it's the right fit for you.

Side sleepers searching for a more supportive night's rest should put shopping Nolah's early Black Friday sale at the top of their shopping list. Shopping editor Audrey Lee, who tested an earlier iteration of the Evolution, praises its high level of comfort and cooling abilities. “I found this design’s contouring quality top-tier for pressure relief, but I still had ample support thanks to the coils,” she says. The brand is currently taking 35% off its mattresses, bringing the queen-size Evolution from $2,277 down to $1,480. Designed to cushion sensitive pressure points without sacrificing support, the luxury hybrid combines responsive AirFoam Luxe, individually wrapped coils, and targeted lumbar support to help keep the spine aligned. Shoppers can choose among Plush, Luxury Firm, and Firm options, all of which can accommodate an optional GlacioTex cooling pillow-top upgrade for additional cushioning and temperature regulation. A 365-night sleep trial, free shipping, and a limited lifetime warranty make the early holiday deal even sweeter.

To make your bedroom a little more environmentally conscious, consider starting with the Birch Natural Mattress, a medium-firm hybrid model that’s free of polyurethane foam, fiberglass, formaldehyde, and pesticides. Instead, it’s made from natural and organic materials, including breathable organic cotton, natural wool, and organic latex that’s certified by the Global Organic Latex Standard (GOLS), breathable organic cotton, and natural wool. Individually wrapped steel coils provide responsive support, while the latex offers gentle contouring (as opposed to the sinking feeling you might get from memory foam). Contributor Zoë Sessums was particularly impressed by the mattress's balance of softness and firmness, calling its feel "Goldilocks perfection." Birch Natural also features reinforced sides for additional stability that you’ll enjoy as you sit on the edge of your bed to put on your shoes. Free shipping, a 365-night sleep trial, and a limited lifetime warranty add even more appeal to the brand’s early holiday sale, which takes 25% off sitewide, bringing the queen-size Birch Natural Mattress down to $1,399 from $1,865.

Handcrafted in Illinois, this mattress combines three inches of GOLS-certified organic Dunlop latex for a breathable, responsive sleeping surface with organic cotton and wool that’s certified by the Global Organic Textile Standard (GOTS). (That’s a third party verification that ensures the organic origin and processing of the materials used in the bed.) The Natural Escape’s individually pocketed coil system features seven zones of targeted lumbar support, making the mattress a strong contender for shoppers who prefer a firmer surface. "I feel super supported," notes contributor Kristi Kellogg of her experience testing the Natural Escape. She also has zero back pain while sleeping on it and appreciates the straightforward delivery and setup process: It arrives compressed in a box and includes free shipping and returns, a 365-night sleep trial, and a 20-year warranty. Need another reason to consider making the switch? My Green Mattress is currently taking 10% off orders during its Go Green Sale, with the Natural Escape Organic Mattress queen going for $1,829, down from $2,079.

Just like the Saatva Classic Mattress, you can save up to $625 off when you buy an adjustable base or upholstered bed frame with this mattress. The luxury mattress is made with sustainable materials such as organic cotton and plant-based flame retardants. In fact, the eco-friendly construction even won over contributor Bailey Berg’s partner, an environmental scientist. It’s been four years since Berg brought the Loom & Leaf Mattress home, and she says it hasn’t lost its composure. “My partner and I joke about feeling like we’re floating on a seat of comfort,” she says. “There’s little to no sag. I think any kind of sleeper—side sleepers, stomach sleepers, and back sleepers alike—would like this supportive pick.”

We’ve all been there: You toss and turn, flip your pillow looking for cool relief, and feel disappointed when you find nothing of the sort. If you tend to overheat easily, Casper's early Black Friday sale could be your chance to finally get a less sweaty night's sleep. Designed to keep nighttime overheating at bay, the mattress uses Casper's Snow Technology, which combines a pleasantly crisp QuickCool cover, phase-change material designed to absorb and disperse heat, and aluminum graphite HeatDelete bands that help draw excess heat away from the body. The mattress’s zoned memory foam and supportive springs also provide cushioning and spinal support without the overly plush sensation of an all-foam mattress. After testing the Snow Hybrid, contributor Kate McGregor describes its feel as "squishy when I lay down," while noting that the foam doesn't make getting out of bed difficult. She also loves its motion isolation and cooling qualities, although there were some lingering off-gassing smells after unboxing. Really, the only hot thing about this bed is its early Black Friday Deal: the sale offers over $800 off the queen-sized hybrid mattress, making now a great time to buy.

Tuft & Needle is an Arizona-based retailer touting reasonably priced mattresses made with the brand’s cooling and pressure-relieving T&N Adaptive Foam Technology. Now, the prices are even better with the pre-Black Friday sitewide sale. Everything is 20% off, including the Tuft & Needle Mint Mattress. Contributor Anna Fixsen is a side sleeper who prefers a medium-firm feel, and says she’s met her match with the Mint mattress. “I fell asleep faster and better on this mattress than my old innerspring mattress—despite being someone prone to tossing and turning—making this a great option out of all the memory foam mattresses out there,” she writes in her review. She attributes her restfulness to the invisible support layer of cushy gel beads, providing a cool, supportive slumber.
